Chapter 25

Advanced Graph Optimization

Orientation

A payments system routes a refund through three accounts, and one transfer carries a credit instead of a fee. The route finder from the last chapter then reports a price that a cheaper route beats. A network team buys a cable plan that follows the shortest routes from one office and pays more than a cheaper plan. A support tool deletes one link at a time to find out which links matter, and it needs an hour per map. Each tool applied a correct method to the wrong question. This chapter teaches the methods for questions that shortest paths from one source cannot answer, and it teaches how to tell the questions apart.

Prerequisites

You should know adjacency lists and depth-first search from chapter 21. You should know directed graphs and union-find from chapter 23, and the heap loop for shortest paths from chapter 24. The PriorityQueue comes from chapter 17. Code samples assume import java.util.*; and a recent JDK.

The Eight Lessons

Each lesson keeps one fact true while a loop runs.

  • Find Shortest Paths With Negative Edges repeats passes over all edges, and after pass i the distances cover routes of at most i edges. A negative cycle is a loop whose weights add up below zero.
  • Find All Distances With Floyd Warshall fills a table of every pair by admitting one more intermediate vertex per round. An intermediate vertex is a stop between the start and the end of a route.
  • Grow A Spanning Tree With Prim adds the cheapest edge that leaves the tree built so far. Such a crossing edge has one end inside the tree and one end outside.
  • Build A Spanning Tree With Kruskal adds sorted edges whose ends lie in different union-find groups. A group is the set of vertices that the chosen edges already connect.
  • Walk Every Edge Once With Hierholzer consumes edges and writes a vertex when no unused edge leaves it. A trail is a walk that never repeats an edge.
  • Find Strongly Connected Components groups directed vertices that reach each other, using finish order and the reversed graph. The finish order lists vertices in the sequence a depth-first search leaves them.
  • Find Bridges With Low Links compares discovery times to find edges that disconnect an undirected graph. A low link is the earliest discovery time that a subtree reaches through one back edge.
  • Choose Edges For A Minimum Spanning Tree explains why the cheapest edge across a cut is safe, and separates spanning trees from shortest paths. A cut is a split of the vertices into two groups.

The Combination Lesson

  • Choose A Method For Each Graph Goal reads the goal of a statement first, and then picks the spanning tree, low link, distance table or trail method that matches it.

How To Work Through Each Lesson

A lesson opens with a program that gives a wrong or slow answer on a realistic input. It then shows a first version and asks you to predict its result. The next parts name the rule, list the variables, follow two traces, show the code and say when the rule stops fitting. Four exercises close each lesson, and they climb through Build, Vary, Boundary and Recognize. Read a hint only after a real attempt.

What You Can Do After This Chapter

You can compute distances when an edge is negative, and you can say when a negative cycle makes the answer undefined. You can build a minimum spanning tree with either of two methods and say which one fits a given input. You can list the bridges of a network, group a directed graph into components, and print a trail that uses every edge once.

What Later Chapters Reuse

Chapter 26 starts dynamic programming and reuses the idea of a table whose entries are final after an ordered sequence of rounds, which the distance table shows first. The union-find structure and the heap return in later design chapters. Flow and matching problems are not part of this chapter.

Find Shortest Paths With Negative Edges

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

A Rebate Makes The Longer Route Cheaper

A payment platform moves money between accounts through partner banks. Every transfer charges a fee, but some transfers pay a rebate, which is a negative fee. Account 0 must reach account 1. The direct transfer charges 4. The route through account 2 charges 6 for the first transfer and then pays a rebate of 5 for the second, so its net fee is only 1. A planner that trusts the first fee it sees overpays by 3.

Treat the graph as directed. Each account is a vertex with a number from 0 to n - 1, and each transfer is an edge that carries a weight, the fee, which may be negative. The cost of a route is the sum of its weights. The task is to find the lowest total fee from one source account to every other account. The method also reports each account that no route reaches. The method reads the arrays it receives and never changes them.

This lesson asks how a search stays correct when a transfer that comes later can lower a fee that already looked final.

Simple approach

Finishing The Cheapest Account First

The familiar tool for lowest-cost routes is the greedy search from the previous chapter. It repeatedly picks the unfinished account with the smallest known fee, marks that account finished, and updates the fees of the accounts it sends to. A finished account is never updated again.

static long[] finishCheapest(int n, int[][] transfers, int src) {
    long[] fee = new long[n];
    Arrays.fill(fee, Long.MAX_VALUE);
    fee[src] = 0;
    boolean[] finished = new boolean[n];
    for (int pass = 0; pass < n; pass++) {
        int u = -1;
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
            if (!finished[v] && fee[v] != Long.MAX_VALUE && (u == -1 || fee[v] < fee[u])) u = v;
        }
        if (u == -1) break;
        finished[u] = true;
        for (int[] t : transfers) {
            if (t[0] == u && !finished[t[1]] && fee[u] + t[2] < fee[t[1]]) fee[t[1]] = fee[u] + t[2];
        }
    }
    return fee;
}

Each pass finishes one account and scans every transfer once, so the method does O(V * E) work. It reaches exactly the accounts that have a route. The open question is whether the stored fee of a finished account is the lowest one.

Predict first: Use the transfers 0 to 1 with fee 4, 0 to 2 with fee 6, and 2 to 1 with fee -5. What fee does finishCheapest store for account 1?

It stores 4, which is wrong. Account 0 finishes first and sets account 1 to 4 and account 2 to 6. Account 1 has the smaller fee, so it finishes next. When account 2 finishes later, the rebate would give account 1 a fee of 1, but the check for finished accounts rejects the update. The true minimum is 1. The method treats the smallest known fee as final, and a rebate that arrives later breaks that belief.

Bottleneck

Later Rebates Reach Finished Accounts

The greedy method works only when a longer route never costs less than the shorter route that it extends. A rebate removes that guarantee. An account with a small fee can still be improved by a route that first pays a large fee and then collects a larger rebate. The search has already stopped looking there.

Updating a finished account again does not fix the problem alone. Every account that depends on the changed fee needs another update too, and the update order decides how often the work repeats. With no bound on the repetition, a loop of transfers whose fees add up to less than zero keeps lowering the same fees forever, so the search never stops.

Listing every route that visits each account at most once gives correct answers, but the number of such routes grows like (V - 1)!, which is exponential. A usable method needs a fixed bound on the work, correct answers when a cheapest route exists, and a clear signal when none exists. The next section finds a bound of O(V * E) edge checks by organizing the work by the number of transfers in a route.

Key idea

Improving Fees One Layer At A Time

The method keeps a best known fee for every account and improves all fees together in passes. Pass i allows routes with one more transfer than pass i - 1.

A Cheapest Route Has Few Transfers

Suppose a route visits some account twice. The part between the two visits is a loop that returns to the same account. If the fees of the loop add up to zero or more, cutting the loop out does not raise the cost of the route. So when no loop has a total below zero, some cheapest route visits every account at most once. A route over n accounts that never repeats an account uses at most n - 1 transfers.

Fees Grouped By Transfer Count

The array dist holds the lowest known fee from the source to each account, with Long.MAX_VALUE for an account that has no known route. Before any pass, only the source has a fee, which is 0. After pass i, dist[v] is the lowest fee over all routes to v that use at most i transfers. This sentence carries the entire method, because every later pass merely extends the allowed transfer count by one.

Relaxing Every Transfer Once

To relax a transfer from u to v with weight w, the method computes dist[u] + w and compares it with dist[v]. When the sum is smaller, the method stores it in dist[v]. One pass relaxes every transfer in the list exactly once, in list order. Since a cheapest route needs at most n - 1 transfers, n - 1 passes are enough.

Reading A Snapshot Of The Previous Pass

A snapshot is a copy of dist taken at the start of a pass. Every relaxation in that pass reads the fee of u from the snapshot and writes only into dist. Skipping the snapshot lets an improvement written early feed a later transfer within the same pass. A pass could then chain two or three transfers, which breaks the transfer-count statement above. Final answers survive that shortcut, but any problem with a cap on transfers does not.

Detecting A Negative Cycle

A negative cycle is a loop of transfers whose fees add up to less than zero. When the source reaches such a loop, every extra lap lowers the fee again, so no lowest fee exists. In that case n - 1 passes are not enough, and pass n still finds an improvement. Without a reachable negative cycle, pass n finds none. So the method runs one extra pass and reports a negative cycle when that pass improves any fee.

Why Infinity Needs A Guard

An unknown fee is Long.MAX_VALUE. Adding a positive weight to it wraps around to a large negative number. Adding a negative weight gives a value just below the maximum, which looks like a real fee and would spread through the array. So the method skips every transfer whose source account is still unknown.

State

Fee Array, Snapshot, And Change Flag

The method reads n, the list edges of triples [from, to, weight], and the source src. It changes none of them. It keeps three working values.

  • dist is a long[] of length n; Long.MAX_VALUE marks an account with no known route.
  • prev is a copy of dist taken at the start of the pass; relaxations read only from it.
  • changed is a boolean that records whether the pass lowered any entry of dist.

When a pass leaves changed false, no later pass can improve anything, because the next pass would read the same values. The method then stops early.

Walkthrough

Two Runs On Small Transfer Maps

Six Accounts With Rebates

The first map lists eight transfers in a fixed order. Account 0 charges 6, 5 and 5 to reach accounts 1, 2 and 3. Account 1 pays a rebate of 1 toward account 4. Account 2 pays a rebate of 2 toward account 1 and charges 1 toward account 4. Account 3 pays rebates of 2 and 1 toward accounts 2 and 5. Each cell shows the table after one pass, and the pointer pass selects it. The variable improved counts entries that dropped, and reached counts entries with a known fee.

The early passes discover accounts and lower fees in bulk. Account 4 needs the longest route, which follows accounts 3, 2 and 1 and uses four transfers, so its final fee of 0 appears only in pass 4. Pass 5 changes nothing, which lets the method stop. The final fees are 0, 1, 3, 5, 0 and 4.

A Loop With A Negative Total

The second map lists four transfers. Account 0 charges 2 toward account 1. Account 1 pays a rebate of 3 toward account 2, while account 2 charges 1 to return, so that loop totals -2. Account 2 also charges 4 toward account 3. Because account 0 reaches the loop, no lowest fees exist. Four accounts allow three ordinary passes, and the variable limit holds that number.

Pass 4 is the extra pass, and it still improves. It lowers account 2 from -1 to -3. That drop proves a reachable negative cycle, so the method reports failure.

Code

Bellman-Ford With A Snapshot Per Pass

The method returns a long[] in which Long.MAX_VALUE marks an unreachable account. It returns null when a negative cycle is reachable from the source. The snapshot uses clone, which copies the whole array in O(V) time.

static long[] bellmanFord(int n, int[][] edges, int src) {
    long[] dist = new long[n];
    Arrays.fill(dist, Long.MAX_VALUE);
    dist[src] = 0;
    for (int pass = 1; pass <= n - 1; pass++) {
        long[] prev = dist.clone();
        boolean changed = false;
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            if (prev[e[0]] == Long.MAX_VALUE) continue;
            long next = prev[e[0]] + e[2];
            if (next < dist[e[1]]) {
                dist[e[1]] = next;
                changed = true;
            }
        }
        if (!changed) return dist;
    }
    for (int[] e : edges) {
        if (dist[e[0]] != Long.MAX_VALUE && dist[e[0]] + e[2] < dist[e[1]]) return null;
    }
    return dist;
}

The skip of unknown sources must come before the sum, so the sum never starts from the marker. The early return inside the loop is valid because every later pass would repeat an unchanged pass unchanged. The final loop is the extra pass, and it only reads, since a single improvement is enough to answer. The method runs in O(V * (V + E)) time, because each of at most n - 1 passes copies the array and scans every edge. It uses O(V) memory for dist and prev, not counting the input.

When to use it

Recognizing Negative Fee Problems

Reading The Cue

Use this method when the statement gives weighted edges that may be negative, or when it limits the number of edges a route may use. Refunds and price adjustments both produce negative weights. A cap such as at most k transfers asks for the same pass structure. The answer is usually a distance array, one distance to a target, or a report that the distances do not exist.

Checking The Invariant

An invariant is a statement about the state that holds before and after every step. Here, after pass i, each finite dist[v] is the lowest fee over routes with at most i transfers, and every finite entry is the cost of a real route. The snapshot protects the first half, and the skip of unknown sources protects the second half. Test an unreachable account, which must keep the marker. Test a graph whose negative cycle cannot be reached from the source, which must not stop the answer. Test one account alone, where zero passes run and the extra pass only checks self loops.

Avoiding The False Friend

A false friend is a well-known technique that seems to apply but returns wrong answers on some inputs. Here the false friend is the greedy search that finishes the smallest fee first. It is correct for nonnegative weights, and the first example of this lesson shows how a rebate breaks it. A second false friend is adding a constant to every weight to remove the negatives. A route with more transfers gains more of the constant, so the cheapest route can change. A third is reading the answer after a fixed number of passes without the extra pass, which silently returns wrong fees when a reachable negative cycle exists.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

One Relaxation Pass

Author exercise

Problem Specification. An array dist of type long[] holds a fee for each account, and Long.MAX_VALUE means that no route is known. Each triple [u, v, w] in edges is a transfer from u to v with fee w. Return a new long[] that results from one pass. For every transfer in which dist[u] is known, propose dist[u] + w for account v. Each account keeps the smallest of its old fee and all proposals. Proposals read only the old array.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Accounts satisfy 1 <= dist.length <= 1000, and u and v are valid indices.
  • Transfers satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 5000, and repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Weight satisfies -1000000 <= w <= 1000000.
  • Distances are Long.MAX_VALUE or lie in -10^12..10^12.
  • Mutation is not allowed, because the input array stays unchanged.

Example 1. Input dist = [0,9223372036854775807,9223372036854775807,9223372036854775807], edges = [[0,1,4],[1,2,-2],[2,3,1]], output [0,4,9223372036854775807,9223372036854775807].

Example 2. Input dist = [0,5,9223372036854775807], edges = [[0,1,7],[1,2,-3],[0,2,9]], output [0,5,2].

Prerequisites. The relaxation and snapshot steps of this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. The method reads each proposal from the old array and writes into a copy. It also skips unknown sources, so no sum starts from the marker.

Hint

If the method wrote account 1 first and then read it for the next transfer, how many transfers would one pass use in the first example?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method copies dist into a new array next and treats the original array as the snapshot of the previous pass. For each transfer, it skips the transfer when the source fee in the snapshot is the unknown marker. Otherwise it computes the proposal as a long sum of the snapshot fee and the weight. It writes the proposal into next only when the proposal is strictly smaller than the stored value. The method never reads from next and never writes into dist.

The invariant is that next[v] equals the smaller of the old fee of v and every proposal read from the old array. So no chain of updates uses more than one new transfer. The guard for the unknown marker matters in both directions. A positive weight added to Long.MAX_VALUE wraps to a negative number, and a negative weight gives a value just below the marker that looks like a real fee. The tests below assert both facts.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(V + E), because the copy touches each account once and the loop touches each transfer once.
  • Space is O(V) for the returned array, and the input array stays unchanged.
import java.util.*;

public final class BfOnePass {
    /**
     * Returns the fees after one pass in which every proposal reads the old array.
     * Time: O(V + E). Space: O(V).
     * Invariant: next[v] is the minimum of dist[v] and all proposals computed from dist.
     */
    static long[] onePass(long[] dist, int[][] edges) {
        // The copy is the only array that receives writes, so dist acts as the snapshot.
        long[] next = dist.clone();
        // Each transfer is visited once, so the loop costs O(E).
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            // An unknown source offers no route, and adding to the marker would corrupt the fee.
            if (dist[e[0]] == Long.MAX_VALUE) continue;
            // The proposal is a long sum of the old fee and the weight, so it cannot wrap here.
            long proposal = dist[e[0]] + e[2];
            // Only a strictly smaller proposal replaces the stored value.
            if (proposal < next[e[1]]) next[e[1]] = proposal;
        }
        // The caller receives the new array, and the input was never written.
        return next;
    }

    /** Oracle: for every account, scan all transfers and take the minimum directly. */
    static long[] oracle(long[] dist, int[][] edges) {
        long[] out = new long[dist.length];
        for (int v = 0; v < dist.length; v++) {
            long best = dist[v];
            for (int[] e : edges) {
                if (e[1] == v && dist[e[0]] != Long.MAX_VALUE && dist[e[0]] + e[2] < best) best = dist[e[0]] + e[2];
            }
            out[v] = best;
        }
        return out;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        final long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE;
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        long[] a = {0, INF, INF, INF};
        long[] aCopy = a.clone();
        if (!Arrays.equals(onePass(a, new int[][] {{0, 1, 4}, {1, 2, -2}, {2, 3, 1}}), new long[] {0, 4, INF, INF})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!Arrays.equals(a, aCopy)) throw new AssertionError("input changed");
        if (!Arrays.equals(onePass(new long[] {0, 5, INF}, new int[][] {{0, 1, 7}, {1, 2, -3}, {0, 2, 9}}), new long[] {0, 5, 2})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // Java facts: a positive weight wraps the marker to a negative number, a negative weight stays near the marker.
        if (Long.MAX_VALUE + 5 >= 0) throw new AssertionError("wraparound");
        if (!(Long.MAX_VALUE + (-5) < Long.MAX_VALUE) || Long.MAX_VALUE + (-5) == Long.MAX_VALUE) throw new AssertionError("near marker");
        // Random arrays and transfers against the oracle, including unknown entries and loops.
        Random rnd = new Random(2501);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            long[] d = new long[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) d[i] = rnd.nextInt(3) == 0 ? INF : rnd.nextInt(41) - 20;
            int m = rnd.nextInt(10);
            int[][] edges = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) edges[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(21) - 10};
            long[] copy = d.clone();
            if (!Arrays.equals(onePass(d, edges), oracle(d, edges))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
            if (!Arrays.equals(d, copy)) throw new AssertionError("mutated " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Cheapest Flights Within K Stops

LeetCode 787

Problem Specification. There are n cities numbered from 0. Each entry [a, b, p] of flights is a one-way flight from a to b with price p, which may be negative to model a credit. A route from src to dst is a sequence of flights in which each flight starts where the previous one ends. Its stops are the cities between the first and last flight. Return an int[] of length k + 1. Entry j is the lowest total price over routes from src to dst with at most j stops, or Integer.MAX_VALUE when no such route exists.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Cities satisfy 2 <= n <= 60, and src != dst with both in 0..n-1.
  • Flights satisfy 0 <= flights.length <= 300, and parallel flights and loops may occur.
  • Prices satisfy -100 <= p <= 1000, and a loop with a negative total may exist.
  • Limit satisfies 0 <= k <= n.

Example 1. Input n = 4, flights = [[0,1,100],[1,2,100],[2,3,100],[0,3,500]], src = 0, dst = 3, k = 2, output [500,500,300].

Example 2. Input n = 3, flights = [[0,1,50],[1,2,-20],[0,2,40]], src = 0, dst = 2, k = 1, output [40,30].

Prerequisites. The exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method reads the destination after every pass and stores it, instead of reading it once at the end. It never checks for a negative cycle, because the pass count is fixed by the limit.

Hint

After which pass does a route with j stops first appear in the fee array, and why does a negative loop stay harmless with a fixed pass count?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. A route with at most j stops uses at most j + 1 flights, so the method runs k + 1 passes of the snapshot relaxation from src. After pass p, the array dist holds the lowest price over routes with at most p flights. The method reads dist[dst] right after each pass and stores it in slot p - 1 of the answer, after converting the unknown marker to Integer.MAX_VALUE. The method does not stop early, because a later pass can still lower the price of the destination even when an earlier pass changed little.

The invariant is that after pass p each finite dist[v] is the lowest price using at most p flights, which needs the snapshot. Without the snapshot, a pass could chain several flights and report a price for a limit that is too small. A loop with a negative total causes no trouble, because the method always runs exactly k + 1 passes and never waits for the fees to settle. The answer is non-increasing along its length, since a larger limit allows every route of a smaller limit.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(k * (V + E)), because each of k + 1 passes copies the array and scans every flight.
  • Space is O(V) for dist and the snapshot, plus O(k) for the answer.
import java.util.*;

public final class BfFlightsByLimit {
    /**
     * Returns the lowest price from src to dst for each stop limit 0..k.
     * Time: O(k * (V + E)). Space: O(V + k).
     * Invariant: after pass p, dist[v] is the lowest price using at most p flights.
     */
    static int[] byLimit(int n, int[][] flights, int src, int dst, int k) {
        // Unknown prices use the largest long value, and the source costs nothing.
        long[] dist = new long[n];
        Arrays.fill(dist, Long.MAX_VALUE);
        dist[src] = 0;
        int[] answer = new int[k + 1];
        // One pass per allowed flight, which is k + 1 passes in total.
        for (int pass = 1; pass <= k + 1; pass++) {
            // The snapshot keeps this pass from chaining two flights.
            long[] prev = dist.clone();
            // Each flight is relaxed once per pass, which costs O(E).
            for (int[] f : flights) {
                // Skip flights that start at a city with no known price.
                if (prev[f[0]] == Long.MAX_VALUE) continue;
                long next = prev[f[0]] + f[2];
                // Keep the strictly cheaper price.
                if (next < dist[f[1]]) dist[f[1]] = next;
            }
            // The destination price after pass p belongs to the limit with p - 1 stops.
            answer[pass - 1] = dist[dst] == Long.MAX_VALUE ? Integer.MAX_VALUE : (int) dist[dst];
        }
        return answer;
    }

    /** Oracle: exhaustive search of every flight sequence with at most limit + 1 flights. */
    static int[] oracle(int n, int[][] flights, int src, int dst, int k) {
        int[] out = new int[k + 1];
        for (int j = 0; j <= k; j++) {
            out[j] = (int) Math.min(Integer.MAX_VALUE, search(flights, src, dst, j + 1));
        }
        return out;
    }

    /** Returns the lowest price from city to dst using at most left flights, or the long marker. */
    static long search(int[][] flights, int city, int dst, int left) {
        long best = city == dst ? 0 : Long.MAX_VALUE;
        if (left == 0) return best;
        for (int[] f : flights) {
            if (f[0] != city) continue;
            long rest = search(flights, f[1], dst, left - 1);
            if (rest != Long.MAX_VALUE) best = Math.min(best, rest + f[2]);
        }
        return best;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        int[][] f1 = {{0, 1, 100}, {1, 2, 100}, {2, 3, 100}, {0, 3, 500}};
        if (!Arrays.equals(byLimit(4, f1, 0, 3, 2), new int[] {500, 500, 300})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        int[][] f2 = {{0, 1, 50}, {1, 2, -20}, {0, 2, 40}};
        if (!Arrays.equals(byLimit(3, f2, 0, 2, 1), new int[] {40, 30})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // A negative loop with a fixed pass count still terminates and matches the oracle.
        int[][] loop = {{0, 1, 1}, {1, 2, -5}, {2, 1, 1}, {2, 3, 1}};
        if (!Arrays.equals(byLimit(4, loop, 0, 3, 4), oracle(4, loop, 0, 3, 4))) throw new AssertionError("loop");
        // The answer never increases when the limit grows.
        int[] chain = byLimit(4, loop, 0, 3, 4);
        for (int i = 1; i < chain.length; i++) if (chain[i] > chain[i - 1]) throw new AssertionError("monotone");
        // Random small graphs with credits, loops and parallel flights.
        Random rnd = new Random(2502);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            int n = 2 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            int m = rnd.nextInt(11);
            int[][] fl = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) fl[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(14) - 4};
            int src = rnd.nextInt(n);
            int dst = (src + 1 + rnd.nextInt(n - 1)) % n;
            int k = rnd.nextInt(n + 1);
            if (!Arrays.equals(byLimit(n, fl, src, dst, k), oracle(n, fl, src, dst, k))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Unreachable And Negative Cycle

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Consider n accounts numbered from 0 and the one-way transfers in edges, where each triple [a, b, w] has fee w. Return a long[] d of length n. Entry d[v] is the lowest total fee from account 0 to account v, or Long.MAX_VALUE when no route exists. If a loop with a negative total fee is reachable from account 0, return an empty array. A negative loop that account 0 cannot reach does not change the result.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Accounts satisfy 1 <= n <= 500.
  • Transfers satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 3000, and repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Weight satisfies -1000000000 <= w <= 1000000000.
  • Sums may exceed the range of int, and the result uses long.

Example 1. Input n = 3, edges = [[0,1,5],[1,2,-3],[2,1,1]], output [].

Example 2. Input n = 5, edges = [[0,1,3],[1,2,-2],[3,4,-5],[4,3,1]], output [0,3,1,9223372036854775807,9223372036854775807].

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method skips unknown sources before any sum, and it runs one more pass after n - 1 passes. An improvement in that pass means the answer is the empty array.

Hint

What happens to the sum when the source fee is Long.MAX_VALUE and the weight is negative, and which pass proves the cycle?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method runs up to n - 1 snapshot passes from account 0 and stops early when a pass changes nothing. Every transfer whose source fee is the unknown marker is skipped before any sum, so a negative weight never turns the marker into a fake fee. After the passes, one more loop looks at every transfer once. If some transfer can still lower a fee, the method returns an empty array. Otherwise it returns the fees with the marker left in place for unreachable accounts.

The invariant is that after pass i, each finite fee is the lowest fee over routes with at most i transfers. Suppose no negative cycle is reachable from account 0. Then some cheapest route to each reachable account repeats no account, so n - 1 passes make every fee exact and the extra loop finds nothing. With such a cycle, going around it lowers the fee of every account on it, so the fees never become stable and the extra loop finds an improvement. A negative cycle that account 0 cannot reach never produces a finite source fee, so the guard keeps it out of the computation and the answer stays valid.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(V * (V + E)), because each of at most n - 1 passes copies the array and scans every transfer.
  • Space is O(V) for dist and the snapshot.
import java.util.*;

public final class BfUnreachableCycle {
    /**
     * Returns the lowest fees from account 0, or an empty array when a negative cycle is reachable.
     * Time: O(V * (V + E)). Space: O(V).
     * Invariant: after pass i, each finite dist[v] is the lowest fee over routes with at most i transfers.
     */
    static long[] fees(int n, int[][] edges) {
        // The marker means no known route, and only account 0 starts with a fee.
        long[] dist = new long[n];
        Arrays.fill(dist, Long.MAX_VALUE);
        dist[0] = 0;
        // A cheapest route without a repeated account has at most n - 1 transfers.
        for (int pass = 1; pass <= n - 1; pass++) {
            // The snapshot keeps the pass from using more than one new transfer.
            long[] prev = dist.clone();
            boolean changed = false;
            // Each transfer is checked once per pass, which costs O(E).
            for (int[] e : edges) {
                // The guard keeps the marker out of every sum.
                if (prev[e[0]] == Long.MAX_VALUE) continue;
                long next = prev[e[0]] + e[2];
                if (next < dist[e[1]]) {
                    dist[e[1]] = next;
                    changed = true;
                }
            }
            // A pass without change repeats forever, so the fees are final.
            if (!changed) return dist;
        }
        // The extra pass only reads, because one improvement already proves a reachable negative cycle.
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            if (dist[e[0]] != Long.MAX_VALUE && dist[e[0]] + e[2] < dist[e[1]]) return new long[0];
        }
        return dist;
    }

    /** Oracle: lowest cost of walks with exactly L transfers for every L, compared at limits n - 1 and 2n. */
    static long[] oracle(int n, int[][] edges) {
        long inf = Long.MAX_VALUE;
        long[] cur = new long[n];
        Arrays.fill(cur, inf);
        cur[0] = 0;
        long[] best = cur.clone();
        long[] bestShort = null;
        // Walks of every length up to 2n are enumerated by dynamic programming over the length.
        for (int len = 1; len <= 2 * n; len++) {
            long[] nxt = new long[n];
            Arrays.fill(nxt, inf);
            for (int[] e : edges) {
                if (cur[e[0]] != inf && cur[e[0]] + e[2] < nxt[e[1]]) nxt[e[1]] = cur[e[0]] + e[2];
            }
            cur = nxt;
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) best[v] = Math.min(best[v], cur[v]);
            if (len == n - 1) bestShort = best.clone();
        }
        if (bestShort == null) bestShort = new long[] {0};
        // A difference between the two limits proves that a longer walk is cheaper.
        return Arrays.equals(bestShort, best) ? bestShort : new long[0];
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        final long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE;
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (fees(3, new int[][] {{0, 1, 5}, {1, 2, -3}, {2, 1, 1}}).length != 0) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        long[] ex2 = fees(5, new int[][] {{0, 1, 3}, {1, 2, -2}, {3, 4, -5}, {4, 3, 1}});
        if (!Arrays.equals(ex2, new long[] {0, 3, 1, INF, INF})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // Java fact: with a negative weight the marker plus the weight is below the marker and looks like a fee.
        if (!(INF + (-5) < INF)) throw new AssertionError("fake fee");
        // One account with a negative self loop is a reachable negative cycle.
        if (fees(1, new int[][] {{0, 0, -1}}).length != 0) throw new AssertionError("self loop");
        // One account with a nonnegative self loop keeps fee 0.
        if (!Arrays.equals(fees(1, new int[][] {{0, 0, 2}}), new long[] {0})) throw new AssertionError("plain loop");
        // Large weights stay exact in long arithmetic.
        if (!Arrays.equals(fees(3, new int[][] {{0, 1, 1_000_000_000}, {1, 2, 1_000_000_000}}), new long[] {0, 1_000_000_000L, 2_000_000_000L})) throw new AssertionError("large");
        // Random graphs with negative weights and unreachable regions against the oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(2503);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            int m = rnd.nextInt(10);
            int[][] edges = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) edges[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(13) - 4};
            if (!Arrays.equals(fees(n, edges), oracle(n, edges))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Negative-Edge Shortest Paths

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A settlement system has accounts numbered from 0. Each triple [a, b, w] in transfers moves funds from a to b with net fee w, which is negative for a rebate. No loop with a negative total fee is reachable from account 0. Among the accounts other than 0 that account 0 can reach, find the one with the lowest net fee, taking the smallest index on a tie. Return a long[] of length 2 with that account and its fee, or {-1, 0} when account 0 reaches no other account.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Accounts satisfy 1 <= n <= 400.
  • Transfers satisfy 0 <= transfers.length <= 2500, and repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Fee satisfies -100000 <= w <= 100000.
  • Guarantee states that no negative loop is reachable from account 0.

Example 1. Input n = 4, transfers = [[0,1,4],[0,2,6],[2,1,-5],[1,3,3]], output [1,1].

Example 2. Input n = 4, transfers = [[0,1,2],[2,3,-4],[3,2,1]], output [1,2].

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method computes all fees with early stopping and then scans only reachable accounts other than 0. The usual statement asks for one target and does not ask for a minimum over accounts.

Hint

Which accounts may enter the comparison for the lowest fee, and why does the loop on accounts 2 and 3 not matter in the second example?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The statement guarantees that no negative cycle is reachable, so the extra detection pass is not needed. The method runs snapshot passes from account 0 and stops as soon as a pass changes nothing, with at most n - 1 passes. It then scans the accounts from 1 upward and keeps the account with the strictly lowest known fee. The strict comparison on an increasing index gives the smallest index on a tie. When no account other than 0 has a known fee, the method returns the pair of minus one and zero.

The invariant here says that each finite fee after pass i is minimal among routes of at most i transfers. The early stop is valid because an unchanged pass repeats forever. Negative fees do not break the scan, because the minimum is taken over final fees, not over a growing frontier. A negative loop among accounts that account 0 cannot reach is harmless, since the guard never lets a sum start from the unknown marker.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(V * (V + E)) in the worst case, because each of up to n - 1 passes copies the array and scans every transfer.
  • Space is O(V) for the fee array and the snapshot.
import java.util.*;

public final class BfNegativeEdgePaths {
    /** Number of passes that the last call used, which the tests read to check the early stop. */
    static int lastPasses;

    /**
     * Returns {account, fee} for the reachable account other than 0 with the lowest fee, or {-1, 0}.
     * Time: O(V * (V + E)). Space: O(V).
     * Invariant: after pass i, each finite fee is the lowest fee over routes with at most i transfers.
     */
    static long[] cheapestOther(int n, int[][] transfers) {
        // The marker means unreachable, and account 0 starts at fee 0.
        long[] dist = new long[n];
        Arrays.fill(dist, Long.MAX_VALUE);
        dist[0] = 0;
        lastPasses = 0;
        // At most n - 1 passes are needed, since no negative cycle is reachable.
        for (int pass = 1; pass <= n - 1; pass++) {
            lastPasses = pass;
            // The snapshot keeps every pass limited to one new transfer.
            long[] prev = dist.clone();
            boolean changed = false;
            // One scan of all transfers per pass costs O(E).
            for (int[] t : transfers) {
                // The guard keeps the marker out of every sum.
                if (prev[t[0]] == Long.MAX_VALUE) continue;
                long next = prev[t[0]] + t[2];
                if (next < dist[t[1]]) {
                    dist[t[1]] = next;
                    changed = true;
                }
            }
            // An unchanged pass means all fees are final.
            if (!changed) break;
        }
        int bestAccount = -1;
        // The scan from index 1 upward with a strict test keeps the smallest index on a tie.
        for (int v = 1; v < n; v++) {
            if (dist[v] != Long.MAX_VALUE && (bestAccount == -1 || dist[v] < dist[bestAccount])) bestAccount = v;
        }
        return bestAccount == -1 ? new long[] {-1, 0} : new long[] {bestAccount, dist[bestAccount]};
    }

    /** Oracle: depth-first search over routes that repeat no account, which suffices without negative cycles. */
    static long[] oracle(int n, int[][] transfers) {
        long[] best = new long[n];
        Arrays.fill(best, Long.MAX_VALUE);
        best[0] = 0;
        boolean[] on = new boolean[n];
        on[0] = true;
        walk(transfers, 0, 0, on, best);
        int bestAccount = -1;
        for (int v = 1; v < n; v++) {
            if (best[v] != Long.MAX_VALUE && (bestAccount == -1 || best[v] < best[bestAccount])) bestAccount = v;
        }
        return bestAccount == -1 ? new long[] {-1, 0} : new long[] {bestAccount, best[bestAccount]};
    }

    /** Extends the route that ends at city with the given cost and records the cost of each account. */
    static void walk(int[][] transfers, int city, long cost, boolean[] on, long[] best) {
        for (int[] t : transfers) {
            if (t[0] != city || on[t[1]]) continue;
            long c = cost + t[2];
            if (c < best[t[1]]) best[t[1]] = c;
            on[t[1]] = true;
            walk(transfers, t[1], c, on, best);
            on[t[1]] = false;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (!Arrays.equals(cheapestOther(4, new int[][] {{0, 1, 4}, {0, 2, 6}, {2, 1, -5}, {1, 3, 3}}), new long[] {1, 1})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!Arrays.equals(cheapestOther(4, new int[][] {{0, 1, 2}, {2, 3, -4}, {3, 2, 1}}), new long[] {1, 2})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // No other account is reachable.
        if (!Arrays.equals(cheapestOther(3, new int[][] {{1, 2, -1}}), new long[] {-1, 0})) throw new AssertionError("none");
        if (!Arrays.equals(cheapestOther(1, new int[0][]), new long[] {-1, 0})) throw new AssertionError("single");
        // A tie goes to the smaller index.
        if (!Arrays.equals(cheapestOther(3, new int[][] {{0, 2, -3}, {0, 1, -3}}), new long[] {1, -3})) throw new AssertionError("tie");
        // The early stop: a forward chain of three transfers needs three changing passes, and the fourth is never started.
        cheapestOther(5, new int[][] {{0, 1, 1}, {1, 2, 1}, {2, 3, 1}});
        if (lastPasses != 4) throw new AssertionError("passes " + lastPasses);
        // Random graphs without negative cycles: weights come from node potentials plus a nonnegative extra.
        Random rnd = new Random(2504);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(7);
            int[] pot = new int[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) pot[i] = rnd.nextInt(8);
            boolean hidden = n >= 4 && rnd.nextBoolean();
            int core = hidden ? n - 2 : n;
            int m = rnd.nextInt(12);
            List<int[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) {
                int u = rnd.nextInt(core), v = rnd.nextInt(core);
                list.add(new int[] {u, v, pot[u] - pot[v] + rnd.nextInt(3)});
            }
            // An unreachable negative cycle sits on the last two accounts.
            if (hidden) {
                list.add(new int[] {n - 2, n - 1, -3});
                list.add(new int[] {n - 1, n - 2, 1});
            }
            int[][] transfers = list.toArray(new int[0][]);
            if (!Arrays.equals(cheapestOther(n, transfers), oracle(n, transfers))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified

Find All Distances With Floyd Warshall

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

One Lookup For Every Server Pair

Latency Between Any Two Servers

A service mesh runs five servers, and the operations team has measured the one-way latency in milliseconds on some of the links between them. A message from server 0 to server 1 often travels faster through server 2 than over the direct link. The router must answer "what is the lowest latency from server a to server b" for any pair, thousands of times per second. Recomputing a route for every query is too slow. The team wants a table that the program fills once and reads in constant time.

The Servers As A Graph

Number the servers 0 to n - 1. Each measured link is a directed edge with a nonnegative weight, which is its latency. A route is a sequence of edges, and its cost is the sum of the weights. The distance from a to b is the lowest cost over all routes from a to b. If no route exists, the pair has no distance. The input lists the edges, and the method must not change that list.

The mesh is small and dense, so many pairs are linked directly or almost directly. This lesson asks how to fill the whole n by n table of distances with code that stays short and reuses work between pairs.

Simple approach

Searching Every Route For Every Pair

The first attempt treats each pair on its own. For a pair (a, b), a depth-first search starts at a and walks along edges. It never visits a server twice on one route. Whenever it reaches b, it compares the cost of the current route with the best cost so far, and then it backs up to try other routes.

static long[][] allRoutes(int n, int[][] edges) {
    long[][] w = new long[n][n];
    for (long[] row : w) Arrays.fill(row, -1);
    for (int[] e : edges) {
        if (w[e[0]][e[1]] == -1 || e[2] < w[e[0]][e[1]]) w[e[0]][e[1]] = e[2];
    }
    long[][] best = new long[n][n];
    for (int a = 0; a < n; a++) {
        for (int b = 0; b < n; b++) {
            best[a][b] = -1;
            if (a == b) { best[a][b] = 0; continue; }
            boolean[] used = new boolean[n];
            used[a] = true;
            best[a][b] = search(w, used, a, b, 0, -1);
        }
    }
    return best;
}

static long search(long[][] w, boolean[] used, int at, int goal, long cost, long best) {
    if (at == goal) return best == -1 || cost < best ? cost : best;
    for (int next = 0; next < w.length; next++) {
        if (w[at][next] == -1 || used[next]) continue;
        used[next] = true;
        best = search(w, used, next, goal, cost + w[at][next], best);
        used[next] = false;
    }
    return best;
}

The method is correct, because it looks at every route that repeats no server. A route with a repeated server never costs less when all weights are nonnegative. The remaining question is how many routes it looks at.

Predict first: Ten servers are linked in both directions by every pair. About how many routes does the search look at for one pair, and how many for the whole table?

For one pair (a, b), the search follows every ordered choice of a subset of the other eight servers as stops. That is 109601 routes for one pair. The table has 90 pairs with a different a and b, so the method follows about 9.9 million routes for only ten servers. Each added server multiplies the work again.

Bottleneck

Routes Are Counted Again And Again

The number of routes grows like the factorial of n, so the worst case is O(n!) per pair. Twenty servers are already far beyond any time limit. The work is also wasteful. The route 0, 2, 3 is the route to server 3, and it is also the start of routes from 0 to 4 and from 0 to 1. The search rebuilds that start for each of them. The cost of the cheapest part from 2 to 3 is computed again for every route that passes through 2.

A faster method must store a cheapest cost for each pair and reuse it. The n by n table has only n squared entries, which is tiny next to the number of routes. The open question is the order in which to improve the entries. Every entry must end with the true minimum after a small, fixed number of passes.

An answer that costs O(n^3) in time and O(n^2) in memory suits a small dense graph. The table must also treat a missing route as a value that never takes part in a sum.

Key idea

Allow One More Intermediate At A Time

The method keeps a table dist and lets more servers act as stops, one server per pass, until every server is allowed.

Intermediate Servers Of A Route

An intermediate of a route is any server on it except the first and the last one. The table has a precise meaning at every moment. After the round for server k, the entry dist[i][j] is the lowest cost of a route from i to j whose intermediates all have an index from 0 to k. Before any round, no intermediate is allowed, so the table holds only direct edges and a zero on the diagonal.

One Round For Server k

A round is one pass over every pair (i, j) for a fixed k. It compares the stored dist[i][j] with the sum dist[i][k] + dist[k][j] and keeps the smaller value. The claim is that this single comparison extends the meaning from "intermediates 0 to k - 1" to "intermediates 0 to k". Take the best route from i to j that may use k. If it does not stop at k, the old entry already holds its cost. If it stops at k, it stops there once, because a repeated stop never helps with nonnegative weights. Then it splits into a part from i to k and a part from k to j. Both parts use intermediates below k, so the old entries dist[i][k] and dist[k][j] already hold their best costs.

Updating The Table In Place

A round reads row k and column k while it writes other entries. These two lines do not change during the round. The entry dist[k][k] is 0, so the sum for dist[i][k] equals the old value, and the same holds for dist[k][j]. The round therefore needs no second table, and the method can write into one array. After the round for n - 1, every server is allowed, and each entry is a true distance. The three nested loops make O(n^3) comparisons.

The Sentinel For A Missing Route

A sentinel is a reserved value that stands for "no route", and this lesson uses Long.MAX_VALUE for it. The method never adds the sentinel to anything. It skips the comparison when dist[i][k] or dist[k][j] equals the sentinel. Without that guard, Long.MAX_VALUE + 1 wraps to a large negative number that looks like a bargain. The table uses long, because a route of many edges with weights near two billion exceeds the range of int.

State

The Table And The Three Loops

The method reads n, the number of servers, and edges, a list of triples [from, to, weight]. It changes neither. It creates one table and three loop counters.

  • dist is a long[n][n] with 0 on the diagonal and Long.MAX_VALUE where no route is known.
  • k is the server allowed as an intermediate in the current round, and it runs in the outermost loop.
  • i and j are the first and last server of the pair under comparison.

Repeated edges between the same two servers keep the lowest weight. A self loop never lowers a diagonal entry, because every weight is nonnegative. The sum dist[i][k] + dist[k][j] is formed only when both parts are finite, so it cannot overflow through the sentinel.

Walkthrough

Two Runs On Five Servers

Correct Order With k Outermost

The first run uses seven links. Server 0 reaches server 1 with weight 4 and server 2 with weight 1. Server 2 reaches server 1 with weight 2 and server 3 with weight 5. Server 1 reaches server 3 with weight 1. Server 3 reaches server 4 with weight 3, and server 4 reaches server 0 with weight 2. Each cell is one round, and the pointer k marks the server allowed in that round. The variable lowered counts the entries that drop in the round. The variable known counts the pairs of different servers that have a finite entry after the round. Round 0 gives server 4 a route to server 1 through server 0, with cost 6. Round 2 lowers the entry from 0 to 1 from 4 to 3, because the route 0, 2, 1 costs 1 plus 2. After round 4, all 20 pairs are known and the table is final.

Wrong Order With k Innermost

The second run uses a ring of five links. Server 0 reaches server 1 with weight 2, and server 1 reaches server 2 with weight 2. Server 2 reaches server 3 with weight 3, and server 3 reaches server 4 with weight 6. Server 4 reaches server 0 with weight 5. This run moves k into the innermost loop and makes a single pass over the rows. Each cell is one finished row i. The variable wrong counts entries of that row that differ from the true distance, and total adds them up. Row 1 needs the route 1, 2, 3, 4, 0 with cost 16. When the pass handles j = 0, the entries from 1 to 4 and from 3 to 0 are still empty, so the row keeps no route. Row 2 has the same problem for its pairs with servers 0 and 1. Rows 3 and 4 happen to be right, so three wrong entries remain in the final table.

Code

Floyd Warshall With Three Loops

The method returns a new long[][] and leaves edges untouched. Pairs without a route keep Long.MAX_VALUE.

static long[][] allDistances(int n, int[][] edges) {
    long[][] dist = new long[n][n];
    for (long[] row : dist) Arrays.fill(row, Long.MAX_VALUE);
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) dist[v][v] = 0;
    for (int[] e : edges) dist[e[0]][e[1]] = Math.min(dist[e[0]][e[1]], e[2]);
    for (int k = 0; k < n; k++) {
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            if (dist[i][k] == Long.MAX_VALUE) continue;
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                if (dist[k][j] == Long.MAX_VALUE) continue;
                long through = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j];
                if (through < dist[i][j]) dist[i][j] = through;
            }
        }
    }
    return dist;
}

The loop over k must stay outermost, so the loops over i and j see a table whose meaning is "intermediates 0 to k - 1". The early continue on dist[i][k] skips a whole row of comparisons when server i cannot reach k. The method runs in O(n^3) time and uses O(n^2) memory for the table. Reading the table afterward costs O(1) for each query.

When to use it

Recognizing All Pairs Problems

Reading The Cue

Use this method when the statement asks for the lowest cost between many or all pairs of vertices on a graph with at most a few hundred vertices. Typical cases are a latency table between servers, a route matrix between warehouses, and a count of how many places lie within a cost limit of each place. When the graph is large and sparse, running Dijkstra's method from every source costs less than O(n^3). Floyd Warshall also works with negative edges when no negative cycle exists. If a negative cycle exists, some diagonal entry dist[i][i] ends below zero, which reveals it. This lesson's inputs stay nonnegative.

Checking The Invariant

An invariant is a condition that stays true at the start and end of each round of a loop. The invariant here is that after the round for k, each entry dist[i][j] equals the lowest cost of a route that uses only intermediates 0 to k. Two conditions in the code protect it. The loop over k is the outermost loop, and no sum starts from the sentinel. Test a graph with no edges, a pair with two equal routes, a self loop, and weights near two billion, where a sum leaves the range of int.

Avoiding The False Friend

A false friend is a familiar approach that looks right and fails. The false friend here is reordering the loops. The three loops look symmetric, and putting k innermost compiles and runs, yet the second trace shows wrong entries. With k innermost, an entry for i and j reads values in other rows that are not finished. Another false friend is Dijkstra's method run once and read as a table, because one run gives distances from a single source only. Bellman Ford is not a false friend here, because it answers a different question with one source.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Allow One Intermediate

Author exercise

Problem Specification. An n by n table dist of type long[][] holds the cost of the best known route between each pair. The value Long.MAX_VALUE means that no route is known. Given a server k, return a new table whose entry (i, j) is the smaller of dist[i][j] and dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]. Form the sum only when both parts are finite. The input table stays unchanged.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Size satisfies 1 <= n <= 200, and 0 <= k < n.
  • Entries are Long.MAX_VALUE or in 0..10^12.
  • Diagonal entries are 0.
  • Mutation of the input is not allowed.

Example 1. Input dist = [[0,5,9223372036854775807],[9223372036854775807,0,2],[1,9223372036854775807,0]], k = 1, output [[0,5,7],[9223372036854775807,0,2],[1,9223372036854775807,0]].

Example 2. Input the same dist with k = 0, output [[0,5,9223372036854775807],[9223372036854775807,0,2],[1,6,0]].

Prerequisites. The round for one server k in this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. The method tries one intermediate only and reads from the old table, so no entry sees an update from the same call.

Hint

What does the sum become when one of the two parts is Long.MAX_VALUE?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method allocates a new table and fills each entry from the old table. For the pair (i, j), it first checks whether dist[i][k] and dist[k][j] are both finite. Only then does it form the sum, because adding to Long.MAX_VALUE wraps around to a negative number that looks like a bargain. It keeps the smaller of the sum and the stored entry.

The invariant is that every finite entry of the result is the cost of a real route. The sum is a known route to k followed by a known route from k, so it is a real route. Reading from the old table keeps one call from seeing its own updates, which is the exact meaning of allowing one more intermediate. The input table is never written.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n^2), because the method visits each of the n squared entries once and does constant work for each.
  • Space is O(n^2) for the new table, and nothing else grows.
import java.math.BigInteger;
import java.util.*;

public final class FwOneIntermediate {
    /**
     * Returns a new table in which each pair may also use server k as a stop.
     * Time: O(n^2). Space: O(n^2).
     * Invariant: every finite entry is the cost of a real route.
     */
    static long[][] allowOne(long[][] dist, int k) {
        int n = dist.length;
        // The result is a separate table, so the input stays unchanged.
        long[][] out = new long[n][n];
        // Each row is visited once, which gives n rows of work.
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            // Each column is visited once, which gives n squared entries in total.
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                // Start from the direct or earlier route.
                out[i][j] = dist[i][j];
                // A missing part means the sum is not a route, and adding to the sentinel would wrap.
                if (dist[i][k] == Long.MAX_VALUE || dist[k][j] == Long.MAX_VALUE) continue;
                // Both parts are finite, so the long sum is exact.
                long through = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j];
                // Only a strictly cheaper route replaces the stored value.
                if (through < out[i][j]) out[i][j] = through;
            }
        }
        // Return the new table.
        return out;
    }

    /** Oracle: the same rule with unbounded integers and no guard tricks. */
    static long[][] oracle(long[][] dist, int k) {
        int n = dist.length;
        long[][] out = new long[n][n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                BigInteger inf = BigInteger.valueOf(Long.MAX_VALUE);
                BigInteger a = BigInteger.valueOf(dist[i][k]);
                BigInteger b = BigInteger.valueOf(dist[k][j]);
                BigInteger cur = BigInteger.valueOf(dist[i][j]);
                // Both parts below the sentinel means a real route exists.
                if (a.compareTo(inf) < 0 && b.compareTo(inf) < 0 && a.add(b).compareTo(cur) < 0) cur = a.add(b);
                out[i][j] = cur.longValueExact();
            }
        }
        return out;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        long M = Long.MAX_VALUE;
        long[][] t = {{0, 5, M}, {M, 0, 2}, {1, M, 0}};
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(allowOne(t, 1), new long[][] {{0, 5, 7}, {M, 0, 2}, {1, M, 0}})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(allowOne(t, 0), new long[][] {{0, 5, M}, {M, 0, 2}, {1, 6, 0}})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // Java fact: adding one to the sentinel wraps to a negative number.
        if (Long.MAX_VALUE + 1 >= 0) throw new AssertionError("wrap");
        // The input table is unchanged after a call.
        long[][] copy = {{0, 5, M}, {M, 0, 2}, {1, M, 0}};
        allowOne(copy, 1);
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(copy, t)) throw new AssertionError("mutated");
        // Random tables against the oracle, with sentinels and large entries.
        Random rnd = new Random(2501);
        for (int c = 0; c < 2000; c++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            long[][] d = new long[n][n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                    d[i][j] = i == j ? 0 : (rnd.nextInt(3) == 0 ? M : (rnd.nextInt(4) == 0 ? 1_000_000_000_000L - rnd.nextInt(3) : rnd.nextInt(20)));
                }
            }
            int k = rnd.nextInt(n);
            if (!Arrays.deepEquals(allowOne(d, k), oracle(d, k))) throw new AssertionError("random " + c);
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

All-Pairs Table

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A directed graph has servers 0 to n - 1, and edges lists triples [a, b, w] for a link from a to b with weight w. Given a limit m, return an n by n table of type long[][]. Entry (i, j) is the lowest cost of a route from i to j whose intermediate servers all have an index below m, or -1 when no such route exists. The diagonal is 0.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Servers satisfy 1 <= n <= 60.
  • Edges satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 400, and repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Weight satisfies 0 <= w <= 1000.
  • Limit satisfies 0 <= m <= n.

Example 1. Input n = 4, edges = [[0,1,1],[1,2,1],[2,3,1],[0,3,9]], m = 2, output [[0,1,2,9],[-1,0,1,-1],[-1,-1,0,1],[-1,-1,-1,0]].

Example 2. Input the same graph with m = 4, output [[0,1,2,3],[-1,0,1,2],[-1,-1,0,1],[-1,-1,-1,0]].

Prerequisites. The exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method stops after m rounds, so the table shows a partial meaning and not the final distances.

Hint

Which loop of the method decides which servers may act as intermediates?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method builds the table of direct edges with zeros on the diagonal, keeping the lowest weight for repeated edges. It then runs the round for server 0, then for server 1, and so on, and it stops after m rounds. The last step converts each remaining sentinel to minus one.

The invariant is that after the round for server k, each entry holds the lowest cost of a route whose intermediates all have an index of at most k. After m rounds, the intermediates are exactly the servers below m, which matches the contract. A route that stops at k splits into two parts that use only smaller intermediates, so one comparison per pair is enough. The conversion happens at the end, because minus one would break the comparisons.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(m * n^2), because each of the m rounds visits every pair once.
  • Space is O(n^2) for the table.
import java.util.*;

public final class FwPrefixTable {
    /**
     * Returns lowest route costs when only servers below m may act as stops.
     * Time: O(m * n^2). Space: O(n^2).
     * Invariant: after the round for k, intermediates are limited to 0..k.
     */
    static long[][] prefixTable(int n, int[][] edges, int m) {
        long[][] dist = new long[n][n];
        // Every pair starts with the sentinel, which means no known route.
        for (long[] row : dist) Arrays.fill(row, Long.MAX_VALUE);
        // A server reaches itself at cost zero.
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) dist[v][v] = 0;
        // Direct edges fill the table, and the lowest weight wins for repeats.
        for (int[] e : edges) dist[e[0]][e[1]] = Math.min(dist[e[0]][e[1]], e[2]);
        // The outermost loop adds one allowed intermediate per round, and it stops at m.
        for (int k = 0; k < m; k++) {
            // The row index i is the first server of the pair.
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                // Without a route to k, no sum for this row exists.
                if (dist[i][k] == Long.MAX_VALUE) continue;
                // The column index j is the last server of the pair.
                for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                    // Without a route from k, the sum is not a route.
                    if (dist[k][j] == Long.MAX_VALUE) continue;
                    // Both parts are finite, so this long sum is exact.
                    long through = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j];
                    // Keep the cheaper route, which preserves the invariant.
                    if (through < dist[i][j]) dist[i][j] = through;
                }
            }
        }
        // The sentinel becomes minus one only after all comparisons are done.
        for (long[] row : dist) for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) if (row[j] == Long.MAX_VALUE) row[j] = -1;
        // Return the finished table.
        return dist;
    }

    /** Oracle: depth-first search over simple routes with stops restricted to indices below m. */
    static long[][] oracle(int n, int[][] edges, int m) {
        long[][] out = new long[n][n];
        for (int a = 0; a < n; a++) {
            for (int b = 0; b < n; b++) {
                boolean[] used = new boolean[n];
                used[a] = true;
                out[a][b] = a == b ? 0 : walk(n, edges, m, used, a, b, 0);
            }
        }
        return out;
    }

    static long walk(int n, int[][] edges, int m, boolean[] used, int at, int goal, long cost) {
        long best = -1;
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            if (e[0] != at || used[e[1]]) continue;
            long next = cost + e[2];
            if (e[1] == goal) {
                if (best == -1 || next < best) best = next;
                continue;
            }
            // A stop must have an index below m.
            if (e[1] >= m) continue;
            used[e[1]] = true;
            long sub = walk(n, edges, m, used, e[1], goal, next);
            used[e[1]] = false;
            if (sub != -1 && (best == -1 || sub < best)) best = sub;
        }
        return best;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        int[][] g = {{0, 1, 1}, {1, 2, 1}, {2, 3, 1}, {0, 3, 9}};
        long[][] a = {{0, 1, 2, 9}, {-1, 0, 1, -1}, {-1, -1, 0, 1}, {-1, -1, -1, 0}};
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(prefixTable(4, g, 2), a)) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        long[][] b = {{0, 1, 2, 3}, {-1, 0, 1, 2}, {-1, -1, 0, 1}, {-1, -1, -1, 0}};
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(prefixTable(4, g, 4), b)) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // With m equal to zero the table holds only direct edges.
        if (prefixTable(4, g, 0)[0][2] != -1) throw new AssertionError("m zero");
        // Random graphs with zero weights, repeats and self loops, for every limit m.
        Random rnd = new Random(2502);
        for (int c = 0; c < 1500; c++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            int cnt = rnd.nextInt(12);
            int[][] edges = new int[cnt][];
            for (int i = 0; i < cnt; i++) edges[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(5)};
            int m = rnd.nextInt(n + 1);
            if (!Arrays.deepEquals(prefixTable(n, edges, m), oracle(n, edges, m))) throw new AssertionError("random " + c);
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Infinity Arithmetic

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Take n servers numbered from 0, with directed links [a, b, w] in edges that run from a to b at weight w. Consider every ordered pair of different servers. Return a long[] that holds two values. Position 0 holds the largest finite distance over these pairs, or 0 when no pair has a route. Position 1 holds the count of these pairs with no route.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Servers satisfy 1 <= n <= 100.
  • Edges satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 2000, and repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Weight satisfies 0 <= w <= 2000000000.
  • Result uses long, since a distance can exceed the range of int.

Example 1. Input n = 3, edges = [[0,1,2000000000],[1,2,2000000000]], output [4000000000,3].

Example 2. Input n = 2, edges = [], output [0,2].

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method guards every sum against the sentinel and counts missing routes separately, so no sentinel value leaks into the largest distance.

Hint

Which value marks a missing route, and what happens when the method adds one to it?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method runs the three loops of this lesson on a long table whose missing routes hold Long.MAX_VALUE. Before it forms any sum, it checks both parts against the sentinel, so the sentinel never takes part in arithmetic. After the loops finish, it scans each ordered pair of different servers. A finite entry updates the largest value, and a sentinel entry adds one to the count of missing routes.

The invariant is that every finite entry is the cost of a real route and the sentinel marks a pair with no route yet. The largest possible distance is below 100 times two billion, about 2 times 10 to the 11, so long holds it. A smaller type fails here: two weights of two billion already exceed the range of int, and a sentinel such as Integer.MAX_VALUE plus any weight wraps negative.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n^3) for the three nested loops, plus O(m) to read the m edges and O(n^2) for the final scan.
  • Space is O(n^2) for the table.
import java.util.*;

public final class FwInfinityArith {
    /**
     * Returns the largest finite distance and the number of ordered pairs with no route.
     * Time: O(n^3). Space: O(n^2).
     * Invariant: the sentinel never appears in a sum.
     */
    static long[] summary(int n, int[][] edges) {
        long[][] dist = new long[n][n];
        // Missing routes hold the sentinel, which is the largest long.
        for (long[] row : dist) Arrays.fill(row, Long.MAX_VALUE);
        // Each server reaches itself at cost zero.
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) dist[v][v] = 0;
        // Direct edges fill the table with the lowest weight for repeats.
        for (int[] e : edges) dist[e[0]][e[1]] = Math.min(dist[e[0]][e[1]], e[2]);
        // Server k is the newest allowed intermediate in this round.
        for (int k = 0; k < n; k++) {
            // The first server of the pair.
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                // A missing route to k makes every sum in this row invalid.
                if (dist[i][k] == Long.MAX_VALUE) continue;
                // The last server of the pair.
                for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                    // A missing route from k makes the sum invalid.
                    if (dist[k][j] == Long.MAX_VALUE) continue;
                    // Both parts are finite, and the long sum cannot wrap here.
                    long through = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j];
                    // Keep the cheaper route.
                    if (through < dist[i][j]) dist[i][j] = through;
                }
            }
        }
        long largest = 0;
        long missing = 0;
        // Scan every ordered pair of different servers once.
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                // Pairs of one server with itself are not counted.
                if (i == j) continue;
                // A sentinel counts as a missing route, and a finite value can raise the maximum.
                if (dist[i][j] == Long.MAX_VALUE) missing++;
                else largest = Math.max(largest, dist[i][j]);
            }
        }
        // Return both values in the order of the contract.
        return new long[] {largest, missing};
    }

    /** Oracle: repeated passes over all edges from every source, until nothing improves. */
    static long[] oracle(int n, int[][] edges) {
        long largest = 0;
        long missing = 0;
        for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
            long[] d = new long[n];
            Arrays.fill(d, -1);
            d[s] = 0;
            boolean changed = true;
            while (changed) {
                changed = false;
                for (int[] e : edges) {
                    if (d[e[0]] >= 0 && (d[e[1]] == -1 || d[e[0]] + e[2] < d[e[1]])) {
                        d[e[1]] = d[e[0]] + e[2];
                        changed = true;
                    }
                }
            }
            for (int t = 0; t < n; t++) {
                if (t == s) continue;
                if (d[t] == -1) missing++;
                else largest = Math.max(largest, d[t]);
            }
        }
        return new long[] {largest, missing};
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (!Arrays.equals(summary(3, new int[][] {{0, 1, 2_000_000_000}, {1, 2, 2_000_000_000}}), new long[] {4_000_000_000L, 3})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!Arrays.equals(summary(2, new int[][] {}), new long[] {0, 2})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // Java facts: the sentinel plus one wraps, and an int sum of two large weights wraps.
        if (Long.MAX_VALUE + 1 >= 0) throw new AssertionError("long wrap");
        int wrapped = 2_000_000_000 + 2_000_000_000;
        if (wrapped >= 0) throw new AssertionError("int wrap");
        // A sentinel of Integer.MAX_VALUE plus a large weight also wraps negative in int arithmetic.
        int bad = Integer.MAX_VALUE + 2_000_000_000;
        if (bad >= 0) throw new AssertionError("int sentinel");
        // Random graphs with weights near the maximum and many missing routes.
        Random rnd = new Random(2503);
        for (int c = 0; c < 1500; c++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(7);
            int cnt = rnd.nextInt(12);
            int[][] edges = new int[cnt][];
            for (int i = 0; i < cnt; i++) edges[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(2) == 0 ? 2_000_000_000 - rnd.nextInt(3) : rnd.nextInt(6)};
            if (!Arrays.equals(summary(n, edges), oracle(n, edges))) throw new AssertionError("random " + c);
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Find the City With the Smallest Number of Neighbors at a Threshold Distance

LeetCode 1334

Problem Specification. There are n cities numbered 0 to n - 1, and edges lists undirected roads as triples [a, b, w] with length w. Given distanceThreshold, the neighbors of a city are the other cities whose shortest distance from it is at most the threshold. Return an int[] that holds two values. Position 0 holds the city with the fewest neighbors, and position 1 holds its neighbor count. When several cities tie, return the one with the smallest index.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Cities satisfy 2 <= n <= 100.
  • Roads satisfy 1 <= edges.length <= n * (n - 1) / 2, and each pair of cities appears at most once.
  • Length satisfies 1 <= w <= 10000.
  • Threshold satisfies 1 <= distanceThreshold <= 10000.

Example 1. Input n = 4, edges = [[0,1,3],[1,2,1],[1,3,4],[2,3,1]], distanceThreshold = 4, output [0,2].

Example 2. Input n = 4, edges = [[0,1,1],[2,3,1]], distanceThreshold = 1, output [0,1].

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method returns the neighbor count with the city, and a tie goes to the smallest index. The usual statement returns only the city with the greatest index on a tie.

Hint

Does an undirected road fill one table entry or two?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The cue is a question about distances between all pairs of cities on a graph with at most 100 cities. The method fills a distance table with the three loops of this lesson. Each undirected road writes the weight into both directions of the table, because one road serves both ways. After the loops, it counts for each city the other cities with a finite distance of at most the threshold. It keeps the city with the smallest count, and a strict comparison keeps the earlier, smaller index on a tie.

The invariant is that after the round for k, each entry is the lowest cost of a route with intermediates 0 to k. The usual statement lets a tie go to the greatest index. This contract sends it to the smallest index and also returns the count, so the scan runs in increasing city order with a strict less-than test. Distances stay below 100 times 10000, so int holds them. The method never adds the sentinel, because it tests both parts first.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n^3) for the table and O(n^2) for the counting scan.
  • Space is O(n^2) for the table.
import java.util.*;

public final class FwCityThreshold {
    /**
     * Returns {city, neighbor count} with the fewest neighbors, and the smallest index on a tie.
     * Time: O(n^3). Space: O(n^2).
     * Invariant: after the round for k, intermediates are limited to 0..k.
     */
    static int[] pick(int n, int[][] edges, int threshold) {
        final int NONE = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
        int[][] dist = new int[n][n];
        // Every pair starts with no known route.
        for (int[] row : dist) Arrays.fill(row, NONE);
        // A city reaches itself at cost zero.
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) dist[v][v] = 0;
        // A road is two-way, so it fills both table entries.
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            dist[e[0]][e[1]] = Math.min(dist[e[0]][e[1]], e[2]);
            dist[e[1]][e[0]] = Math.min(dist[e[1]][e[0]], e[2]);
        }
        // The outermost loop adds one allowed intermediate city per round.
        for (int k = 0; k < n; k++) {
            // First city of the pair.
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                // The sentinel is never added to anything.
                if (dist[i][k] == NONE) continue;
                // Last city of the pair.
                for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                    if (dist[k][j] == NONE) continue;
                    // Both parts are finite, and the sum stays far below the int limit.
                    int through = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j];
                    // Keep the cheaper route.
                    if (through < dist[i][j]) dist[i][j] = through;
                }
            }
        }
        int bestCity = -1;
        int bestCount = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
        // Cities are scanned in increasing order, so a strict test keeps the smaller index on a tie.
        for (int c = 0; c < n; c++) {
            int count = 0;
            // Count other cities within the threshold, skipping the sentinel by the comparison itself.
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) if (j != c && dist[c][j] <= threshold) count++;
            // A strictly smaller count replaces the current best.
            if (count < bestCount) {
                bestCount = count;
                bestCity = c;
            }
        }
        // Return the city and its neighbor count.
        return new int[] {bestCity, bestCount};
    }

    /** Oracle: repeated passes over all roads from every source, then the same counting rule. */
    static int[] oracle(int n, int[][] edges, int threshold) {
        int bestCity = -1;
        int bestCount = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
        for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
            long[] d = new long[n];
            Arrays.fill(d, -1);
            d[s] = 0;
            boolean changed = true;
            while (changed) {
                changed = false;
                for (int[] e : edges) {
                    for (int dir = 0; dir < 2; dir++) {
                        int a = dir == 0 ? e[0] : e[1];
                        int b = dir == 0 ? e[1] : e[0];
                        if (d[a] >= 0 && (d[b] == -1 || d[a] + e[2] < d[b])) {
