                            d[b] = d[a] + e[2];
                            changed = true;
                        }
                    }
                }
            }
            int count = 0;
            for (int t = 0; t < n; t++) if (t != s && d[t] != -1 && d[t] <= threshold) count++;
            if (count < bestCount) {
                bestCount = count;
                bestCity = s;
            }
        }
        return new int[] {bestCity, bestCount};
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (!Arrays.equals(pick(4, new int[][] {{0, 1, 3}, {1, 2, 1}, {1, 3, 4}, {2, 3, 1}}, 4), new int[] {0, 2})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!Arrays.equals(pick(4, new int[][] {{0, 1, 1}, {2, 3, 1}}, 1), new int[] {0, 1})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // Java fact: an int sentinel added to a positive weight would wrap, which the guard avoids.
        if (Integer.MAX_VALUE + 1 >= 0) throw new AssertionError("wrap");
        // Random connected-or-not graphs with distinct pairs, against the oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(2504);
        for (int c = 0; c < 2000; c++) {
            int n = 2 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            List<int[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int a = 0; a < n; a++) for (int b = a + 1; b < n; b++) if (rnd.nextInt(3) == 0) list.add(new int[] {a, b, 1 + rnd.nextInt(6)});
            // The contract needs at least one road.
            if (list.isEmpty()) list.add(new int[] {0, 1, 1 + rnd.nextInt(6)});
            int[][] edges = list.toArray(new int[0][]);
            int threshold = 1 + rnd.nextInt(10);
            if (!Arrays.equals(pick(n, edges, threshold), oracle(n, edges, threshold))) throw new AssertionError("random " + c);
        }
    }
}verified

Grow A Spanning Tree With Prim

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Wiring Offices With Least Cable

A Plan That Overpays

A company opens six offices in one city and must link them all to one network. A cable run between two offices has a known price, and a river or a highway blocks some pairs entirely. The first plan lays the cheapest cable out of the head office, then the cheapest cable out of the office it just reached, and so on. The plan links every office, yet the invoice is larger than a different layout would cost. The plan looks at the cables of one office and ignores the cables that other linked offices could offer.

Modeling The Problem

Model the offices as vertices and each possible cable run as an undirected edge with a weight, which is its price. A spanning tree is a set of edges that connects all n vertices and has no cycle, so it uses exactly n - 1 edges. Its cost is the sum of its weights. The task is to find a spanning tree of minimum cost, and to report when no tree exists because some office is out of reach.

This lesson assumes an undirected graph with vertices numbered 0 to n - 1, edges given as triples [a, b, weight], and an input that the method never changes. Weights are whole numbers that are never negative. It asks which edge the method should add next, so that the finished tree is the cheapest one.

Simple approach

Walking To The Nearest Office

The first attempt follows the plan from the story. It keeps the set of linked vertices and a current vertex, which starts at vertex 0. At each step it looks at the edges of the current vertex only. It picks the cheapest edge to a vertex that is not linked yet. It adds that vertex and makes it the new current vertex.

static long nearestWalk(int n, int[][] edges) {
    List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int[] e : edges) {
        adj.get(e[0]).add(new int[] {e[1], e[2]});
        adj.get(e[1]).add(new int[] {e[0], e[2]});
    }
    boolean[] linked = new boolean[n];
    linked[0] = true;
    int cur = 0;
    int count = 1;
    long total = 0;
    while (count < n) {
        int bestV = -1;
        long bestW = Long.MAX_VALUE;
        for (int[] r : adj.get(cur)) {
            if (!linked[r[0]] && r[1] < bestW) {
                bestW = r[1];
                bestV = r[0];
            }
        }
        if (bestV == -1) return -1;
        linked[bestV] = true;
        total += bestW;
        cur = bestV;
        count++;
    }
    return total;
}

Each step scans the edges of one vertex, so the whole walk reads every edge at most twice. It always picks the lowest price it can see. The question is whether the lowest visible price leads to the lowest total.

Predict first: Use the edges 0 to 1 with weight 1, 0 to 2 with weight 2, and 1 to 2 with weight 5. What total does nearestWalk return, and what is the minimum cost?

It returns 6, but the minimum is 3. The walk takes edge 0 to 1 for 1, makes vertex 1 current, and then sees only the edge 1 to 2 for 5. The cheap edge 0 to 2 for 2 belongs to vertex 0, which is no longer current, so the walk never compares it. The tree 0 to 1 and 0 to 2 costs 3.

Bottleneck

The Walk Sees One Vertex

A Wrong Or Missing Answer

The walk compares only the edges of the latest vertex. Every earlier vertex still owns edges that may be cheaper, and the walk forgets them. This is a wrong answer, and it can be worse than a high price. Take the edges 0 to 1 and 0 to 2 in a graph where vertices 1 and 2 have no other edges. The walk moves to vertex 1 and finds no unlinked neighbor. It then returns minus one, although the graph is connected.

Why A Rescan Is Too Slow

A correct repair compares the edges of all linked vertices at every step. A plain version scans the full edge list in each of the n - 1 rounds, keeps the cheapest edge with exactly one endpoint linked, and adds it. That version is correct, but it costs O(V * E) time, where V is the number of vertices and E is the number of edges. A network with 10,000 offices and 100,000 possible runs needs about a billion edge reads.

A better method must remember the useful edges of every linked vertex between steps. It must also return the cheapest of them quickly, without rescanning the list. Then each edge enters the structure once, and the cost of ordering the edges is the only extra price.

Key idea

Always Take The Cheapest Crossing Edge

The method grows one tree from vertex 0. At each step it adds the cheapest edge that joins the tree to a vertex outside the tree.

The Crossing Edge

Call the vertices already in the tree the inside, and all other vertices the outside. An edge is crossing when exactly one of its endpoints is inside. Every spanning tree must contain at least one crossing edge, because the outside vertices have to connect to the inside somehow. An edge with both endpoints inside would close a cycle, and an edge with both endpoints outside does not touch the tree yet.

Why The Cheapest One Is Safe

Take the cheapest crossing edge e and any minimum spanning tree T built on the current tree. Suppose T omits e. Adding e to T closes a cycle. That cycle leaves the inside through e, so it must come back through another crossing edge f. Because e is the cheapest crossing edge, f weighs at least as much as e. Remove f and keep e. The result is still a spanning tree, and its cost is not higher. So some minimum spanning tree contains e, and adding e never spoils the final answer. Each added edge keeps that statement true, so the last tree is minimum.

Keeping The Frontier In A Heap

The frontier is the set of crossing edges at one moment. The method stores the frontier in a priority queue, a binary heap that returns its smallest weight first. When a vertex joins the tree, the method pushes every edge from that vertex to an outside vertex, as the pair of weight and far vertex. Removing the smallest pair gives the cheapest crossing edge in O(log E) time, with no rescan of the edge list.

Skipping Outdated Heap Entries

An edge can leave the frontier without leaving the heap. Suppose two inside vertices both have an edge to the same outside vertex x. The cheaper entry joins x, and the other entry now points to a vertex that is inside. Java's PriorityQueue cannot delete an arbitrary entry cheaply, so the entry stays. It is stale, because its far vertex is already in the tree and it is no longer crossing. When a stale entry leaves the heap, the method finds the far vertex flagged as inside and skips the entry. The first trace below shows four such entries leaving the heap and being skipped.

State

Tree Flags, Heap And Total

The method reads n, the list edges of triples [a, b, weight], and it changes none of them. It builds an adjacency list in which each edge appears under both endpoints, and it keeps these working values.

  • inTree is a boolean[] of length n; true marks a vertex that already joined the tree.
  • heap is a PriorityQueue<long[]> whose entries are {weight, vertex}, ordered by weight with Long.compare.
  • total is a long sum of the accepted weights, because many large weights exceed the range of int.
  • joined is the number of vertices in the tree, and the tree is complete when it equals n.

The first heap entry is {0, 0}, which joins vertex 0 at no cost. A graph that is not connected leaves joined below n when the heap empties, and the method then returns minus one. That value only signals joined < n, and the method returns it in no other case. The inputs of this lesson have weights of zero or more.

Walkthrough

Two Runs On Small Networks

Six Offices With One Cheapest Tree

The first network has six vertices and nine edges, written as pairs with weights: 0-1 costs 4, 0-2 costs 3, 1-2 costs 1, 1-3 costs 2, 2-3 costs 4, 2-4 costs 5, 3-4 costs 6, 3-5 costs 7 and 4-5 costs 3. Each cell is one heap entry in the order in which the entries leave the heap, written as vertex and weight. The pointer pop marks the entry that leaves at that step. The variable w is the weight of that entry, heap is the number of entries left afterward, and total is the tree weight so far. At step 1, vertex 2 joins by the edge of weight 3, which is cheaper than the edge to vertex 1 of weight 4. At step 4 the entry 1@4 leaves the heap, and the method drops it as stale, because vertex 1 joined at step 2. The final tree weighs 14, and it uses the edges of weight 3, 1, 2, 5 and 3. Four of the ten heap entries are stale.

A Heap Key That Adds Distances

The second network also has six vertices. A hub edge joins 0 to each of 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5 with the weights 5, 6, 7, 8 and 9, and a chain edge of weight 2 joins each neighbor pair from 1 to 5. This run changes one thing in the method. It pushes the distance from vertex 0 as the heap key and not the edge weight, which is the rule of Dijkstra's method. The variable key is the removed key, edge is the weight of the edge that attached the vertex, and total is the tree weight so far. Every direct edge from vertex 0 beats the chain route to its vertex. So each vertex attaches straight to vertex 0, and the keys equal the direct weights 5 to 9. The tree weighs 35, while the minimum spanning tree uses the chain and weighs 13.

Code

Prim With A Priority Queue

The method returns the minimum tree weight as a long, or minus one when the graph is not connected. It assumes n >= 1 and never changes edges.

static long primTotal(int n, int[][] edges) {
    List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int[] e : edges) {
        adj.get(e[0]).add(new int[] {e[1], e[2]});
        adj.get(e[1]).add(new int[] {e[0], e[2]});
    }
    boolean[] inTree = new boolean[n];
    PriorityQueue<long[]> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Long.compare(a[0], b[0]));
    heap.add(new long[] {0, 0});
    long total = 0;
    int joined = 0;
    while (!heap.isEmpty() && joined < n) {
        long[] top = heap.poll();
        int v = (int) top[1];
        if (inTree[v]) continue;
        inTree[v] = true;
        total += top[0];
        joined++;
        for (int[] r : adj.get(v)) {
            if (!inTree[r[0]]) heap.add(new long[] {r[1], r[0]});
        }
    }
    return joined == n ? total : -1;
}

The test inTree[v] must come before the update, so that a stale entry adds no weight. Each edge appears in two adjacency lists, so the heap receives at most 2E entries plus the first one. Every entry costs O(log E) to insert and to remove, so the method runs in O(E log E) time, which equals O(E log V). It uses O(V + E) memory for the adjacency list, the flags and the heap. A self loop lands on a vertex that is already inside, so the method never pushes it.

When to use it

Recognizing Spanning Tree Problems

Reading The Cue

Use this method when the statement gives an undirected weighted graph and asks for the cheapest way to connect every vertex. Cable between offices, pipes between buildings and links between data centers all have this shape. The answer is usually the total weight, the list of chosen edges, or a number derived from the chosen edges, such as the heaviest one. A graph that gives each vertex many neighbors suits Prim well, since the method expands one vertex at a time.

Checking The Invariant

An invariant is a fact that no pass of the loop is allowed to break. Here the invariant is that the tree built so far lies inside some minimum spanning tree, and that the heap holds every crossing edge. It depends on two conditions in the code. The stale test must run before any update, and the method must push only edges from a newly joined vertex. Test a graph with two equal cheapest crossing edges, where either choice is safe. Test a disconnected graph, where the heap empties early. Test one vertex alone, which needs no edge and costs 0.

Avoiding The False Friend

A false friend is a familiar method that looks right and fails. The first false friend is Dijkstra's method. It has the same heap loop, but its key is the distance from the source, so it builds a shortest path tree and not a minimum spanning tree. The second trace shows the difference in weight. A second false friend is the nearest neighbor walk of the naive stage, which compares the edges of one vertex and not the whole frontier.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Grow One Tree Edge

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A two-way network has n sites numbered from 0. The array inTree of type boolean[] marks the vertices that are in the tree. The array edges lists triples [a, b, w]. An edge is crossing when exactly one of a and b is in the tree. Return the index in edges of the crossing edge with the smallest weight, preferring the smaller index on a tie. Return -1 when no edge is crossing.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 1000, and inTree.length == n.
  • Edges satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 5000, and repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Weight satisfies 0 <= w <= 2000000000.
  • Mutation is not allowed, and the method changes neither array.

Example 1. Input inTree = [true,true,false,false], edges = [[0,1,1],[1,2,6],[0,3,4],[2,3,4]], output 2.

Example 2. Input inTree = [true,true,true], edges = [[0,1,2],[1,2,3]], output -1.

Prerequisites. The crossing edge from this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. The method returns the position of the edge and not its weight, so the tie rule decides the output.

Hint

Which edges does the test "exactly one endpoint inside" reject, and what does a self loop do to that test?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method scans the edge list once and keeps the index of the best crossing edge seen so far. For each edge it tests whether exactly one endpoint is in the tree, by comparing the two flags with !=. A self loop has equal flags on both ends, so the test rejects it. An edge replaces the current best only when its weight is strictly smaller, so the first edge among equal weights keeps its place and the smaller index wins.

The invariant is that after reading the first i edges, best holds the crossing edge with the smallest weight among them, with the smallest index among equals. The strict comparison keeps the invariant when a tie appears. The method returns -1 when best never changed, which means that no edge is crossing.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(E), because the method reads each edge once.
  • Space is O(1), because it keeps one index and no copy of the input.
import java.util.*;

public final class PrimOneEdge {
    /**
     * Returns the index of the cheapest crossing edge, or -1 when none crosses.
     * Time: O(E). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: best is the cheapest crossing edge among the edges read so far, smallest index on ties.
     */
    static int cheapestCrossing(boolean[] inTree, int[][] edges) {
        // No crossing edge is known before the first edge is read.
        int best = -1;
        // One pass reads every edge once, which is the whole cost of the method.
        for (int i = 0; i < edges.length; i++) {
            // Exactly one endpoint inside means the two flags differ; a self loop never differs.
            if (inTree[edges[i][0]] == inTree[edges[i][1]]) continue;
            // A strictly smaller weight replaces the best, so an equal weight keeps the earlier index.
            if (best == -1 || edges[i][2] < edges[best][2]) best = i;
        }
        // The value -1 survives only when no edge crossed.
        return best;
    }

    /** Oracle: collect all crossing indices, sort by weight then index, take the first. */
    static int oracle(boolean[] inTree, int[][] edges) {
        List<Integer> cand = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < edges.length; i++) {
            int a = edges[i][0], b = edges[i][1];
            if ((inTree[a] && !inTree[b]) || (!inTree[a] && inTree[b])) cand.add(i);
        }
        cand.sort((x, y) -> edges[x][2] != edges[y][2] ? Integer.compare(edges[x][2], edges[y][2]) : Integer.compare(x, y));
        return cand.isEmpty() ? -1 : cand.get(0);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (cheapestCrossing(new boolean[] {true, true, false, false}, new int[][] {{0, 1, 1}, {1, 2, 6}, {0, 3, 4}, {2, 3, 4}}) != 2) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (cheapestCrossing(new boolean[] {true, true, true}, new int[][] {{0, 1, 2}, {1, 2, 3}}) != -1) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // A tie keeps the smaller index, and a self loop never crosses.
        if (cheapestCrossing(new boolean[] {true, false}, new int[][] {{1, 1, 0}, {0, 1, 5}, {1, 0, 5}}) != 1) throw new AssertionError("tie");
        // The method leaves its input unchanged.
        boolean[] flags = {true, false, false};
        int[][] es = {{0, 1, 3}, {1, 2, 1}};
        cheapestCrossing(flags, es);
        if (!Arrays.equals(flags, new boolean[] {true, false, false}) || es[0][2] != 3 || es[1][2] != 1) throw new AssertionError("mutation");
        // Random graphs with repeats, self loops, equal weights and the largest weight.
        Random rnd = new Random(2531);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            boolean[] in = new boolean[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) in[i] = rnd.nextBoolean();
            int m = rnd.nextInt(10);
            int[][] e = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) e[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(5) == 0 ? 2_000_000_000 : rnd.nextInt(4)};
            if (cheapestCrossing(in, e) != oracle(in, e)) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Skip Stale Heap Edges

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Consider a network of n offices numbered from 0, where each element [a, b, w] of edges is a two-way cable of price w. Run Prim's method from vertex 0 with a heap of pairs {w, vertex} ordered by weight and then by vertex. When a vertex joins, push one pair for each incident edge whose other endpoint is not in the tree yet. Continue until the heap is empty. Return the number of removed pairs whose vertex is already in the tree.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 1000.
  • Edges satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 5000, and repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Weight satisfies 0 <= w <= 1000.
  • Graph may be disconnected, and only vertices reachable from vertex 0 take part.

Example 1. Input n = 3, edges = [[0,1,1],[0,2,2],[1,2,5]], output 1.

Example 2. Input n = 3, edges = [[0,1,4],[1,2,2]], output 0.

Prerequisites. The exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method counts the skipped pairs and not the tree weight, so the order in which equal weights leave the heap decides how many pairs become stale.

Hint

When does a pair stay in the heap after its vertex joined by another pair?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method runs the lazy Prim loop from vertex 0 and counts instead of summing. The heap compares weight first and vertex second, so equal weights still leave in a fixed order. A removed pair whose vertex is already in the tree increments the counter and does nothing else. Any other pair joins its vertex and pushes one pair for each incident edge that leads to a vertex outside the tree.

The invariant is that the heap holds only pairs pushed from tree vertices, and a pair is stale exactly when its vertex joined after the push. A pair becomes stale only when another pair for the same vertex leaving the heap first. The loop runs until the heap is empty, so every pushed pair leaves. The count therefore equals the number of pushes minus the number of joined vertices other than vertex 0.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(E log E), because the heap receives at most 2E pairs and each pair costs O(log E) to insert and to remove.
  • Space is O(V + E) for the adjacency list, the flags and a heap of at most 2E pairs.
import java.util.*;

public final class PrimStaleEdges {
    /**
     * Returns how many removed heap pairs are stale in a Prim run from vertex 0.
     * Time: O(E log E). Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: a pair is stale exactly when its vertex joined the tree after the pair was pushed.
     */
    static int staleCount(int n, int[][] edges) {
        List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
        // Each undirected edge is stored under both endpoints.
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            adj.get(e[0]).add(new int[] {e[1], e[2]});
            adj.get(e[1]).add(new int[] {e[0], e[2]});
        }
        boolean[] inTree = new boolean[n];
        // Weight decides first, and the vertex index breaks a tie.
        PriorityQueue<long[]> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> a[0] != b[0] ? Long.compare(a[0], b[0]) : Long.compare(a[1], b[1]));
        heap.add(new long[] {0, 0});
        int stale = 0;
        // Each pass removes one pair, and the heap receives at most two pairs per edge.
        while (!heap.isEmpty()) {
            long[] top = heap.poll();
            int v = (int) top[1];
            // A pair whose vertex is inside is stale, so it is counted and skipped.
            if (inTree[v]) {
                stale++;
                continue;
            }
            inTree[v] = true;
            // Only edges that lead outside the tree enter the heap.
            for (int[] r : adj.get(v)) {
                if (!inTree[r[0]]) heap.add(new long[] {r[1], r[0]});
            }
        }
        return stale;
    }

    /** Oracle: the same rules with a linear scan of a list in place of the heap. */
    static int oracle(int n, int[][] edges) {
        boolean[] in = new boolean[n];
        List<long[]> pool = new ArrayList<>();
        pool.add(new long[] {0, 0});
        int stale = 0;
        while (!pool.isEmpty()) {
            int bi = 0;
            for (int i = 1; i < pool.size(); i++) {
                long[] p = pool.get(i), q = pool.get(bi);
                if (p[0] < q[0] || (p[0] == q[0] && p[1] < q[1])) bi = i;
            }
            long[] top = pool.remove(bi);
            int v = (int) top[1];
            if (in[v]) { stale++; continue; }
            in[v] = true;
            for (int[] e : edges) {
                if (e[0] == v && !in[e[1]]) pool.add(new long[] {e[2], e[1]});
                if (e[1] == v && !in[e[0]]) pool.add(new long[] {e[2], e[0]});
            }
        }
        return stale;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (staleCount(3, new int[][] {{0, 1, 1}, {0, 2, 2}, {1, 2, 5}}) != 1) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (staleCount(3, new int[][] {{0, 1, 4}, {1, 2, 2}}) != 0) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // A disconnected vertex adds no pairs, and a lone vertex has none either.
        if (staleCount(4, new int[][] {{0, 1, 2}}) != 0 || staleCount(1, new int[0][]) != 0) throw new AssertionError("isolated");
        // Random graphs with repeats, self loops and many equal weights.
        Random rnd = new Random(2532);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(7);
            int m = rnd.nextInt(14);
            int[][] e = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) e[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(4)};
            if (staleCount(n, e) != oracle(n, e)) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Disconnected Graph

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Take n vertices numbered from 0 and the undirected cables edges, where [a, b, w] joins a and b at weight w. Return the weight of a minimum spanning tree as a long. Return -1 when no spanning tree exists because the graph is not connected. A graph with one vertex has a tree of weight 0.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 2000.
  • Edges satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 8000, and repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Weight satisfies 0 <= w <= 2000000000.
  • Sums may exceed the range of int, and the result uses long.

Example 1. Input n = 3, edges = [[0,1,2000000000],[1,2,2000000000]], output 4000000000.

Example 2. Input n = 4, edges = [[0,1,3],[2,3,1]], output -1.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method compares the number of joined vertices with n after the heap empties, and it returns minus one in place of a partial total.

Hint

What does the heap contain when the last reachable vertex has joined, and how many vertices joined by then?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method runs lazy Prim from vertex 0 and counts the vertices that join. The total is a long, because two weights near two billion already exceed the range of int. When the heap empties, the method compares joined with n. A smaller count means that some vertex has no path to vertex 0, so no spanning tree exists and the method returns minus one. A graph with one vertex joins its only vertex at weight 0 and returns 0.

The invariant is that the tree is a minimum spanning tree of the vertices it covers, after each join, and the heap holds the crossing edges of that tree. When the heap is empty, no crossing edge remains, so the tree already spans the whole component. The tree covers the whole graph exactly when joined == n.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(E log E), because the method inserts and removes each of at most 2E pairs once.
  • Space is O(V + E) for the adjacency list, the flags and the heap.
import java.util.*;

public final class PrimDisconnected {
    /**
     * Returns the minimum spanning tree weight, or -1 when the graph is not connected.
     * Time: O(E log E). Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: the tree is a minimum spanning tree of the vertices it covers.
     */
    static long mstWeight(int n, int[][] edges) {
        List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
        // Each undirected edge is stored under both endpoints.
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            adj.get(e[0]).add(new int[] {e[1], e[2]});
            adj.get(e[1]).add(new int[] {e[0], e[2]});
        }
        boolean[] inTree = new boolean[n];
        PriorityQueue<long[]> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Long.compare(a[0], b[0]));
        // The first pair joins vertex 0 at no cost.
        heap.add(new long[] {0, 0});
        long total = 0;
        int joined = 0;
        // The loop ends when the heap is empty, which is when no crossing edge remains.
        while (!heap.isEmpty()) {
            long[] top = heap.poll();
            int v = (int) top[1];
            // A stale pair adds nothing.
            if (inTree[v]) continue;
            inTree[v] = true;
            // The sum is a long, so large weights cannot wrap.
            total += top[0];
            joined++;
            for (int[] r : adj.get(v)) {
                if (!inTree[r[0]]) heap.add(new long[] {r[1], r[0]});
            }
        }
        // A short count means a vertex is unreachable, so no tree exists.
        return joined == n ? total : -1;
    }

    /** Oracle: Kruskal with a union-find array, then a check that one component remains. */
    static long oracle(int n, int[][] edges) {
        int[] parent = new int[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) parent[i] = i;
        int[][] sorted = edges.clone();
        Arrays.sort(sorted, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[2], b[2]));
        long total = 0;
        int used = 0;
        for (int[] e : sorted) {
            int a = find(parent, e[0]), b = find(parent, e[1]);
            if (a != b) {
                parent[a] = b;
                total += e[2];
                used++;
            }
        }
        return used == n - 1 ? total : -1;
    }

    static int find(int[] parent, int x) {
        while (parent[x] != x) {
            parent[x] = parent[parent[x]];
            x = parent[x];
        }
        return x;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (mstWeight(3, new int[][] {{0, 1, 2_000_000_000}, {1, 2, 2_000_000_000}}) != 4_000_000_000L) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (mstWeight(4, new int[][] {{0, 1, 3}, {2, 3, 1}}) != -1) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // Java fact: the same sum in int arithmetic wraps to a negative value.
        int wrapped = 2_000_000_000 + 2_000_000_000;
        if (wrapped >= 0) throw new AssertionError("int overflow");
        // One vertex costs 0, and two vertices without an edge have no tree.
        if (mstWeight(1, new int[0][]) != 0 || mstWeight(2, new int[0][]) != -1) throw new AssertionError("tiny");
        // Random graphs, many of them disconnected, with large and small weights.
        Random rnd = new Random(2533);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            int m = rnd.nextInt(12);
            int[][] e = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) e[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(3) == 0 ? 2_000_000_000 - rnd.nextInt(3) : rnd.nextInt(6)};
            if (mstWeight(n, e) != oracle(n, e)) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Min Cost to Connect All Points

LeetCode 1584

Problem Specification. Each point [x, y] in points is a vertex. The edge between two points has weight |x1 - x2| + |y1 - y2|. Return a two-element long[] whose entry 0 holds the weight of a minimum spanning tree of the complete graph on the points. The second entry is the weight of the heaviest edge in that tree, or 0 when the tree has no edge.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Points satisfy 1 <= points.length <= 1000, and all points are distinct.
  • Coordinates satisfy -1000000 <= x, y <= 1000000.
  • Sums may exceed the range of int, and the result uses long.
  • Mutation is not allowed, and the method changes no array.

Example 1. Input points = [[0,0],[1,1],[1,4],[6,1]], output [10,5].

Example 2. Input points = [[0,0],[0,5],[0,6]], output [6,5].

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method returns the heaviest tree edge as well as the total. The usual statement asks for the total only.

Hint

The graph has an edge for every pair of points, which is n(n-1)/2 edges. List those edges, then run heap Prim on them.

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The graph is complete, so it has about n * n / 2 edges, and a heap of all of them would hold up to a million pairs. The method uses the array form of Prim instead. The array key holds, for each outside point, the weight of the cheapest edge to the tree, and it starts at infinity except for point 0. Each round scans key for the smallest outside entry and adds it to the total. It updates the longest edge and then lowers the other outside entries by their Manhattan distance to the new point. The scan replaces the heap. The method computes each distance on demand, so no edge list exists.

The invariant is that key[v] is the cheapest edge from the tree to the outside point v, which means the array holds the frontier in compact form. The smallest key is therefore the cheapest crossing edge and is safe. The longest edge is the maximum of the accepted keys. The first accepted key is 0 for point 0, so a single point returns {0, 0}.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n^2), because each of n rounds scans n entries once to pick and once to update.
  • Space is O(n) for the key and flag arrays, since the edges are never stored.
import java.util.*;

public final class PrimConnectPoints {
    /**
     * Returns {minimum spanning tree weight, heaviest tree edge} under Manhattan distance.
     * Time: O(n^2). Space: O(n).
     * Invariant: key[v] is the cheapest edge from the tree to the outside point v.
     */
    static long[] connect(int[][] points) {
        int n = points.length;
        long[] key = new long[n];
        // Long.MAX_VALUE marks a point with no known edge to the tree.
        Arrays.fill(key, Long.MAX_VALUE);
        key[0] = 0;
        boolean[] inTree = new boolean[n];
        long total = 0;
        long longest = 0;
        // Each round adds exactly one point, so n rounds build the tree.
        for (int round = 0; round < n; round++) {
            int pick = -1;
            // This scan finds the cheapest crossing edge in O(n).
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
                if (!inTree[v] && (pick == -1 || key[v] < key[pick])) pick = v;
            }
            inTree[pick] = true;
            // The accepted key is the weight of the edge that attaches the point.
            total += key[pick];
            longest = Math.max(longest, key[pick]);
            // This scan lowers each outside key by the distance to the new point.
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
                if (inTree[v]) continue;
                long d = (long) Math.abs(points[pick][0] - points[v][0]) + Math.abs(points[pick][1] - points[v][1]);
                if (d < key[v]) key[v] = d;
            }
        }
        return new long[] {total, longest};
    }

    /** Oracle: Kruskal over all pairs; the last accepted edge is the heaviest. */
    static long[] oracle(int[][] points) {
        int n = points.length;
        List<long[]> all = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
            for (int j = i + 1; j < n; j++)
                all.add(new long[] {(long) Math.abs(points[i][0] - points[j][0]) + Math.abs(points[i][1] - points[j][1]), i, j});
        all.sort((a, b) -> Long.compare(a[0], b[0]));
        int[] parent = new int[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) parent[i] = i;
        long total = 0, longest = 0;
        for (long[] e : all) {
            int a = find(parent, (int) e[1]), b = find(parent, (int) e[2]);
            if (a != b) {
                parent[a] = b;
                total += e[0];
                longest = e[0];
            }
        }
        return new long[] {total, longest};
    }

    static int find(int[] parent, int x) {
        while (parent[x] != x) {
            parent[x] = parent[parent[x]];
            x = parent[x];
        }
        return x;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (!Arrays.equals(connect(new int[][] {{0, 0}, {1, 1}, {1, 4}, {6, 1}}), new long[] {10, 5})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!Arrays.equals(connect(new int[][] {{0, 0}, {0, 5}, {0, 6}}), new long[] {6, 5})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // A single point needs no edge.
        if (!Arrays.equals(connect(new int[][] {{3, 3}}), new long[] {0, 0})) throw new AssertionError("single");
        // Java fact: the distance between extreme points exceeds int range once many edges are summed, so long is needed.
        long far = (long) Math.abs(1_000_000 - -1_000_000) * 2;
        if (far * 1000 <= Integer.MAX_VALUE) throw new AssertionError("range");
        // Random point sets, including extreme coordinates and collinear points.
        Random rnd = new Random(2534);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            Set<Long> seen = new HashSet<>();
            List<int[]> pts = new ArrayList<>();
            int span = rnd.nextInt(3) == 0 ? 1_000_000 : 6;
            while (pts.size() < n) {
                int x = rnd.nextInt(2 * span + 1) - span, y = rnd.nextBoolean() ? 0 : rnd.nextInt(2 * span + 1) - span;
                if (seen.add(x * 4_000_000L + y)) pts.add(new int[] {x, y});
            }
            int[][] p = pts.toArray(new int[0][]);
            if (!Arrays.equals(connect(p), oracle(p))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified

Build A Spanning Tree With Kruskal

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Cheap Links Can Leave Islands

A Plan That Leaves Two Islands

A database team runs six replicas in different data halls. Any two replicas can sync over a dedicated line, and every line has a monthly bill. The team wants each replica to reach every other one, directly or through other replicas, at the lowest total bill. An engineer sorts the quotes and buys lines from the cheapest upward. The engineer skips a line when both of its replicas already own some line. After four purchases, two replicas still sit in separate islands, and no remaining quote is allowed to join them. The total looks cheap, but the network is broken.

The Graph Model

Model the replicas as vertices numbered 0 to n - 1 and each line as an undirected edge with a weight, the monthly bill. A spanning tree is a set of exactly n - 1 edges that connects all n vertices. The task is to find the spanning tree with the smallest total weight, or to report that none exists for a disconnected graph.

The graph is undirected. The vertices are numbered 0 to n - 1. Weights are whole numbers that are never negative. The input list of edges stays unchanged. This lesson asks how a program decides, one edge at a time, whether an edge belongs in the cheapest network.

Simple approach

Skip Edges Between Used Vertices

The first attempt sorts the edges by weight and walks through them. It keeps a boolean array touched, where touched[v] is true once some bought edge ends at v. The method buys an edge unless both of its endpoints are already touched. The reasoning is that such an edge adds no new vertex.

static long touchedRule(int n, int[][] edges) {
    int[][] sorted = edges.clone();
    Arrays.sort(sorted, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[2], b[2]));
    boolean[] touched = new boolean[n];
    long total = 0;
    for (int[] e : sorted) {
        if (touched[e[0]] && touched[e[1]]) continue;
        touched[e[0]] = true;
        touched[e[1]] = true;
        total += e[2];
    }
    return total;
}

The method sorts once and scans each edge once. It never buys an edge that touches only used vertices, so it looks careful. The question is whether the bought edges connect everything.

Predict first: A graph has five vertices and the edges 0-1 with weight 1, 2-3 with weight 2, 1-2 with weight 3, 3-4 with weight 4 and 0-3 with weight 5. What does touchedRule return, and is the network connected?

It returns 7, the sum of the edges 0-1, 2-3 and 3-4. The edge 1-2 is skipped, because vertices 1 and 2 are both touched by earlier purchases. But vertex 1 belongs to the pair 0-1 and vertex 2 belongs to the chain 2-3-4, and those two parts have no edge between them. The network has two islands, and a connected one needs the edge 1-2 as well, for a total of 10.

Bottleneck

A Used Vertex Does Not Mean Connected

What The Flag Cannot Tell

The flag touched[v] records that some edge ends at v. It does not record which other vertices v can reach through the bought edges. Two touched vertices can sit in different islands, and the method has no way to see that. The rule skips edges that it must buy.

A correct rule asks a different question before each purchase. Can the first endpoint already reach the second one through the edges bought so far? If it can, the new edge closes a cycle and wastes money. If it cannot, the edge joins two islands and is worth buying.

The Cost Of Asking By Search

The direct way to answer is a graph search over the bought edges. A search visits up to n vertices and n - 1 edges, so it costs O(n) per candidate edge. With m candidates the loop costs O(m * n) after the sort. For 100,000 vertices and 200,000 edges, that is about 2 * 10^10 steps, which is far too slow. The sort needs only a few million comparisons, so the repeated question is the bottleneck.

The question never changes: do these two vertices share an island? A search rediscovers the island from scratch each time. The program needs a structure that remembers the islands and merges two of them in almost constant time.

Key idea

Buy An Edge Only Between Groups

The method keeps the islands as groups and buys the cheapest edge that joins two different groups.

Groups Stored As Parent Links

Each vertex has one group, the set of vertices it reaches through bought edges. The array parent stores each group as a tree of links. The entry parent[v] is the next vertex toward the root of the group, and a root satisfies parent[v] == v. The call find(v) follows the links from v and returns the root. Two vertices share a group exactly when find returns the same root for both. This representation comes from the union-find lesson in chapter 23, and the definition here is the only one the code below needs.

The array size holds the number of vertices of a group at its root. To merge two groups, the method links the root of the smaller group below the root of the larger one, which keeps the trees shallow. The call find also halves its path, so that a later search walks fewer links.

The Bought Edges Form A Forest

A forest is a graph without a cycle. Before the first purchase, the bought edges form a forest of n single vertices. The method buys an edge only when find returns two different roots. Such an edge joins two trees of the forest and cannot close a cycle. The method skips an edge with equal roots, because that edge would close a cycle. So the bought edges stay a forest after every step, and each purchase lowers the number of groups by one. A spanning tree appears when one group is left, after exactly n - 1 purchases.

Why The Cheapest Joining Edge Is Safe

A safe edge is an edge that some cheapest spanning tree contains together with all edges bought so far. The reason a cheapest joining edge is safe is the cut property. Split the vertices into two nonempty sets. Among the edges that cross the split, a lightest edge belongs to some cheapest spanning tree.

Apply this to one group A at the moment the method examines an edge that leaves A. Every lighter edge was examined earlier. Each of those edges either lies inside a group or was bought. So no edge lighter than the current one leaves A. The current edge is a lightest edge crossing the split between A and the other vertices. Any cheapest tree that contains the bought edges can swap in this edge without raising the total.

Equal Weights And Stopping Early

When several edges have the same weight, any order among them is fine. The cut property only needs that no lighter edge crosses the split. The tree may differ between orders, but the total weight is the same. After n - 1 purchases the tree is complete, so the method stops. A disconnected graph never reaches n - 1 purchases, and the method reports that instead.

State

Groups, Sizes And A Running Total

The method reads n, the list edges of triples {u, v, weight}, and it changes none of them. It sorts a copy and keeps five values between edges.

  • sorted is a copy of edges in nondecreasing weight, and ties keep their input order because Arrays.sort on objects is stable.
  • parent is an int[] of length n where parent[v] == v marks a root.
  • size is an int[] of length n that holds the group size and is read only at a root.
  • bought counts the accepted edges, and the loop stops when it equals n - 1.
  • total is a long that adds the weights of the bought edges, because many int weights sum past the int range.

In the traces, the pointer i marks the edge under examination, and the cells hold the sorted edges written as endpoints and weight. In the first trace, ru and rv are the roots that find returns for the first and second endpoint, shown in edge order before any size swap. The method returns -1 when bought is below n - 1 at the end. That value signals a disconnected graph and is not a cost. Weights are whole numbers, and these lessons use weights of zero or more.

Walkthrough

Two Runs On Small Graphs

Seven Vertices With Equal Weights

The first graph has seven vertices and eleven edges. The cells show the sorted order. Each step shows the roots of the first and second endpoint, in edge order, and the decision. The first five edges join separate groups, so the method buys each one. The edge 0-1 has equal roots, because vertex 0 joined vertex 2 and vertex 2 joined vertex 1 earlier. The method skips it without paying. The edges 1-3 and 2-3 both weigh 5, and the stable sort puts 1-3 first. The method buys 1-3 and reaches n - 1 = 6 purchases, so it stops. The last four edges are never examined. The total is 16.

The Touched Rule Against Kruskal

The second graph has six vertices and eight edges. Each step compares the touched rule from the naive stage with the group rule. The variables naive and kruskal count the edges each rule has bought, and gap is their difference. Both rules buy the first three edges, which join separate vertices. At the fourth edge, 1-2, both endpoints are touched, so the touched rule skips it. The roots differ, so the group rule buys it. After that the touched rule buys nothing more. It ends with 3 edges and total 4, so three groups remain. The group rule stops after 5 edges and total 10.

Code

Kruskal With Parent Links

The method returns the total weight of a cheapest spanning tree as a long, or -1 when the graph is disconnected. The comparator uses Integer.compare, because the subtraction of two int weights can overflow and flip the sign. The helper find halves its path.

import java.util.Arrays;

final class KruskalTree {
    private static int find(int[] parent, int x) {
        while (parent[x] != x) {
            parent[x] = parent[parent[x]];
            x = parent[x];
        }
        return x;
    }

    static long cheapestTree(int n, int[][] edges) {
        int[][] sorted = edges.clone();
        Arrays.sort(sorted, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[2], b[2]));
        int[] parent = new int[n];
        int[] size = new int[n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
            parent[v] = v;
            size[v] = 1;
        }
        long total = 0;
        int bought = 0;
        for (int[] e : sorted) {
            if (bought == n - 1) break;
            int ru = find(parent, e[0]);
            int rv = find(parent, e[1]);
            if (ru == rv) continue;
            if (size[ru] < size[rv]) {
                int t = ru;
                ru = rv;
                rv = t;
            }
            parent[rv] = ru;
            size[ru] += size[rv];
            total += e[2];
            bought++;
        }
        return bought == n - 1 ? total : -1;
    }
}

The test ru == rv must run before the merge, because a merge of two vertices of one group would corrupt size. The early break keeps the loop from calling find on edges that cannot change the answer. The call edges.clone() copies only the array of references, which is enough because the method never writes into an edge. The method never changes the caller's array order.

Sorting costs O(m log m) for m edges. With both rules in place, each find runs in amortized near constant time. The loop adds O(m * alpha(n)), where alpha is the inverse Ackermann function. It stays below 5 for any n that fits in memory. The total time is O(m log m). The space is O(n + m), because parent and size hold n entries each and the sorted copy holds m references.

When to use it

Recognizing When Kruskal Applies

Reading The Cue

Use Kruskal when the statement asks to connect every vertex at the lowest total weight. The edges are listed or can be generated. Words such as "lowest total", "every site reachable", "no redundant link" and "report failure when it cannot connect" point to it. The edge list can be sorted once, and a union-find structure answers the group question. When the input is points with a cost for each pair, the program first generates the pairs as edges.

Checking The Invariant

The invariant is a statement that stays true before and after every loop step. Here the bought edges form a forest. The groups of parent equal the connected pieces of that forest. Every bought edge was a lightest edge leaving its group at that moment. A missing size update only slows find and leaves the answer correct. The invariant breaks when the loop buys an edge without comparing roots, or sorts by a different key. Test a graph with parallel edges, a self-loop and a single vertex. A self-loop always has equal roots and is never bought. A single vertex needs zero purchases and returns 0.

Avoiding The False Friend

A false friend is a plausible method that gives a wrong answer on some input. The false friend here is the touched rule of the naive stage, which tests vertices and not groups. It passes on graphs where the cheapest edges happen to form a tree. A second false friend is a shortest path tree from one source. That tree minimizes each distance from the source, and it can cost more in total than a cheapest spanning tree. A third mistake is to skip the cycle test when all weights are distinct and assume no cycle can form. Distinct weights do not prevent a cycle among cheap edges.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Sorted Safe Edges

Author exercise

Problem Specification. An undirected graph labels its vertices from 0 up to n - 1. The array edges holds triples {u, v, w} and is already sorted by nondecreasing w. Scan the edges in the given order and keep groups of vertices that the accepted edges connect. Accept edge k when its endpoints lie in different groups at that moment, and skip it otherwise. Scan every edge, with no early stop. Return a boolean[] of length edges.length where entry k is true when edge k is accepted.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 1000.
  • Edges satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 5000, and repeats and self-loops may occur.
  • Weights are int values in 1..1000000000.
  • Mutation does not occur, and edges stays unchanged.

Example 1. Input n = 4, edges = [[0,1,1],[2,3,2],[1,2,3],[0,3,4],[1,3,5]], output [true,true,true,false,false].

Example 2. Input n = 3, edges = [[0,0,1],[0,1,1],[0,1,2],[1,2,2]], output [false,true,false,true], because the self-loop and the repeated edge 0-1 are skipped.

Prerequisites. The group rule and the find helper of this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. The method reports the decision for every edge as a flag and does not add up any weights.

Hint

Which two values must differ for an edge to join two groups, and what do they equal for a self-loop?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method creates a parent array in which every vertex is its own root. It scans the edges in the given order. For each edge it finds the roots of both endpoints. Different roots mean the endpoints lie in different groups, so the method records true and links one root below the other. Equal roots mean the edge would close a cycle, so the method records false and changes nothing. A self-loop has the same vertex on both sides, so its roots are always equal and it is always skipped.

The invariant is that the groups of parent equal the connected pieces of the accepted edges, so the accepted edges form a forest. A merge joins exactly two pieces, and a skip leaves the pieces alone. The input is already sorted, so the method does not sort and does not touch the array.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(m * alpha(n)) for m edges, because each edge costs two find calls and at most one merge. The sorted input needs no sort.
  • Space is O(n + m), because parent holds n entries and the result holds m flags.
import java.util.*;

public final class KruskalSortedSafeEdges {
    /**
     * Returns one flag per edge that tells whether the scan accepts that edge.
     * Time: O(m * alpha(n)). Space: O(n + m).
     * Invariant: the groups of parent equal the connected pieces of the accepted edges.
     */
    static boolean[] safeFlags(int n, int[][] edges) {
        // Each vertex starts as the root of its own group.
        int[] parent = new int[n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) parent[v] = v;
        boolean[] flags = new boolean[edges.length];
        // One pass over the edges, with no early stop, as the problem requires.
        for (int k = 0; k < edges.length; k++) {
            int ru = find(parent, edges[k][0]);
            int rv = find(parent, edges[k][1]);
            // Equal roots mean the edge lies inside a group, so the flag stays false.
            if (ru == rv) continue;
            // Different roots mean the edge joins two groups, so one root moves below the other.
            parent[rv] = ru;
            flags[k] = true;
        }
        return flags;
    }

    /** Follows parent links to the root and halves the path on the way. Time: amortized near O(1). Space: O(1). */
    static int find(int[] parent, int x) {
        // The loop ends at a root, which is the only vertex that points at itself.
        while (parent[x] != x) {
            // Pointing x at its grandparent shortens later searches.
            parent[x] = parent[parent[x]];
            x = parent[x];
        }
        return x;
    }

    /** Oracle: a label per vertex, relabeled in full on every accepted edge. */
    static boolean[] oracle(int n, int[][] edges) {
        int[] label = new int[n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) label[v] = v;
        boolean[] flags = new boolean[edges.length];
        for (int k = 0; k < edges.length; k++) {
            int a = label[edges[k][0]], b = label[edges[k][1]];
            if (a == b) continue;
            // Every vertex of the second group takes the label of the first group.
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (label[v] == b) label[v] = a;
            flags[k] = true;
        }
        return flags;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (!Arrays.equals(safeFlags(4, new int[][] {{0, 1, 1}, {2, 3, 2}, {1, 2, 3}, {0, 3, 4}, {1, 3, 5}}), new boolean[] {true, true, true, false, false})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!Arrays.equals(safeFlags(3, new int[][] {{0, 0, 1}, {0, 1, 1}, {0, 1, 2}, {1, 2, 2}}), new boolean[] {false, true, false, true})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // An empty edge list gives an empty result, and one vertex accepts nothing.
        if (safeFlags(1, new int[0][]).length != 0) throw new AssertionError("empty");
        // The method leaves the input array untouched.
        int[][] keep = {{0, 1, 5}, {1, 2, 6}};
        int[][] snapshot = {{0, 1, 5}, {1, 2, 6}};
        safeFlags(3, keep);
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(keep, snapshot)) throw new AssertionError("mutated");
        // Random sorted edge lists, including self-loops and repeated edges.
        Random rnd = new Random(2504);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            int m = rnd.nextInt(14);
            int[][] edges = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) edges[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), 1 + rnd.nextInt(6)};
            Arrays.sort(edges, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[2], b[2]));
            if (!Arrays.equals(safeFlags(n, edges), oracle(n, edges))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Stop At V Minus One

Author exercise

Problem Specification. An undirected connected graph draws vertices from 0 to n - 1 and has the unsorted edge array edges of triples {u, v, w}. Sort a copy by w, keeping the input order among equal weights. Scan the sorted copy and stop as soon as n - 1 edges are accepted. Return the number of edges that the scan skips because their endpoints already share a group. Edges after the stop are not examined and are not counted.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 1000.
  • Edges satisfy n - 1 <= edges.length <= 5000, and the graph is connected.
  • Weights are int values in 0..1000000000, and equal weights occur.
  • Mutation does not occur, and edges stays unchanged.

Example 1. Input n = 5, edges = [[0,1,4],[1,2,1],[0,2,3],[2,3,2],[1,3,5],[3,4,6],[0,3,2],[1,4,7]], output 3.

Example 2. Input n = 4, edges = [[0,1,2],[1,2,2],[0,2,2],[2,3,9],[0,3,9]], output 1, because the last edge comes after the stop.

Prerequisites. The exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method counts skipped edges and not examined edges, so the early stop decides which skips are counted.

Hint

At which sorted position does the count of accepted edges reach n - 1, and which skipped edges lie before that position?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method sorts a copy of the edges by weight. Arrays.sort on an array of objects is stable, so equal weights keep their input order. It then runs the same group rule as before and counts every edge whose roots are equal. After each purchase it tests whether n - 1 edges are accepted. It leaves the loop at that moment. Edges after the stop are never examined, so they never add to the count.

The invariant has two parts. The count skipped equals the number of examined edges that closed a cycle. The groups equal the connected pieces of the accepted edges. The stop is valid because a connected graph with n - 1 accepted edges has one group, and every later edge would have equal roots. The exercise states that the graph is connected, so the loop always reaches n - 1 purchases before the edges run out.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(m log m), because the sort dominates and the loop adds O(m * alpha(n)).
  • Space is O(n + m), because the sorted copy holds m references and parent holds n entries.
import java.util.*;

public final class KruskalStopCount {
    /**
     * Returns how many edges the scan skips before it accepts n - 1 edges.
     * Time: O(m log m). Space: O(n + m).
     * Invariant: skipped counts examined edges with equal roots, and the groups match the accepted edges.
     */
    static int skippedBeforeStop(int n, int[][] edges) {
        // The copy keeps the caller's order intact, and the stable sort keeps input order among ties.
        int[][] sorted = edges.clone();
        Arrays.sort(sorted, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[2], b[2]));
        int[] parent = new int[n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) parent[v] = v;
        int bought = 0, skipped = 0;
        for (int[] e : sorted) {
            // A complete tree ends the scan, so later edges are not examined.
            if (bought == n - 1) break;
            int ru = find(parent, e[0]);
            int rv = find(parent, e[1]);
            // Equal roots mean a cycle, so the edge counts as skipped.
            if (ru == rv) {
                skipped++;
                continue;
            }
            // Different roots mean a purchase and a merge.
            parent[rv] = ru;
            bought++;
        }
        return skipped;
    }

    /** Follows parent links to the root and halves the path. Time: amortized near O(1). Space: O(1). */
    static int find(int[] parent, int x) {
        // Stops at the root, which points at itself.
        while (parent[x] != x) {
            parent[x] = parent[parent[x]];
            x = parent[x];
        }
        return x;
    }

    /** Oracle: full relabeling over a manually sorted copy, with an insertion sort that keeps ties stable. */
    static int oracle(int n, int[][] edges) {
        List<int[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            int pos = list.size();
            // Insert after the last edge of weight at most e[2], which keeps equal weights in input order.
            while (pos > 0 && list.get(pos - 1)[2] > e[2]) pos--;
            list.add(pos, e);
        }
        int[] label = new int[n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) label[v] = v;
        int bought = 0, skipped = 0;
        for (int[] e : list) {
            if (bought == n - 1) break;
            int a = label[e[0]], b = label[e[1]];
            if (a == b) { skipped++; continue; }
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (label[v] == b) label[v] = a;
            bought++;
        }
        return skipped;
    }

    /** Builds a random connected multigraph by chaining a random order of vertices and adding extra edges. */
    static int[][] randomConnected(Random rnd, int n) {
        List<int[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int v = 1; v < n; v++) list.add(new int[] {rnd.nextInt(v), v, rnd.nextInt(6)});
        int extra = rnd.nextInt(8);
        for (int i = 0; i < extra; i++) list.add(new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(6)});
        Collections.shuffle(list, rnd);
        return list.toArray(new int[0][]);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        int[][] a = {{0, 1, 4}, {1, 2, 1}, {0, 2, 3}, {2, 3, 2}, {1, 3, 5}, {3, 4, 6}, {0, 3, 2}, {1, 4, 7}};
        if (skippedBeforeStop(5, a) != 3) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        int[][] b = {{0, 1, 2}, {1, 2, 2}, {0, 2, 2}, {2, 3, 9}, {0, 3, 9}};
        if (skippedBeforeStop(4, b) != 1) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // One vertex needs no edge, and no scan happens.
        if (skippedBeforeStop(1, new int[0][]) != 0) throw new AssertionError("single");
        // Java fact: Arrays.sort on an object array is stable, so a tie keeps input order.
        int[][] ties = {{9, 9, 1}, {8, 8, 1}, {7, 7, 1}};
        int[][] copy = ties.clone();
        Arrays.sort(copy, (x, y) -> Integer.compare(x[2], y[2]));
        if (copy[0][0] != 9 || copy[1][0] != 8 || copy[2][0] != 7) throw new AssertionError("stable");
        // The input array order stays as the caller gave it.
        skippedBeforeStop(4, b);
        if (b[0][2] != 2 || b[3][2] != 9 || b[4][0] != 0) throw new AssertionError("mutated");
        // Random connected graphs with zero weights and repeated edges.
        Random rnd = new Random(2514);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            int[][] edges = randomConnected(rnd, n);
            if (skippedBeforeStop(n, edges) != oracle(n, edges)) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Equal Weights And Disconnection

Author exercise

Problem Specification. An undirected graph uses vertex labels 0 through n - 1 and the edge array edges of triples {u, v, w}. The graph may be disconnected. Accept the edges by Kruskal's rule over the whole sorted list, with any order among equal weights. The result is a long[] with two slots. Slot 0 holds the number of groups left at the end, and slot 1 holds the total weight of the accepted edges.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 1000.
  • Edges satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 5000, and repeats and self-loops may occur.
  • Weights are int values in 0..2000000000, and the total uses long.
  • Ties between equal weights may be broken in any order, and the result does not depend on it.

Example 1. Input n = 3, edges = [[0,1,2000000000],[1,2,2000000000],[0,2,2000000000]], output [1,4000000000].

Example 2. Input n = 5, edges = [[0,1,2],[1,0,2],[2,3,7]], output [3,9], because vertex 4 stays alone.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method returns a count of groups and a total for a possibly disconnected graph and does not return -1.

Hint

What does the number of accepted edges tell about the number of groups, and which type holds the sum of two large weights?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method sorts a copy of the edges, then scans all of them. It starts with groups equal to n. Each accepted edge merges two groups, so the method lowers groups by one and adds the weight to a long total. An edge with equal roots changes nothing. The scan has no early stop based on n - 1, because a disconnected graph never reaches that count. The scan ends with the last edge. The result pairs the final groups with the total.

The invariant is that groups equals the number of connected pieces of the accepted edges, and total is the sum of their weights. Equal weights may appear in any order. Two lightest crossing edges of the same weight are both safe, so either choice keeps the total the same. The solution checks this by shuffling ties. The total uses long because two weights of 2000000000 already exceed Integer.MAX_VALUE, and the solution asserts that fact.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(m log m), because the sort dominates and the loop adds O(m * alpha(n)).
  • Space is O(n + m), because the sorted copy holds m references and parent holds n entries.
import java.util.*;

public final class KruskalForestTally {
    /**
     * Returns {groups left, total weight of accepted edges} for a possibly disconnected graph.
     * Time: O(m log m). Space: O(n + m).
     * Invariant: groups counts the connected pieces of the accepted edges, and total sums their weights.
     */
    static long[] forestTally(int n, int[][] edges) {
        // Sorting a copy leaves the caller's array unchanged.
        int[][] sorted = edges.clone();
        Arrays.sort(sorted, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[2], b[2]));
        int[] parent = new int[n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) parent[v] = v;
        // Every vertex starts alone, so there are n groups.
        long groups = n;
        // A long accumulator holds sums beyond the int range.
        long total = 0;
        for (int[] e : sorted) {
            int ru = find(parent, e[0]);
            int rv = find(parent, e[1]);
            // Equal roots mean the edge would close a cycle.
            if (ru == rv) continue;
            // A merge joins two groups into one.
            parent[rv] = ru;
            groups--;
            total += e[2];
        }
        return new long[] {groups, total};
    }

    /** Follows parent links to the root and halves the path. Time: amortized near O(1). Space: O(1). */
    static int find(int[] parent, int x) {
        // The loop stops at the root.
        while (parent[x] != x) {
            parent[x] = parent[parent[x]];
            x = parent[x];
        }
        return x;
    }

    /** Oracle: counts pieces by repeated search and takes the cheapest crossing edge in each round (Boruvka style, with index ties). */
    static long[] oracle(int n, int[][] edges) {
        int[] label = new int[n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) label[v] = v;
        long total = 0;
        while (true) {
            // Find the lightest edge that joins two different pieces; the lower index wins a tie.
            int best = -1;
            for (int k = 0; k < edges.length; k++) {
                if (label[edges[k][0]] == label[edges[k][1]]) continue;
                if (best == -1 || edges[k][2] < edges[best][2]) best = k;
            }
            if (best == -1) break;
            int a = label[edges[best][0]], b = label[edges[best][1]];
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (label[v] == b) label[v] = a;
            total += edges[best][2];
        }
        Set<Integer> pieces = new HashSet<>();
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) pieces.add(label[v]);
        return new long[] {pieces.size(), total};
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        long big = 2_000_000_000L;
        if (!Arrays.equals(forestTally(3, new int[][] {{0, 1, 2_000_000_000}, {1, 2, 2_000_000_000}, {0, 2, 2_000_000_000}}), new long[] {1, 2 * big})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!Arrays.equals(forestTally(5, new int[][] {{0, 1, 2}, {1, 0, 2}, {2, 3, 7}}), new long[] {3, 9})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // Java fact: two weights of 2000000000 overflow an int sum but not a long sum.
        if (2_000_000_000 + 2_000_000_000 >= 0) throw new AssertionError("int overflow");
        if (2_000_000_000L + 2_000_000_000 != 4_000_000_000L) throw new AssertionError("long sum");
        // Java fact: subtracting extreme int weights flips the sign, which is why the sort uses Integer.compare.
        if (Integer.MAX_VALUE - (-5) >= 0) throw new AssertionError("subtract overflow");
        // No edges leave every vertex alone with total zero.
        if (!Arrays.equals(forestTally(4, new int[0][]), new long[] {4, 0})) throw new AssertionError("no edges");
        // Random graphs, and the same graph with tied edges shuffled gives the same result.
        Random rnd = new Random(2524);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            int m = rnd.nextInt(12);
            int[][] edges = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) edges[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(4)};
            long[] got = forestTally(n, edges);
            if (!Arrays.equals(got, oracle(n, edges))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
            List<int[]> shuffled = new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList(edges));
            Collections.shuffle(shuffled, rnd);
            if (!Arrays.equals(got, forestTally(n, shuffled.toArray(new int[0][])))) throw new AssertionError("ties " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Min Cost to Connect All Points

LeetCode 1584

Problem Specification. The array points holds distinct integer points {x, y}. The cost of a connection between two points is their Manhattan distance, the value |x1 - x2| + |y1 - y2|. A set of connections joins all points when every point reaches every other point through them. Among all such sets of minimum total cost, every set has the same largest connection. Return that largest connection cost, or 0 when there is one point. Use Kruskal's method on all pairs.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Points satisfy 1 <= points.length <= 500, and all points are distinct.
  • Coordinates are int values in -1000000..1000000.
  • Distances are at most 4000000 and fit in an int.
  • Mutation does not occur, and points stays unchanged.

Example 1. Input points = [[0,0],[1,1],[1,0],[5,5]], output 8.

Example 2. Input points = [[0,0],[0,4],[9,0]], output 9.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method returns the largest accepted edge and not the total cost. The solution also compares the n * (n - 1) / 2 stored edges with the memory that Prim needs.

Hint

Which accepted edge of the sorted scan carries the largest weight, and when does the scan stop?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method turns the points into edges, one edge for every pair, with the Manhattan distance as the weight. It sorts the edges, then runs the group rule. Every purchase has a weight at least as large as the one before it, so the last purchase is the heaviest accepted edge. The method returns the weight of the last purchase, and it returns 0 for a single point because no purchase happens. The answer does not depend on tie order, because every cheapest spanning tree has the same sorted list of weights.

The invariant has two parts. The accepted edges form a forest whose groups equal the connected pieces. Each accepted edge is a lightest edge that leaves its group. The heaviest accepted edge is also the smallest limit t for which the edges of weight at most t connect all points. The oracle uses that second description. The method stores all n * (n - 1) / 2 edges, which is more memory than Prim needs. Prim keeps one best distance per point and stays at O(n) extra memory on top of the points. Kruskal trades that memory for a simpler loop and an early stop.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n^2 log n), because there are about n^2 / 2 edges. The sort costs O(n^2 log n^2), which equals O(n^2 log n), and the union-find calls add O(n^2 * alpha(n)).
  • Space is O(n^2), because the method stores every pair as an edge, whereas Prim needs only O(n) beyond the input.
import java.util.*;

public final class KruskalHeaviestLink {
    /**
     * Returns the largest connection cost among the connections of a cheapest plan that joins all points.
     * Time: O(n^2 log n). Space: O(n^2).
     * Invariant: accepted edges form a forest, and each is a lightest edge leaving its group.
     */
    static int heaviestLink(int[][] points) {
        int n = points.length;
        // A single point needs no connection.
        if (n == 1) return 0;
        // One edge per pair, stored as {i, j, distance}; this is the n * (n - 1) / 2 memory cost.
        int[][] edges = new int[n * (n - 1) / 2][];
        int idx = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            for (int j = i + 1; j < n; j++) {
                int d = Math.abs(points[i][0] - points[j][0]) + Math.abs(points[i][1] - points[j][1]);
                edges[idx++] = new int[] {i, j, d};
            }
        }
        // Integer.compare avoids the overflow that a subtraction of weights could cause.
        Arrays.sort(edges, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[2], b[2]));
        int[] parent = new int[n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) parent[v] = v;
        int bought = 0, last = 0;
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            // The tree is complete after n - 1 purchases, so the scan stops.
            if (bought == n - 1) break;
            int ri = find(parent, e[0]);
            int rj = find(parent, e[1]);
            // Equal roots mean the pair is already connected.
            if (ri == rj) continue;
            parent[rj] = ri;
            // Weights come in nondecreasing order, so the latest purchase is the heaviest so far.
            last = e[2];
            bought++;
        }
        return last;
    }

    /** Follows parent links to the root and halves the path. Time: amortized near O(1). Space: O(1). */
    static int find(int[] parent, int x) {
        // The loop ends at the root.
        while (parent[x] != x) {
            parent[x] = parent[parent[x]];
            x = parent[x];
        }
        return x;
    }

    /** Oracle: the smallest limit t such that pairs at distance at most t connect all points, tested by flood fill. */
    static int oracle(int[][] points) {
        int n = points.length;
        for (int t = 0; ; t++) {
            boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
            Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
            stack.push(0);
            seen[0] = true;
            int count = 1;
            while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
                int u = stack.pop();
                for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
                    int d = Math.abs(points[u][0] - points[v][0]) + Math.abs(points[u][1] - points[v][1]);
                    if (!seen[v] && d <= t) {
                        seen[v] = true;
                        count++;
                        stack.push(v);
                    }
                }
            }
            if (count == n) return t;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (heaviestLink(new int[][] {{0, 0}, {1, 1}, {1, 0}, {5, 5}}) != 8) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (heaviestLink(new int[][] {{0, 0}, {0, 4}, {9, 0}}) != 9) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // One point returns 0, and two points return their distance.
        if (heaviestLink(new int[][] {{7, -3}}) != 0) throw new AssertionError("single");
        if (heaviestLink(new int[][] {{-1000000, -1000000}, {1000000, 1000000}}) != 4_000_000) throw new AssertionError("far");
        // Java fact: the pair count for 500 points is 124750 and fits an int array index.
        if (500 * 499 / 2 != 124_750) throw new AssertionError("pairs");
        // The method leaves the points unchanged.
        int[][] pts = {{3, 3}, {0, 0}};
        heaviestLink(pts);
        if (pts[0][0] != 3 || pts[1][1] != 0) throw new AssertionError("mutated");
        // Random distinct point sets against the flood fill oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(2534);
        for (int t = 0; t < 1500; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(7);
            Set<Long> used = new HashSet<>();
            int[][] pts2 = new int[n][];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                int x, y;
                do { x = rnd.nextInt(15) - 7; y = rnd.nextInt(15) - 7; } while (!used.add(x * 100L + y));
                pts2[i] = new int[] {x, y};
            }
            if (heaviestLink(pts2) != oracle(pts2)) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified

Walk Every Edge Once With Hierholzer

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

One Probe Per Link

A network tester must check every one-way link between servers. It sends one probe packet along each link. The probes travel in one continuous route, so each probe starts on the server where the previous probe ended. A link probed twice wastes time, and a link never probed stays untested. The tester has five links to cover: 0 to 1, 1 to 2, 2 to 0, 1 to 3 and 3 to 1. The route 0, 1, 3, 1, 2, 0 covers each of them exactly once.

Model the servers as vertices numbered 0 to n - 1. Model each link as a directed edge from one vertex to another. A route is a sequence of vertices in which every adjacent pair is a link. The task is to find a route that starts at a given vertex and uses every link exactly once. The method must also report when no such route exists. This lesson assumes a directed graph, a link list links of pairs [from, to], and an input that the method never changes.

The first question is how to build the route so that the work grows with the number of links. The second question is how to detect that no route exists without trying every order.

Simple approach

Following Any Unused Link

The first attempt is a greedy walk. It starts at the given vertex and follows the first unused link out of the current vertex. It repeats until the current vertex has no unused link, and then it returns the vertices it visited.

static List<Integer> greedyWalk(int[][] links, int start) {
    boolean[] used = new boolean[links.length];
    List<Integer> route = new ArrayList<>();
    route.add(start);
    int cur = start;
    boolean moved = true;
    while (moved) {
        moved = false;
        for (int i = 0; i < links.length; i++) {
            if (!used[i] && links[i][0] == cur) {
                used[i] = true;
