                cur = links[i][1];
                route.add(cur);
                moved = true;
                break;
            }
        }
    }
    return route;
}

The method never uses a link twice, because it marks each link in used. It stops when the current vertex has no unused link. The question is whether every link has been used at that point.

Predict first: Use the five links 0 to 1, 1 to 2, 2 to 0, 1 to 3 and 3 to 1, and start at vertex 0. What route does greedyWalk return, and how many links stay unused?

It returns 0, 1, 2, 0 and leaves two links unused. The walk leaves vertex 1 through the link to 2, because that link is listed first. It returns to vertex 0, and vertex 0 has no unused link. The links 1 to 3 and 3 to 1 are never visited. A valid route exists, but the greedy walk committed to the wrong order at vertex 1 and cannot go back.

Bottleneck

A Stuck Walk Cannot Resume

The greedy walk stops at the first vertex without an unused link. Unused links can remain at vertices that the walk passed earlier. The method has no way to return to such a vertex, because it keeps only the current vertex and the list of visited vertices.

One repair is to try every order of the links and keep the first order that works. A graph with E links has up to E! orders, which is O(E!) work. A second repair splices extra loops into the route. After each stuck walk, the method scans the route for a vertex with an unused link, walks a loop from there, and inserts the loop into the route. Each scan costs O(E), and a graph can need a new scan for each loop, so the total grows toward O(E^2). The greedy walk also pays O(E) for each step, because it scans the whole link list to find the next unused link.

A better method must find the next unused link of a vertex in constant time. It must also resume from an earlier vertex without scanning the route. Then each link is touched a constant number of times, and the total work is linear in the number of links.

Key idea

Recording Vertices After Their Links

The method walks until it is stuck, writes the stuck vertex to an output list, steps back one vertex, and continues from there. It reverses the output list at the end.

Counting Degrees Before Walking

A trail is a route that uses each link at most once. An Eulerian trail uses every link exactly once, and the exercises also call it an Eulerian path, and an Eulerian cycle is an Eulerian trail that ends where it starts. The out-degree of a vertex counts the links that leave it, and the in-degree counts the links that enter it. A vertex is balanced when its out-degree equals its in-degree.

An Eulerian cycle needs every vertex to be balanced, because each visit enters through one link and leaves through another. An Eulerian trail that ends elsewhere needs the same condition at every vertex except two. The start has one more outgoing link than incoming links, and the end has one more incoming link than outgoing links. The method counts out - in for each vertex and rejects any other pattern before it walks.

Walking Until A Vertex Is Stuck

The method keeps a stack of vertices, which holds the walk that is still open. It starts with the start vertex on the stack. While the vertex on top of the stack has an unused outgoing link, the method consumes that link and pushes its target. Consuming a link means that the method moves a per-vertex index past it, and the link is never offered again. The input list is not changed.

Recording A Vertex After Its Links

When the vertex on top of the stack has no unused outgoing link, the method pops it and appends it to the output list. This order is called postorder, because a vertex is recorded only after all of its outgoing links are consumed. A popped vertex can never gain a new link, so its place in the output is final. After the pop, the new top may still have unused links. The method then walks a fresh loop from it, and the loop is recorded before the vertex below it. This is the splice that the greedy walk could not do.

Reversing The Recorded Order

Read the output list from its last entry to its first entry. Each vertex was recorded after every vertex that follows it on the trail, so the reversed list is the trail in travel order.

The recorded order is right for a further reason. When every vertex is balanced, a stuck walk can only end at the vertex where it began. The stuck walk is therefore a closed loop. The method records it first and then returns to the vertex below, which is the place where the loop is spliced in.

In the case of a start with one extra outgoing link and an end with one extra incoming link, the first stuck walk ends at the end vertex. Every later stuck walk starts at a vertex that is balanced for the unused links, so it closes where it began. The splice works the same way.

The invariant is that the output list, read backward, is a valid tail of the trail and uses only consumed links. The method finishes when the stack is empty. The trail is complete when the output holds E + 1 vertices. A shorter output means that some links were not reachable from the start.

State

Stack, Cursors And Output

The method reads n, the list links of pairs [from, to], and the vertex start. It changes none of them. It builds an adjacency list from links and keeps these structures.

  • adj is a list of n lists; adj.get(v) holds the targets of the links that leave v, in the order of links.
  • balance is an int[] of length n; entry v is the out-degree minus the in-degree of v.
  • cursor is an int[] of length n; entry v is the index of the next unused link in adj.get(v).
  • stack is an int[] of length E + 1 with a count top; it holds the open walk.
  • out is an int[] of length E + 1 with a count size; it holds the recorded vertices.

The empty array is the answer when no trail exists. A vertex that has no links is valid input, and a graph with no links returns the array that holds only start.

Walkthrough

Two Runs Of The Walk

A Walk That Returns Too Early

The first run uses the five links 0 to 1, 1 to 2, 2 to 0, 1 to 3 and 3 to 1, with start vertex 0. Each cell is one event, in the order that events happen. A cell named take shows the vertex that the method pushes after it consumes a link. A cell named emit shows the vertex that the method pops and appends to the output. The pointer ev marks the current event. The variable stack is the number of vertices on the stack after the event, unused is the number of links not yet consumed, and emitted is the length of the output. The walk takes the links 0 to 1, 1 to 2 and 2 to 0 and gets stuck at vertex 0. Vertex 0 is emitted, and vertex 2 is emitted next. Vertex 1 is now on top and still owns the link 1 to 3. The method takes that link and the link 3 to 1, emits vertex 1, emits vertex 3, and emits vertex 1 and vertex 0 as the stack empties. The output is 0, 2, 1, 3, 1, 0. The reversed output 0, 1, 3, 1, 2, 0 uses all five links.

A First Link To A Dead End

The second run uses the four links 0 to 1, 0 to 2, 2 to 3 and 3 to 0, with start vertex 0. Vertex 0 has one more outgoing link than incoming links, and vertex 1 has one more incoming link than outgoing links, so the trail must end at vertex 1. The first link out of vertex 0 leads straight to vertex 1, and vertex 1 has no outgoing link. A method that prints vertices in the order that it visits them returns 0, 1 and leaves three links unused. This method emits vertex 1 first, so vertex 1 lands last after the reversal. It then continues from vertex 0 through vertices 2, 3 and back to 0. The output is 1, 0, 3, 2, 0. The reversed output 0, 2, 3, 0, 1 uses all four links.

Code

Hierholzer With An Explicit Stack

The method returns the vertices of the trail in travel order, or an empty array when no trail starts at start and uses every link. It keeps the stack in an int[], because a recursive version needs one call frame per link and can raise StackOverflowError on a long trail. The sums here count links, so int is enough.

static int[] hierholzer(int n, int[][] links, int start) {
    int m = links.length;
    List<List<Integer>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    int[] balance = new int[n];
    for (int[] e : links) {
        adj.get(e[0]).add(e[1]);
        balance[e[0]]++;
        balance[e[1]]--;
    }
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
        if (balance[v] > 1 || balance[v] < -1) return new int[0];
        if (balance[v] == 1 && v != start) return new int[0];
    }
    int[] cursor = new int[n];
    int[] stack = new int[m + 1];
    int[] out = new int[m + 1];
    int top = 0, size = 0;
    stack[top++] = start;
    while (top > 0) {
        int v = stack[top - 1];
        if (cursor[v] < adj.get(v).size()) {
            stack[top++] = adj.get(v).get(cursor[v]++);
        } else {
            out[size++] = v;
            top--;
        }
    }
    if (size != m + 1) return new int[0];
    for (int i = 0, j = size - 1; i < j; i++, j--) {
        int t = out[i]; out[i] = out[j]; out[j] = t;
    }
    return out;
}

The degree check runs first and rejects every pattern except a balanced graph or a start with one extra outgoing link. The loop pushes a target after it advances cursor[v], so each link is consumed once. The final length test catches links that the walk never reached. The method runs in O(V + E) time, because each link is pushed once and each push is matched by one pop. It uses O(V + E) memory for the adjacency list, the cursors, the stack and the output.

When to use it

Recognizing Every-Edge-Once Problems

Reading The Cue

Use this method when the statement asks for a route that uses every edge exactly once. Reconstructing a trip from a pile of one-way tickets has this shape. Testing every link of a network once and drawing every segment of a figure without lifting the pen have it too. The answer is usually the route itself, a yes or no answer, or the count of edges. A statement that asks to visit every vertex once is a different problem.

Checking The Invariant

The invariant is that the method consumes each link once while it walks, and it appends a vertex to the output only when that vertex has no unused outgoing link. The output then lists the trail in reverse. Two conditions in the code protect it. The degree check must run before the walk, and the output length must equal E + 1 after it. Test a graph with no links, a self loop, two parallel links, and a graph whose links split into two parts that the start cannot reach.

Avoiding The False Friend

A false friend is a method that looks correct for the problem and gives a wrong answer on some inputs. Here it is an ordinary depth-first search that records each vertex when it first visits it. That order is a valid route only when the search never gets stuck early, and the second run of this lesson shows the failure. A second false friend is the degree check alone. Balanced degrees do not prove that every link is reachable from the start, so the length test is also required.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Eulerian Cycle

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A directed graph is given on n vertices numbered from zero and has a list links of pairs [a, b], each a link from a to b. An Eulerian cycle is a route that starts and ends at vertex 0 and uses every link exactly once. At each vertex the method consumes the unused link that appears earliest in links. Return the cycle as an int[] of vertices. Return an empty array when no Eulerian cycle through vertex 0 exists. A graph with no links returns [0].

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 1000.
  • Links satisfy 0 <= links.length <= 5000.
  • Repeats do not occur, and no link joins a vertex to itself.
  • Mutation of links is not allowed.

Example 1. Input n = 5, links = [[0,1],[1,2],[2,0],[1,3],[3,1],[1,4],[4,1]], output [0,1,3,1,4,1,2,0].

Example 2. Input n = 4, links = [[0,1],[1,2],[2,0],[2,3]], output [].

Prerequisites. The code stage of this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. The method returns the reversed postorder and not the order of first visits, and it returns an empty array when the cycle cannot exist.

Hint

Which vertices must be balanced, and what does the length of the recorded output reveal about unreachable links?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method counts out - in for every vertex and returns an empty array unless all counts are zero. It then keeps an ArrayDeque of open vertices, starting with vertex 0. While the top vertex has an unused outgoing link, the method moves that vertex's cursor forward and pushes the target. When the top vertex has none, the method pops it and appends it to the output list. After the stack empties, the output holds E + 1 vertices exactly when every link was reachable from vertex 0, and the method reverses it.

The invariant is that the output, read backward, is a valid tail of the cycle made only of consumed links. A popped vertex has no unused outgoing link, so nothing can ever be inserted after it. The adjacency lists keep the order of links, so the earliest listed unused link is the one at the cursor. A graph with no links pops vertex 0 alone and returns [0]. The method never writes to links, because it copies the targets into its own lists.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(V + E), because each link is pushed once and popped once, and the degree pass reads each link once.
  • Space is O(V + E) for the adjacency lists, the cursors, the stack and the output.
import java.util.*;

public final class HierEulerCycle {
    /**
     * Returns an Eulerian cycle from vertex 0, or an empty array when none exists.
     * Time: O(V + E). Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: the output, read backward, is a valid tail of the cycle made of consumed links.
     */
    static int[] cycle(int n, int[][] links) {
        // Adjacency lists keep the order of links, so the earliest listed link comes first.
        List<List<Integer>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
        int[] balance = new int[n];
        // One pass over the links fills the lists and the degree differences.
        for (int[] e : links) {
            adj.get(e[0]).add(e[1]);
            balance[e[0]]++;
            balance[e[1]]--;
        }
        // A cycle needs every vertex balanced, so any other count ends the search.
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (balance[v] != 0) return new int[0];
        int[] cursor = new int[n];
        Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
        List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
        stack.push(0);
        // Each pass pushes one link or pops one vertex, so the loop runs at most 2E + 1 times.
        while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
            int v = stack.peek();
            if (cursor[v] < adj.get(v).size()) {
                // Advancing the cursor consumes the link, and the target joins the open walk.
                stack.push(adj.get(v).get(cursor[v]++));
            } else {
                // A vertex without unused links is final, so it joins the output.
                out.add(stack.pop());
            }
        }
        // Fewer than E + 1 recorded vertices means some links are unreachable from vertex 0.
        if (out.size() != links.length + 1) return new int[0];
        int[] res = new int[out.size()];
        // Reading the output from the end gives the travel order.
        for (int i = 0; i < res.length; i++) res[i] = out.get(res.length - 1 - i);
        return res;
    }

    /** Recursive reference with the same edge order; it is correct but uses one frame per link. */
    static void rec(int v, List<List<Integer>> adj, int[] cursor, List<Integer> out) {
        // The loop consumes links in listed order and recurses on each target.
        while (cursor[v] < adj.get(v).size()) rec(adj.get(v).get(cursor[v]++), adj, cursor, out);
        // The vertex is recorded after all of its links.
        out.add(v);
    }

    /** Oracle: the recursive version after the same degree test. */
    static int[] recursive(int n, int[][] links) {
        List<List<Integer>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
        int[] bal = new int[n];
        for (int[] e : links) { adj.get(e[0]).add(e[1]); bal[e[0]]++; bal[e[1]]--; }
        for (int b : bal) if (b != 0) return new int[0];
        List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
        rec(0, adj, new int[n], out);
        if (out.size() != links.length + 1) return new int[0];
        int[] res = new int[out.size()];
        for (int i = 0; i < res.length; i++) res[i] = out.get(res.length - 1 - i);
        return res;
    }

    /** Brute force: tries every order of the links and reports whether some order forms a cycle from 0. */
    static boolean exists(int[][] links, boolean[] used, int v, int count) {
        // All links used and back at vertex 0 means a cycle exists.
        if (count == links.length) return v == 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < links.length; i++) {
            if (!used[i] && links[i][0] == v) {
                used[i] = true;
                boolean ok = exists(links, used, links[i][1], count + 1);
                used[i] = false;
                if (ok) return true;
            }
        }
        return false;
    }

    /** Checks that a vertex array is a cycle from 0 that uses every link exactly once. */
    static boolean valid(int[][] links, int[] route) {
        if (route.length != links.length + 1 || route[0] != 0 || route[route.length - 1] != 0) return false;
        boolean[] used = new boolean[links.length];
        for (int k = 0; k + 1 < route.length; k++) {
            int found = -1;
            for (int i = 0; i < links.length; i++) {
                if (!used[i] && links[i][0] == route[k] && links[i][1] == route[k + 1]) { found = i; break; }
            }
            if (found < 0) return false;
            used[found] = true;
        }
        return true;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        int[][] g1 = {{0, 1}, {1, 2}, {2, 0}, {1, 3}, {3, 1}, {1, 4}, {4, 1}};
        if (!Arrays.equals(cycle(5, g1), new int[] {0, 1, 3, 1, 4, 1, 2, 0})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (cycle(4, new int[][] {{0, 1}, {1, 2}, {2, 0}, {2, 3}}).length != 0) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // A graph with no links returns only vertex 0.
        if (!Arrays.equals(cycle(3, new int[0][]), new int[] {0})) throw new AssertionError("empty");
        // Balanced degrees alone do not suffice: the second loop is unreachable from vertex 0.
        if (cycle(4, new int[][] {{0, 1}, {1, 0}, {2, 3}, {3, 2}}).length != 0) throw new AssertionError("split");
        // Java fact: a recursive walk needs one frame per link and overflows a small thread stack.
        int big = 200_000;
        int[][] ring = new int[big][];
        for (int i = 0; i < big; i++) ring[i] = new int[] {i, (i + 1) % big};
        boolean[] overflowed = {false};
        Thread t = new Thread(null, () -> {
            try { recursive(big, ring); } catch (StackOverflowError e) { overflowed[0] = true; }
        }, "small-stack", 256 * 1024);
        t.start();
        t.join();
        if (!overflowed[0]) throw new AssertionError("recursion should overflow");
        // The explicit stack handles the same ring.
        if (cycle(big, ring).length != big + 1) throw new AssertionError("ring");
        // Random graphs, half of them built from closed walks so that cycles are common.
        Random rnd = new Random(2501);
        for (int tr = 0; tr < 3000; tr++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(4);
            List<int[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
            if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                int v = 0, len = rnd.nextInt(7);
                for (int s = 0; s < len; s++) { int w = rnd.nextInt(n); list.add(new int[] {v, w}); v = w; }
                if (v != 0) list.add(new int[] {v, 0});
            } else {
                int m = rnd.nextInt(8);
                for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) list.add(new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n)});
            }
            // The exercise forbids repeats and self loops, so such inputs are skipped.
            boolean ok = true;
            Set<String> seen = new HashSet<>();
            for (int[] e : list) if (e[0] == e[1] || !seen.add(e[0] + "," + e[1])) ok = false;
            if (!ok) continue;
            int[][] links = list.toArray(new int[0][]);
            int[] got = cycle(n, links);
            boolean want = exists(links, new boolean[links.length], 0, 0);
            if ((got.length > 0) != want) throw new AssertionError("existence " + tr);
            if (want && !valid(links, got)) throw new AssertionError("valid " + tr);
            if (!Arrays.equals(got, recursive(n, links))) throw new AssertionError("recursive " + tr);
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Eulerian Path

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A directed graph has the vertex labels 0 to n - 1 and has a list links of pairs [a, b]. An Eulerian path is a route that uses every link exactly once and may end at a different vertex than it starts. The start vertex is the one whose out-degree is one more than its in-degree. When every vertex is balanced, the start is the smallest vertex that has an outgoing link. The method always takes the earliest listed unused link out of the current vertex. Return the path as an int[], or an empty array when none exists. A graph with no links returns [0].

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 1000.
  • Links satisfy 0 <= links.length <= 5000.
  • Repeats do not occur, and no link joins a vertex to itself.
  • Mutation of links is not allowed.

Example 1. Input n = 5, links = [[2,0],[0,1],[1,2],[2,3],[3,4]], output [2,0,1,2,3,4].

Example 2. Input n = 4, links = [[1,2],[2,3],[1,3]], output [].

Prerequisites. The exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method chooses the start from the degree counts and does not take it as input.

Hint

Which degree pattern names the start vertex, and what happens to the start when all vertices are balanced?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method first counts out - in for each vertex. Any count outside minus one to one rejects the graph, and so does a second vertex with count one. The start is the vertex with count one when it exists. Otherwise the start is the smallest vertex with an outgoing link, and a graph with no links returns [0]. The method stores the links in a head and next array pair, which are linked lists kept in plain arrays, and it fills them from the last link to the first so that the earliest link is read first. The walk then pops and pushes with an int[] stack.

The invariant is that the output, read backward, is a valid tail of the path made of consumed links. A vertex enters the output only after its links are gone. The start rule matters: when the graph is balanced, every vertex with a link lies on one cycle if the walk reaches all links, so the smallest such vertex is a safe start. The final length test rejects graphs whose links the walk cannot reach.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(V + E), because the degree pass, the array fill and the walk each touch every link a constant number of times.
  • Space is O(V + E) for the head, next and target arrays, the stack and the output.
import java.util.*;

public final class HierEulerPath {
    /**
     * Returns an Eulerian path whose start follows the degree rule, or an empty array.
     * Time: O(V + E). Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: the output, read backward, is a valid tail of the path made of consumed links.
     */
    static int[] path(int n, int[][] links) {
        int m = links.length;
        // A graph with no links is the single vertex 0 by the exercise rule.
        if (m == 0) return new int[] {0};
        int[] balance = new int[n];
        for (int[] e : links) { balance[e[0]]++; balance[e[1]]--; }
        int start = -1;
        int plus = 0;
        // One pass rejects bad degrees and finds the vertex with one extra outgoing link.
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
            if (balance[v] > 1 || balance[v] < -1) return new int[0];
            if (balance[v] == 1) { plus++; start = v; }
        }
        if (plus > 1) return new int[0];
        // With all vertices balanced, the smallest vertex that has an outgoing link starts the path.
        if (start == -1) {
            start = n;
            for (int[] e : links) start = Math.min(start, e[0]);
        }
        // head[v] is the first link of v, next[i] is the following link, filled from the back to keep the order.
        int[] head = new int[n];
        Arrays.fill(head, -1);
        int[] next = new int[m];
        for (int i = m - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
            next[i] = head[links[i][0]];
            head[links[i][0]] = i;
        }
        int[] stack = new int[m + 1];
        int[] out = new int[m + 1];
        int top = 0, size = 0;
        stack[top++] = start;
        // Each pass consumes one link or records one vertex.
        while (top > 0) {
            int v = stack[top - 1];
            if (head[v] != -1) {
                // Taking the link and moving the head forward consumes it.
                int i = head[v];
                head[v] = next[i];
                stack[top++] = links[i][1];
            } else {
                // A vertex with no link left is recorded and removed.
                out[size++] = v;
                top--;
            }
        }
        if (size != m + 1) return new int[0];
        int[] res = new int[size];
        // The reversed output is the path in travel order.
        for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) res[i] = out[size - 1 - i];
        return res;
    }

    /** Brute force: reports whether some order of the links forms a path from the given start. */
    static boolean exists(int[][] links, boolean[] used, int v, int count) {
        if (count == links.length) return true;
        for (int i = 0; i < links.length; i++) {
            if (!used[i] && links[i][0] == v) {
                used[i] = true;
                boolean ok = exists(links, used, links[i][1], count + 1);
                used[i] = false;
                if (ok) return true;
            }
        }
        return false;
    }

    /** Checks that the route starts at the given vertex and uses every link exactly once. */
    static boolean valid(int[][] links, int[] route, int start) {
        if (route.length != links.length + 1 || route[0] != start) return false;
        boolean[] used = new boolean[links.length];
        for (int k = 0; k + 1 < route.length; k++) {
            int found = -1;
            for (int i = 0; i < links.length; i++) {
                if (!used[i] && links[i][0] == route[k] && links[i][1] == route[k + 1]) { found = i; break; }
            }
            if (found < 0) return false;
            used[found] = true;
        }
        return true;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (!Arrays.equals(path(5, new int[][] {{2, 0}, {0, 1}, {1, 2}, {2, 3}, {3, 4}}), new int[] {2, 0, 1, 2, 3, 4})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (path(4, new int[][] {{1, 2}, {2, 3}, {1, 3}}).length != 0) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // A balanced graph starts at the smallest vertex with an outgoing link.
        if (!Arrays.equals(path(4, new int[][] {{3, 1}, {1, 3}}), new int[] {1, 3, 1})) throw new AssertionError("balanced");
        if (!Arrays.equals(path(3, new int[0][]), new int[] {0})) throw new AssertionError("empty");
        // Random graphs, half of them cut from a real walk so that a path usually exists.
        Random rnd = new Random(2502);
        for (int tr = 0; tr < 3000; tr++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            List<int[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
            if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(n), len = rnd.nextInt(7);
                for (int s = 0; s < len; s++) { int w = rnd.nextInt(n); list.add(new int[] {v, w}); v = w; }
                Collections.shuffle(list, rnd);
            } else {
                int m = rnd.nextInt(8);
                for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) list.add(new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n)});
            }
            // The exercise forbids repeats and self loops, so such inputs are skipped.
            boolean ok = true;
            Set<String> seen = new HashSet<>();
            for (int[] e : list) if (e[0] == e[1] || !seen.add(e[0] + "," + e[1])) ok = false;
            if (!ok || list.isEmpty()) continue;
            int[][] links = list.toArray(new int[0][]);
            int[] got = path(n, links);
            // The oracle tries every vertex as a start, because the degree rule names only one of them.
            boolean want = false;
            for (int s = 0; s < n && !want; s++) want = exists(links, new boolean[links.length], s, 0);
            if ((got.length > 0) != want) throw new AssertionError("existence " + tr);
            if (want) {
                int[] bal = new int[n];
                for (int[] e : links) { bal[e[0]]++; bal[e[1]]--; }
                int start = -1;
                for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (bal[v] == 1) start = v;
                if (start == -1) { start = n; for (int[] e : links) start = Math.min(start, e[0]); }
                if (!valid(links, got, start)) throw new AssertionError("valid " + tr);
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Parallel Edges

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A directed multigraph uses vertex labels 0 through n - 1. The matrix count has size n by n, and count[u][v] is the number of links from u to v. Self loops are allowed, so count[u][u] may be positive. Find an Eulerian cycle that starts and ends at vertex 0 and uses every link exactly once. At each vertex the method takes the link to the smallest target that still has an unused link. Return the cycle as an int[], or an empty array when none exists. A graph with no links returns [0].

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 100.
  • Counts satisfy 0 <= count[u][v] <= 50.
  • Total number of links is at most 10^6.
  • Mutation of count is not allowed.

Example 1. Input count = [[1,2],[2,0]], output [0,0,1,0,1,0].

Example 2. Input count = [[1,0,0],[0,0,1],[0,1,0]], output [].

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method stores how many links remain between two vertices, and a yes or no entry for each pair would lose the repeats.

Hint

What does the method need to store for a pair of vertices when one link can appear several times?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method copies count into a working matrix left, because the input must stay unchanged. It checks that each row sum equals the matching column sum. Then it runs the stack walk. For the top vertex v, a pointer col[v] moves right past entries of left[v] that are zero. When the pointer stays inside the row, the method subtracts one from left[v][col[v]] and pushes that column. The pointer does not advance, because the same target may still hold more parallel links. When the pointer leaves the row, the method pops v and records it.

The invariant is that left[u][v] counts the links from u to v that are still unused, and the output, read backward, is a valid tail of the cycle. A yes or no entry for each pair would treat two parallel links as one, and the cycle would stop short. A self loop is a link from a vertex to itself, so the walk pushes the same vertex again and the row sum equals the column sum at that vertex. Taking the smallest column first makes the cycle the smallest in lexical order, which the oracle confirms.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n^2 + E), because copying and summing the matrix costs O(n^2), the pointers move at most n steps per row, and every link is pushed once.
  • Space is O(n^2 + E) for the working matrix, the stack and the output.
import java.util.*;

public final class HierParallelEdges {
    /**
     * Returns the lexically smallest Eulerian cycle from vertex 0, or an empty array.
     * Time: O(n^2 + E). Space: O(n^2 + E).
     * Invariant: left[u][v] counts unused links, and the output, read backward, is a valid cycle tail.
     */
    static int[] cycle(int[][] count) {
        int n = count.length;
        // The working copy keeps the input unchanged.
        int[][] left = new int[n][];
        long total = 0;
        for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) left[u] = count[u].clone();
        // Row sums are out-degrees and column sums are in-degrees; they must match for every vertex.
        for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) {
            long out = 0, in = 0;
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) { out += count[u][v]; in += count[v][u]; }
            if (out != in) return new int[0];
            total += out;
        }
        int[] col = new int[n];
        int[] stack = new int[(int) total + 1];
        int[] rec = new int[(int) total + 1];
        int top = 0, size = 0;
        stack[top++] = 0;
        // Each pass pushes one link or records one vertex.
        while (top > 0) {
            int v = stack[top - 1];
            // Skip targets whose links are all used; the pointer only moves right.
            while (col[v] < n && left[v][col[v]] == 0) col[v]++;
            if (col[v] < n) {
                // One parallel link is consumed, and the pointer stays for the remaining copies.
                left[v][col[v]]--;
                stack[top++] = col[v];
            } else {
                // The row is empty, so the vertex is final.
                rec[size++] = v;
                top--;
            }
        }
        // A short record means some links are unreachable from vertex 0.
        if (size != total + 1) return new int[0];
        int[] res = new int[size];
        for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) res[i] = rec[size - 1 - i];
        return res;
    }

    /** Brute force: the first complete route found with the smallest target tried first is the smallest cycle. */
    static int[] brute(int[][] left, int v, int remaining, List<Integer> path) {
        // All links are used; the route must end at vertex 0.
        if (remaining == 0) return v == 0 ? path.stream().mapToInt(Integer::intValue).toArray() : null;
        for (int w = 0; w < left.length; w++) {
            if (left[v][w] > 0) {
                left[v][w]--;
                path.add(w);
                int[] r = brute(left, w, remaining - 1, path);
                path.remove(path.size() - 1);
                left[v][w]++;
                if (r != null) return r;
            }
        }
        return null;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (!Arrays.equals(cycle(new int[][] {{1, 2}, {2, 0}}), new int[] {0, 0, 1, 0, 1, 0})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (cycle(new int[][] {{1, 0, 0}, {0, 0, 1}, {0, 1, 0}}).length != 0) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // The input matrix is not changed.
        int[][] keep = {{1, 2}, {2, 0}};
        cycle(keep);
        if (!Arrays.deepEquals(keep, new int[][] {{1, 2}, {2, 0}})) throw new AssertionError("mutated");
        // A graph with no links returns only vertex 0.
        if (!Arrays.equals(cycle(new int[][] {{0, 0}, {0, 0}}), new int[] {0})) throw new AssertionError("empty");
        // Random matrices built from closed walks plus noise, so both outcomes occur.
        Random rnd = new Random(2503);
        for (int tr = 0; tr < 3000; tr++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(4);
            int[][] c = new int[n][n];
            int walks = rnd.nextInt(3);
            for (int w = 0; w < walks; w++) {
                int v = 0, len = rnd.nextInt(4);
                for (int s = 0; s < len; s++) { int x = rnd.nextInt(n); c[v][x]++; v = x; }
                c[v][0]++;
            }
            if (rnd.nextInt(4) == 0) c[rnd.nextInt(n)][rnd.nextInt(n)]++;
            int total = 0;
            for (int[] row : c) for (int x : row) total += x;
            if (total > 9) continue;
            int[][] copy = new int[n][];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) copy[i] = c[i].clone();
            int[] got = cycle(c);
            int[] want = total == 0 ? new int[] {0} : brute(copy, 0, total, new ArrayList<>(List.of(0)));
            if (want == null) want = new int[0];
            if (!Arrays.equals(got, want)) throw new AssertionError("random " + tr + " " + Arrays.toString(got) + " " + Arrays.toString(want));
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Reconstruct Itinerary

LeetCode 332

Problem Specification. A list tickets holds pairs [from, to] of airport codes, and each pair is one flight. The trip starts at JFK and uses every ticket exactly once. Among all valid itineraries, return the one that is lexically largest when compared airport by airport. Return an empty list when no valid itinerary exists. Equal tickets may appear several times.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Tickets satisfy 1 <= tickets.length <= 300.
  • Codes are strings of three uppercase letters.
  • Repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Mutation of tickets is not allowed.

Example 1. Input tickets = [["JFK","SFO"],["JFK","ATL"],["SFO","ATL"],["ATL","JFK"],["ATL","SFO"]], output ["JFK","SFO","ATL","JFK","ATL","SFO"].

Example 2. Input tickets = [["JFK","ATL"],["JFK","SFO"]], output [].

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method prefers the largest destination and not the smallest, and it must report a missing itinerary because the input does not guarantee one.

Hint

Which order of the outgoing tickets gives the largest result, and how does the method notice that no itinerary exists?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method maps each airport to a PriorityQueue that orders its destinations from largest to smallest. Equal tickets enter the queue as separate entries, so repeats are consumed one by one. It counts out - in for each airport, and it returns an empty list when a count leaves the range minus one to one or when an airport with count one is not JFK. It then runs the stack walk from JFK, polling the largest destination of the top airport, and it records an airport when its queue is empty. A recorded list shorter than tickets.length + 1 means some tickets are unreachable, so the method returns an empty list.

The invariant is that the recorded list, read backward, is a valid tail of the itinerary. Taking the largest destination first gives the largest itinerary for the same reason that the smallest first gives the smallest: the first dead end is the airport where the trip must end, and it is recorded first. The degree rule matters because a graph with a wrong start can still produce E + 1 recorded airports that do not form a trip. The method reads tickets and never modifies it.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(E log E), because each ticket enters and leaves a priority queue once.
  • Space is O(V + E) for the queues, the stack and the recorded list.
import java.util.*;

public final class HierItinerary {
    /**
     * Returns the lexically largest itinerary from JFK that uses every ticket, or an empty list.
     * Time: O(E log E). Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: the recorded list, read backward, is a valid tail of the itinerary.
     */
    static List<String> largest(String[][] tickets) {
        Map<String, PriorityQueue<String>> dest = new HashMap<>();
        Map<String, Integer> balance = new HashMap<>();
        // Every ticket joins the queue of its origin and shifts the degree difference.
        for (String[] t : tickets) {
            dest.computeIfAbsent(t[0], k -> new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder())).add(t[1]);
            balance.merge(t[0], 1, Integer::sum);
            balance.merge(t[1], -1, Integer::sum);
        }
        // Only JFK may have one extra departure, and no airport may differ by more than one.
        for (Map.Entry<String, Integer> e : balance.entrySet()) {
            if (Math.abs(e.getValue()) > 1) return new ArrayList<>();
            if (e.getValue() == 1 && !e.getKey().equals("JFK")) return new ArrayList<>();
        }
        Deque<String> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
        List<String> out = new ArrayList<>();
        stack.push("JFK");
        // Each pass takes one ticket or records one airport.
        while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
            PriorityQueue<String> q = dest.get(stack.peek());
            if (q != null && !q.isEmpty()) {
                // Polling consumes the largest remaining destination.
                stack.push(q.poll());
            } else {
                // An airport without tickets is final and joins the record.
                out.add(stack.pop());
            }
        }
        // A short record means some tickets are unreachable from JFK.
        if (out.size() != tickets.length + 1) return new ArrayList<>();
        Collections.reverse(out);
        return out;
    }

    /** Brute force: tries the largest destination first, so the first complete itinerary is the largest. */
    static List<String> brute(String[][] tickets, boolean[] used, String at, List<String> path) {
        if (path.size() == tickets.length + 1) return new ArrayList<>(path);
        String[] order = new String[tickets.length];
        int k = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < tickets.length; i++) if (!used[i] && tickets[i][0].equals(at)) order[k++] = tickets[i][1];
        Arrays.sort(order, 0, k, Comparator.reverseOrder());
        for (int j = 0; j < k; j++) {
            if (j > 0 && order[j].equals(order[j - 1])) continue;
            int pick = -1;
            for (int i = 0; i < tickets.length; i++) {
                if (!used[i] && tickets[i][0].equals(at) && tickets[i][1].equals(order[j])) { pick = i; break; }
            }
            used[pick] = true;
            path.add(order[j]);
            List<String> r = brute(tickets, used, order[j], path);
            path.remove(path.size() - 1);
            used[pick] = false;
            if (r != null) return r;
        }
        return null;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        String[][] a = {{"JFK", "SFO"}, {"JFK", "ATL"}, {"SFO", "ATL"}, {"ATL", "JFK"}, {"ATL", "SFO"}};
        if (!largest(a).equals(List.of("JFK", "SFO", "ATL", "JFK", "ATL", "SFO"))) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!largest(new String[][] {{"JFK", "ATL"}, {"JFK", "SFO"}}).isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // The input is not changed.
        if (!a[0][1].equals("SFO") || a.length != 5) throw new AssertionError("mutated");
        // Equal tickets are consumed one at a time.
        if (!largest(new String[][] {{"JFK", "LAX"}, {"LAX", "JFK"}, {"JFK", "LAX"}, {"LAX", "JFK"}}).equals(List.of("JFK", "LAX", "JFK", "LAX", "JFK"))) throw new AssertionError("repeats");
        // Random tickets over a small airport set, half built from a real trip.
        String[] names = {"JFK", "ATL", "SFO", "LAX"};
        Random rnd = new Random(2504);
        for (int tr = 0; tr < 3000; tr++) {
            List<String[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
            if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                String v = "JFK";
                int len = 1 + rnd.nextInt(7);
                for (int s = 0; s < len; s++) { String w = names[rnd.nextInt(names.length)]; list.add(new String[] {v, w}); v = w; }
                Collections.shuffle(list, rnd);
            } else {
                int m = 1 + rnd.nextInt(7);
                for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) list.add(new String[] {names[rnd.nextInt(names.length)], names[rnd.nextInt(names.length)]});
            }
            String[][] tickets = list.toArray(new String[0][]);
            List<String> got = largest(tickets);
            List<String> want = brute(tickets, new boolean[tickets.length], "JFK", new ArrayList<>(List.of("JFK")));
            if (want == null) want = new ArrayList<>();
            if (!got.equals(want)) throw new AssertionError("random " + tr + " " + got + " " + want);
        }
    }
}verified

Find Strongly Connected Components

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Modules That Import Each Other

A language runtime loads source modules and runs each module's start-up code once. Module A imports B, B imports C, and C imports A. The runtime cannot run A before B, B before C, or C before A, so any fixed order fails for these three. It must treat them as one unit and initialize them together. Module D imports A but nothing imports D back, so D can wait until that unit is ready.

Model the modules as vertices of a directed graph and each import as a directed edge. Vertex v reaches vertex u when a path of edges leads from v to u. A strongly connected component is a largest set of vertices in which every vertex reaches every other vertex. The three modules A, B and C form one such set, and D forms a set of its own.

The task is to label every vertex with its component, so that two vertices share a label exactly when each reaches the other. This lesson asks how to find all components in linear time, without testing every pair of vertices.

Simple approach

Testing Every Pair Of Vertices

The direct definition suggests a direct method. For each vertex, run a depth-first search and record which vertices it reaches in a table reach. Two vertices belong together when reach[u][v] and reach[v][u] are both true. The method then gives each unlabeled vertex a new label and copies that label to every vertex that is mutually reachable with it.

static int[] byPairs(int n, int[][] edges) {
    List<List<Integer>> out = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) out.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int[] e : edges) out.get(e[0]).add(e[1]);
    boolean[][] reach = new boolean[n][n];
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
        stack.push(s);
        reach[s][s] = true;
        while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
            int u = stack.pop();
            for (int v : out.get(u)) {
                if (!reach[s][v]) {
                    reach[s][v] = true;
                    stack.push(v);
                }
            }
        }
    }
    int[] label = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(label, -1);
    int next = 0;
    for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) {
        if (label[u] != -1) continue;
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
            if (reach[u][v] && reach[v][u]) label[v] = next;
        }
        next++;
    }
    return label;
}

The answer is correct, because mutual reachability is exactly the definition. The question is the cost on a large module graph.

Predict first: A project has 100000 modules and 300000 imports. Roughly how many edge visits does byPairs perform, and how much memory does the table reach need?

It performs about 100000 searches that each visit up to 300000 edges, which is on the order of 3 * 10^10 visits. The table has 10^10 boolean cells, so it needs about 10 GB. Both numbers are far beyond a practical limit, although the answer is correct. The cost comes from repeating a full search for every start vertex.

Bottleneck

One Search Per Vertex Is Too Slow

The method runs one search from each of the V vertices, and each search may scan all E edges. The time is O(V * (V + E)). The table reach has V * V cells, so the memory is O(V^2). For a graph with 100000 vertices, the table alone has 10^10 cells, which is about 10 GB at one byte per cell.

Most of this work repeats facts. If vertex a reaches vertex b, then everything that b reaches is also reachable from a, and the method rediscovers this from scratch for every start. The labeling step adds another O(V^2) pair checks.

A better method must visit each vertex and each edge a constant number of times, so the time is O(V + E). It must also store O(V) values, one label per vertex, and no table of pairs. That requires a fixed order from two linear sweeps that exposes the components, instead of a separate search per vertex.

Key idea

Two Sweeps With A Reversed Graph

The method sweeps the graph twice. The first sweep records an order, and the second sweep uses that order to cut the graph into components.

Recording The Finish Order

A depth-first search finishes a vertex when it has examined every outgoing edge of that vertex and returns from it. The finish order lists the vertices in the sequence in which the first sweep finishes them. The first sweep starts a search from each vertex that is still unvisited, so every vertex receives a place in the list.

Collapsing Components Into One Graph

Merge each component into a single vertex. Keep one edge from component C to component D when some edge leads from a vertex of C to a vertex of D. The result is the condensed graph. It has no cycle. A cycle through two components would make every vertex of one reach every vertex of the other and back, and the two components would be one component.

A condensed graph without a cycle has at least one source, a component with no incoming edge.

Why The Latest Finisher Is Safe

The key fact is this. If the condensed graph has an edge from component C to component D, then some vertex of C finishes after every vertex of D. When the search enters C first, it can reach D, and all of D finishes before the search returns from the entry vertex of C. When the search enters D first, it cannot reach C, so all of D finishes before the search ever touches C.

It follows that the vertex with the latest finish lies in a source component of the condensed graph.

Searching The Reversed Graph

The reversed graph has the same vertices, and every edge from a to b appears as an edge from b to a. Reversal keeps the components unchanged, because a path from u to v becomes a path from v to u. It changes the direction of every condensed edge, so a source component now has no outgoing edge.

The second sweep takes vertices in decreasing finish order and searches the reversed graph from each vertex that has no label. The first start lies in a source component, and the search cannot leave it. It labels exactly that component. After removing it, the next unlabeled vertex with the latest finish lies in a source of what remains, and the argument repeats. Every search therefore labels one full component and nothing more.

State

Order, Reversed Lists And Labels

The method reads the vertex count n and the array edges of pairs [from, to]. The graph is directed, and the vertices carry the numbers 0 to n - 1. Repeated edges and self loops may occur. The method does not change edges. It keeps these structures.

  • out is the list of outgoing neighbours of each vertex, in the order of edges.
  • back is the list of incoming neighbours of each vertex, which forms the reversed graph.
  • order is an int[] of length n that holds the vertices in finish order.
  • cursor is an int[] where cursor[u] is the position of the next edge of u to examine.
  • seen is a boolean[] of length n that marks vertices the first sweep has pushed.
  • placed is the number of vertices written to order so far.
  • label is an int[] of length n where -1 marks a vertex that no search has reached.

The labels count up from 0 in the order in which the second sweep opens components. In the second sweep, the method assigns a label when it pushes a vertex, so a vertex never enters a stack twice. The first sweep uses seen for the same purpose. The first sweep uses an explicit stack, because a recursive search over a long chain of modules can exhaust the Java call stack.

Walkthrough

Two Runs On Module Graphs

Eight Modules In Four Groups

The first graph has eight modules and eleven imports. The imports run 0 to 3, 3 to 6, 6 to 0, 6 to 1, 1 to 4 and 4 to 1. They continue with 4 to 2, 2 to 5, 5 to 2, 5 to 7 and 3 to 7. The first sweep produces the finish order 7, 5, 2, 4, 1, 6, 3, 0. The second sweep reads that order from the right, so each cell below is one vertex in the order 0, 3, 6, 1, 4, 2, 5, 7. The pointer i marks the vertex under examination. The variable label is the label of that vertex after the step. The variable found is the number of vertices that a new search collects. The variable labeled is the total number of labeled vertices afterward. At step 0, vertex 0 opens label 0 and the reversed edges lead to 6 and then to 3, so found is 3. Vertices 3 and 6 need no search, because they already have a label. The sweep ends with four labels, and the groups are 0 3 6, 1 4, 2 5 and 7.

The first sweep derives its order from a single search that starts at vertex 0. That search walks 0, 3, 6, 1, 4, 2, 5 and then 7. Vertex 7 has no outgoing import, so it finishes first. The search then backs up and finishes 5, 2, 4, 1, 6, 3 and 0, because every other edge of those vertices leads to a vertex that is already visited.

Searching The Wrong Direction

The second graph has five modules and six imports: 0 to 1, 1 to 0, 1 to 2, 2 to 3, 3 to 2 and 3 to 4. Its true groups are 0 1, 2 3 and 4. The finish order is 4, 3, 2, 1, 0. This run is a variant that keeps the decreasing finish order but searches the original edges in the second sweep. The variable truth is the size of the true group of the vertex under examination. The search from vertex 0 follows the one-way edges onward and collects all five modules, so found is 5 where truth is 2. The later vertices are already labeled. The variant ends with one label instead of three, and the reversed graph is what prevents this.

Code

Kosaraju With Two Sweeps

The method returns an int[] of labels, where equal labels mean the same component. It uses explicit stacks in both sweeps.

static int[] strongComponents(int n, int[][] edges) {
    List<List<Integer>> out = new ArrayList<>();
    List<List<Integer>> back = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
        out.add(new ArrayList<>());
        back.add(new ArrayList<>());
    }
    for (int[] e : edges) {
        out.get(e[0]).add(e[1]);
        back.get(e[1]).add(e[0]);
    }
    boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
    int[] cursor = new int[n];
    int[] order = new int[n];
    int placed = 0;
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
        if (seen[s]) continue;
        Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
        stack.push(s);
        seen[s] = true;
        while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
            int u = stack.peek();
            if (cursor[u] < out.get(u).size()) {
                int v = out.get(u).get(cursor[u]++);
                if (!seen[v]) {
                    seen[v] = true;
                    stack.push(v);
                }
            } else {
                stack.pop();
                order[placed++] = u;
            }
        }
    }
    int[] label = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(label, -1);
    int next = 0;
    for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
        if (label[order[i]] != -1) continue;
        Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
        stack.push(order[i]);
        label[order[i]] = next;
        while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
            int u = stack.pop();
            for (int v : back.get(u)) {
                if (label[v] == -1) {
                    label[v] = next;
                    stack.push(v);
                }
            }
        }
        next++;
    }
    return label;
}

The first sweep appends a vertex to order only when its cursor has passed the last edge. The second loop walks order from the last index to the first, which gives decreasing finish order. Each sweep pushes each vertex once and moves each edge once, so the time is O(V + E). The two adjacency lists, the order, the cursors and the labels take O(V + E) memory in total.

When to use it

Recognizing Mutual Reachability Problems

Reading The Cue

Use this method when a statement on a directed graph asks which vertices can reach each other in both directions. Typical forms are grouping mutually dependent modules, finding sets of web pages that link back to one another, and counting groups of accounts that can all transfer to each other. The answer is usually a label array, a component count, the size of the largest group, or a statement about how the groups connect.

Checking The Invariant

An invariant is a statement that holds before and after every step of a loop, and it is what proves the method correct. Here the invariant is that every search of the second sweep starts in a component with no unlabeled component pointing into it, so the search labels exactly one whole component. Two details of the code protect it. The second sweep must walk order from the latest finisher backward, and it must search the reversed lists. Test a vertex with a self loop, which stays alone. Test a vertex that has no edges at all, which forms a component of one.

Avoiding The False Friend

A false friend is a well-known technique that seems to fit the problem and gives wrong answers on some inputs. The false friend here is connectivity of an undirected graph, such as a union-find structure or a plain search that ignores direction. It merges two modules after a single one-way import, although the second module cannot reach the first. The second trace above shows the same failure from another side, where the order is right but the direction of the search is wrong. A related trap is to call two vertices connected after one search succeeds. Reaching v from u proves one direction only.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Finish Order

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A directed graph consists of the vertex set 0, ..., n - 1, and edges lists pairs [a, b] for an edge from a to b. Run a depth-first search that starts from vertices 0, 1, ..., n - 1 in ascending order and skips visited vertices. At each vertex the search follows outgoing edges in the order in which they appear in edges. Return an int[] of length n that lists the vertices in the order in which the search finishes them.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 2000.
  • Edges satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 6000, and repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Mutation is not allowed, and edges stays unchanged.

Example 1. Input n = 4, edges = [[0,1],[1,2],[0,2],[2,3]], output [3,2,1,0].

Example 2. Input n = 5, edges = [[1,0],[0,1],[3,4],[2,3]], output [1,0,4,3,2].

Prerequisites. The first sweep of this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. The method records a vertex after its last outgoing edge completes and not when it first reaches the vertex.

Hint

When does the search append a vertex, and does a cycle back to a vertex on the stack append it early?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method builds an adjacency list that keeps each neighbour list in input order. It then starts a depth-first search from every unvisited vertex, taking the starts in ascending order. The search keeps an explicit stack and one cursor per vertex, where the cursor holds the position of the next outgoing edge to examine. A vertex stays on the stack while its cursor still points at an edge. When the cursor runs past the last edge, the method pops the vertex and appends it to the result, because its outgoing work is complete.

The invariant is that the method appends a vertex only after it appends every vertex reachable from it through unvisited vertices. A vertex that is already on the stack is never pushed again, so a cycle cannot cause a second visit. The explicit stack replaces recursion, so a long chain of vertices cannot exhaust the Java call stack.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(V + E), because the method pushes and pops each vertex once and examines each edge once through its cursor.
  • Space is O(V + E) for the adjacency list, plus O(V) for the stack, the cursors and the result.
import java.util.*;

public final class SccFinishOrder {
    /**
     * Returns the vertices in the order in which their outgoing search completes.
     * Time: O(V + E). Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: a vertex is appended only after all vertices reachable through unvisited ones are appended.
     */
    static int[] finishOrder(int n, int[][] edges) {
        // The adjacency list keeps neighbours in input order, so the output is deterministic.
        List<List<Integer>> out = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) out.add(new ArrayList<>());
        for (int[] e : edges) out.get(e[0]).add(e[1]);
        boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
        // cursor[u] is the position of the next outgoing edge of u to examine.
        int[] cursor = new int[n];
        int[] stack = new int[n];
        int[] order = new int[n];
        int count = 0;
        // Starts are taken in ascending order, so every vertex gets visited.
        for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
            if (seen[s]) continue;
            int top = 0;
            stack[top++] = s;
            seen[s] = true;
            // Each iteration either advances one cursor or pops one vertex, so the loop runs V + E times in total.
            while (top > 0) {
                int u = stack[top - 1];
                if (cursor[u] < out.get(u).size()) {
                    // Take the next outgoing edge and advance the cursor so the edge is never examined twice.
                    int v = out.get(u).get(cursor[u]++);
                    // Push only unvisited vertices, which bounds the stack at n entries.
                    if (!seen[v]) {
                        seen[v] = true;
                        stack[top++] = v;
                    }
                } else {
                    // All outgoing edges are done, so this vertex finishes now.
                    top--;
                    order[count++] = u;
                }
            }
        }
        return order;
    }

    /** Oracle: the textbook recursive search on small inputs. */
    static int[] oracle(int n, int[][] edges) {
        List<List<Integer>> out = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) out.add(new ArrayList<>());
        for (int[] e : edges) out.get(e[0]).add(e[1]);
        boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
        List<Integer> order = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) visit(s, out, seen, order);
        int[] res = new int[n];
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) res[i] = order.get(i);
        return res;
    }

    static void visit(int u, List<List<Integer>> out, boolean[] seen, List<Integer> order) {
        if (seen[u]) return;
        seen[u] = true;
        for (int v : out.get(u)) visit(v, out, seen, order);
        order.add(u);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (!Arrays.equals(finishOrder(4, new int[][] {{0, 1}, {1, 2}, {0, 2}, {2, 3}}), new int[] {3, 2, 1, 0})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!Arrays.equals(finishOrder(5, new int[][] {{1, 0}, {0, 1}, {3, 4}, {2, 3}}), new int[] {1, 0, 4, 3, 2})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // An empty edge list finishes the vertices in ascending order.
        if (!Arrays.equals(finishOrder(3, new int[0][]), new int[] {0, 1, 2})) throw new AssertionError("empty");
        // A self loop does not push its vertex twice.
        if (!Arrays.equals(finishOrder(2, new int[][] {{0, 0}, {0, 1}}), new int[] {1, 0})) throw new AssertionError("self");
        // A chain far deeper than the default call stack still works because the stack is explicit.
        int deep = 100_000;
        int[][] chain = new int[deep - 1][];
        for (int i = 0; i + 1 < deep; i++) chain[i] = new int[] {i, i + 1};
        int[] deepOrder = finishOrder(deep, chain);
        if (deepOrder[0] != deep - 1 || deepOrder[deep - 1] != 0) throw new AssertionError("deep");
        // The input array is not modified.
        int[][] keep = {{2, 1}, {1, 0}};
        finishOrder(3, keep);
        if (keep[0][0] != 2 || keep[0][1] != 1 || keep[1][0] != 1 || keep[1][1] != 0) throw new AssertionError("mutated");
        // Random graphs against the recursive oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(2506);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            int m = rnd.nextInt(14);
            int[][] edges = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) edges[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n)};
            if (!Arrays.equals(finishOrder(n, edges), oracle(n, edges))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Reverse-Graph Components

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Take a directed graph whose vertex labels run 0 through n - 1, with edges [a, b]. Compute the finish order of the original graph as in the exercise above. Then take vertices in decreasing finish order and search the reversed graph from each vertex that has no label yet. Return an int[] label where label[v] names the group of v. The groups carry the numbers 0, 1, 2, ... in the order in which these searches begin.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 2000.
  • Edges satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 6000, and repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Labels are consecutive integers from 0.
  • Mutation is not allowed, and edges stays unchanged.

Example 1. Input n = 6, edges = [[0,1],[1,2],[2,0],[2,3],[3,4],[4,3],[5,4]], output [1,1,1,2,2,0].

Example 2. Input n = 4, edges = [[3,2],[2,3],[1,0],[0,1],[2,1]], output [1,1,0,0].

Prerequisites. The exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method returns a label for every vertex, and the numbering depends on the order of the second sweep.

Hint

Which vertex starts the first search, and why does that search stay inside its own component?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method first computes the finish order of the original graph with the explicit-stack search from the previous solution. It then builds a second adjacency list in which every edge from a to b also appears from b to a. The second pass walks the finish order from the last entry to the first. A vertex that has no label yet receives the next label, and a search over the reversed lists gives the same label to every unlabeled vertex it reaches.

The invariant is that every search of the second pass starts in a component that has no unlabeled component pointing into it, so the search labels exactly that component. The reversed lists point from each component back toward its callers, and all callers are already labeled. A search over the original lists would leave the component and label vertices of later components.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(V + E), because both passes touch each vertex and each edge a constant number of times.
  • Space is O(V + E) for the two adjacency lists, plus O(V) for the order, the stack and the labels.
import java.util.*;

public final class SccReverseComponents {
    /**
     * Labels every vertex with its strongly connected component, numbering components in discovery order.
     * Time: O(V + E). Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: a search of the second pass stays inside one component because all edges into it come from labeled vertices.
     */
    static int[] labels(int n, int[][] edges) {
        List<List<Integer>> out = new ArrayList<>();
        List<List<Integer>> back = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
            out.add(new ArrayList<>());
            back.add(new ArrayList<>());
        }
        // Each edge a to b is stored forward for pass one and reversed for pass two.
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            out.get(e[0]).add(e[1]);
            back.get(e[1]).add(e[0]);
        }
        int[] order = finish(n, out);
        int[] label = new int[n];
        // The value minus one marks a vertex that no search has reached yet.
        Arrays.fill(label, -1);
        int next = 0;
        int[] stack = new int[n];
        // Walk the finish order from the latest finisher to the earliest.
        for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
            int s = order[i];
            // A labeled vertex belongs to a component that an earlier search has already collected.
            if (label[s] != -1) continue;
            int top = 0;
            stack[top++] = s;
            label[s] = next;
            // Each vertex is pushed once, so the second pass costs O(V + E) in total.
            while (top > 0) {
                int u = stack[--top];
                for (int v : back.get(u)) {
                    // Label on push so that no vertex enters the stack twice.
                    if (label[v] == -1) {
                        label[v] = next;
                        stack[top++] = v;
                    }
                }
            }
            // The next unlabeled start opens a new component.
            next++;
        }
        return label;
    }

    /** Finish order by an explicit-stack search over the given adjacency lists. */
    static int[] finish(int n, List<List<Integer>> out) {
        boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
        int[] cursor = new int[n];
        int[] stack = new int[n];
        int[] order = new int[n];
        int count = 0;
        for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
            if (seen[s]) continue;
            int top = 0;
            stack[top++] = s;
            seen[s] = true;
            while (top > 0) {
                int u = stack[top - 1];
                if (cursor[u] < out.get(u).size()) {
                    int v = out.get(u).get(cursor[u]++);
                    if (!seen[v]) {
                        seen[v] = true;
                        stack[top++] = v;
                    }
                } else {
                    top--;
                    order[count++] = u;
                }
            }
        }
        return order;
    }

    /** Oracle: u and v share a component exactly when each reaches the other in the transitive closure. */
    static boolean[][] closure(int n, int[][] edges) {
        boolean[][] r = new boolean[n][n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) r[v][v] = true;
        for (int[] e : edges) r[e[0]][e[1]] = true;
        for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
                for (int j = 0; j < n; j++)
                    if (r[i][k] && r[k][j]) r[i][j] = true;
        return r;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (!Arrays.equals(labels(6, new int[][] {{0, 1}, {1, 2}, {2, 0}, {2, 3}, {3, 4}, {4, 3}, {5, 4}}), new int[] {1, 1, 1, 2, 2, 0})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!Arrays.equals(labels(4, new int[][] {{3, 2}, {2, 3}, {1, 0}, {0, 1}, {2, 1}}), new int[] {1, 1, 0, 0})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // Searching the original lists in pass two merges components, which shows why the lists are reversed.
        int[][] hazard = {{0, 1}, {1, 0}, {1, 2}, {2, 3}, {3, 2}, {3, 4}};
        int[] right = labels(5, hazard);
        if (right[0] == right[2] || right[2] == right[4]) throw new AssertionError("hazard setup");
        // Random graphs: same partition as the closure, and labels are consecutive from zero.
        Random rnd = new Random(2507);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            int m = rnd.nextInt(16);
            int[][] edges = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) edges[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n)};
            int[] got = labels(n, edges);
            boolean[][] r = closure(n, edges);
            int max = -1;
            for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) {
                max = Math.max(max, got[u]);
                for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
                    boolean same = r[u][v] && r[v][u];
                    if (same != (got[u] == got[v])) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
                }
            }
            // Labels 0..max are all used, so they are consecutive.
            boolean[] used = new boolean[max + 1];
            for (int x : got) used[x] = true;
            for (boolean b : used) if (!b) throw new AssertionError("gap " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Singleton And One-Way Edge

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Consider a directed graph with vertices 0 to n - 1 and edges [a, b]. A component is a singleton when it contains exactly one vertex. Return the number of singleton components. A vertex with only a self loop, only a one-way edge, or no edge counts as a singleton.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 2000.
  • Edges satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 6000, and repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Result lies in 0..n.
  • Mutation is not allowed, and edges stays unchanged.

Example 1. Input n = 3, edges = [[0,1],[1,2]], output 3.

Example 2. Input n = 4, edges = [[0,0],[1,2],[2,1],[2,3]], output 2.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method counts component sizes and keeps vertices apart when only one direction of reachability exists.

Hint

Does an edge from a to b without a way back from b to a put the two in one component, and what does a self loop add?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method runs the two passes and counts how many vertices each label receives. Components of size one are then counted. A one-way edge from a to b lets a reach b, but b cannot return, so the two vertices end in separate components. A self loop on a gives a a path to itself, which every vertex already has, so it does not join a to anyone else.

The invariant is that two vertices share a label only when the second pass reaches one from the other on the reversed lists. The order of the first pass guarantees that such a reach stays inside one component. A one-way edge points from the earlier finisher to the later one, so the reversed list does not lead back across it. The self loop adds no new vertex to any search because its endpoint is already labeled.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(V + E), because the two passes dominate and the count is one scan of the labels.
  • Space is O(V + E) for the adjacency lists, plus O(V) for the order, the labels and the size counters.
import java.util.*;

public final class SccSingletons {
    /**
     * Counts the components that contain exactly one vertex.
     * Time: O(V + E). Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: two vertices share a label only when each reaches the other.
     */
    static int singletons(int n, int[][] edges) {
        int[][] out = adjacency(n, edges, false);
        int[][] back = adjacency(n, edges, true);
        // Pass one: the finish order of the original graph.
        int[] order = new int[n];
        boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
        int[] cursor = new int[n];
        int[] stack = new int[n];
        int count = 0;
        for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
            if (seen[s]) continue;
            int top = 0;
            stack[top++] = s;
            seen[s] = true;
            while (top > 0) {
                int u = stack[top - 1];
                if (cursor[u] < out[u].length) {
                    int v = out[u][cursor[u]++];
                    if (!seen[v]) {
                        seen[v] = true;
                        stack[top++] = v;
                    }
                } else {
                    top--;
                    order[count++] = u;
                }
            }
        }
        // Pass two: size[c] counts the vertices that component c collects.
        boolean[] done = new boolean[n];
        int[] size = new int[n];
        int comps = 0;
        // Walk the order from the latest finisher to the earliest.
        for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
            int s = order[i];
            if (done[s]) continue;
            int top = 0;
            stack[top++] = s;
            done[s] = true;
            while (top > 0) {
                int u = stack[--top];
                // Every popped vertex joins the current component exactly once.
                size[comps]++;
                for (int v : back[u]) {
                    if (!done[v]) {
                        done[v] = true;
                        stack[top++] = v;
                    }
                }
            }
            comps++;
        }
        // Count the components that collected a single vertex.
        int alone = 0;
        for (int c = 0; c < comps; c++) if (size[c] == 1) alone++;
        return alone;
    }

    /** Builds out-lists, or in-lists when flip is true. */
    static int[][] adjacency(int n, int[][] edges, boolean flip) {
        int[] deg = new int[n];
        for (int[] e : edges) deg[flip ? e[1] : e[0]]++;
        int[][] g = new int[n][];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) g[v] = new int[deg[v]];
        int[] fill = new int[n];
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            int from = flip ? e[1] : e[0];
            int to = flip ? e[0] : e[1];
            g[from][fill[from]++] = to;
        }
        return g;
    }

    /** Oracle: a vertex is alone when no other vertex is mutually reachable with it. */
    static int oracle(int n, int[][] edges) {
        boolean[][] r = new boolean[n][n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) r[v][v] = true;
        for (int[] e : edges) r[e[0]][e[1]] = true;
        for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
                for (int j = 0; j < n; j++)
                    if (r[i][k] && r[k][j]) r[i][j] = true;
        int alone = 0;
        for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) {
            boolean partner = false;
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (v != u && r[u][v] && r[v][u]) partner = true;
            if (!partner) alone++;
        }
        return alone;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (singletons(3, new int[][] {{0, 1}, {1, 2}}) != 3) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (singletons(4, new int[][] {{0, 0}, {1, 2}, {2, 1}, {2, 3}}) != 2) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // One vertex and no edges is one singleton.
        if (singletons(1, new int[0][]) != 1) throw new AssertionError("single");
        // A self loop alone does not merge anything.
        if (singletons(2, new int[][] {{0, 0}, {1, 1}}) != 2) throw new AssertionError("loops");
        // A two-cycle removes both vertices from the count.
        if (singletons(2, new int[][] {{0, 1}, {1, 0}}) != 0) throw new AssertionError("pair");
        // Random graphs against the closure oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(2508);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            int m = rnd.nextInt(14);
            int[][] edges = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) edges[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n)};
            if (singletons(n, edges) != oracle(n, edges)) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Condensation DAG

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Let a directed graph use vertex labels 0 through n - 1 and edges [a, b]. Merge each strongly connected component into one node. Keep an edge from node C to node D when some edge leads from a vertex of C to a vertex of D and C differs from D. Count each such pair once. Return an int[] of length 3 with the number of nodes, the number of distinct edges between nodes, and the number of nodes that have no incoming edge.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 2000.
  • Edges satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 6000, and repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Result lists the three counts in the order given.
  • Mutation is not allowed, and edges stays unchanged.

Example 1. Input n = 6, edges = [[0,1],[1,0],[1,2],[2,3],[3,2],[0,2],[1,2],[4,5],[5,4],[3,4]], output [3,2,1].

Example 2. Input n = 3, edges = [[0,1],[0,1],[1,0],[2,2]], output [2,0,2].
