Prerequisites. The three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method counts distinct edges of the merged graph, so edges inside a component and repeated edges between components must not add to the count.

Hint

Which edges disappear after merging, and which edges now repeat between the same two nodes?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method labels every vertex with its component, using the two passes of this lesson. It then scans the edges. An edge whose endpoints carry different labels becomes a pair of labels, and a hash set stores each pair once, so parallel edges between two components count once. The method ignores an edge inside one component. The method counts the pairs and counts the labels that never appear as the target of a pair, which are the components that nothing points into.

The invariant is that the condensed graph has no cycle. If two components lay on a cycle, every vertex of one would reach every vertex of the other and back, so the two components would be one. The test harness checks this claim with a topological sort that consumes every component of the condensed graph.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(V + E) on average, because the two passes are linear and each edge makes one expected O(1) hash insertion.
  • Space is O(V + E) for the lists, the labels and the set of distinct pairs.
import java.util.*;

public final class SccCondensation {
    /**
     * Returns {component count, distinct edges between components, components with no incoming edge}.
     * Time: O(V + E) expected. Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: the condensed graph has no cycle, because a cycle would merge its components.
     */
    static int[] summary(int n, int[][] edges) {
        int[] label = labels(n, edges);
        int comps = 0;
        for (int x : label) comps = Math.max(comps, x + 1);
        // A pair key packs two labels into one long, so no boxing of arrays is needed.
        Set<Long> pairs = new HashSet<>();
        boolean[] hasIncoming = new boolean[comps];
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            int a = label[e[0]];
            int b = label[e[1]];
            // Edges inside a component disappear in the condensed graph.
            if (a == b) continue;
            // The set keeps one copy of each pair, so repeated edges count once.
            if (pairs.add((long) a * comps + b)) hasIncoming[b] = true;
        }
        int sources = 0;
        // A component with no incoming pair is a source of the condensed graph.
        for (boolean h : hasIncoming) if (!h) sources++;
        return new int[] {comps, pairs.size(), sources};
    }

    /** Kosaraju labels in discovery order of the second pass. */
    static int[] labels(int n, int[][] edges) {
        List<List<Integer>> out = new ArrayList<>();
        List<List<Integer>> back = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
            out.add(new ArrayList<>());
            back.add(new ArrayList<>());
        }
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            out.get(e[0]).add(e[1]);
            back.get(e[1]).add(e[0]);
        }
        boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
        int[] cursor = new int[n];
        int[] stack = new int[n];
        int[] order = new int[n];
        int count = 0;
        for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
            if (seen[s]) continue;
            int top = 0;
            stack[top++] = s;
            seen[s] = true;
            while (top > 0) {
                int u = stack[top - 1];
                if (cursor[u] < out.get(u).size()) {
                    int v = out.get(u).get(cursor[u]++);
                    if (!seen[v]) {
                        seen[v] = true;
                        stack[top++] = v;
                    }
                } else {
                    top--;
                    order[count++] = u;
                }
            }
        }
        int[] label = new int[n];
        Arrays.fill(label, -1);
        int next = 0;
        for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
            if (label[order[i]] != -1) continue;
            int top = 0;
            stack[top++] = order[i];
            label[order[i]] = next;
            while (top > 0) {
                int u = stack[--top];
                for (int v : back.get(u)) {
                    if (label[v] == -1) {
                        label[v] = next;
                        stack[top++] = v;
                    }
                }
            }
            next++;
        }
        return label;
    }

    /** Oracle: components from the transitive closure, then a set of component pairs. */
    static int[] oracle(int n, int[][] edges) {
        boolean[][] r = new boolean[n][n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) r[v][v] = true;
        for (int[] e : edges) r[e[0]][e[1]] = true;
        for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
                for (int j = 0; j < n; j++)
                    if (r[i][k] && r[k][j]) r[i][j] = true;
        // The smallest vertex of each component serves as its name.
        int[] rep = new int[n];
        for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) {
            rep[u] = u;
            for (int v = 0; v < u; v++) if (r[u][v] && r[v][u]) { rep[u] = v; break; }
        }
        Set<String> pairs = new HashSet<>();
        Set<Integer> names = new HashSet<>();
        Set<Integer> targets = new HashSet<>();
        for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) names.add(rep[u]);
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            if (rep[e[0]] != rep[e[1]]) {
                pairs.add(rep[e[0]] + ":" + rep[e[1]]);
                targets.add(rep[e[1]]);
            }
        }
        return new int[] {names.size(), pairs.size(), names.size() - targets.size()};
    }

    /** Harness check: a topological sort consumes every component of the condensed graph. */
    static boolean isAcyclic(int n, int[][] edges) {
        int[] label = labels(n, edges);
        int comps = 0;
        for (int x : label) comps = Math.max(comps, x + 1);
        List<List<Integer>> g = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int c = 0; c < comps; c++) g.add(new ArrayList<>());
        int[] indeg = new int[comps];
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            if (label[e[0]] != label[e[1]]) {
                g.get(label[e[0]]).add(label[e[1]]);
                indeg[label[e[1]]]++;
            }
        }
        ArrayDeque<Integer> ready = new ArrayDeque<>();
        for (int c = 0; c < comps; c++) if (indeg[c] == 0) ready.add(c);
        int taken = 0;
        while (!ready.isEmpty()) {
            int c = ready.poll();
            taken++;
            for (int d : g.get(c)) if (--indeg[d] == 0) ready.add(d);
        }
        return taken == comps;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        int[][] e1 = {{0, 1}, {1, 0}, {1, 2}, {2, 3}, {3, 2}, {0, 2}, {1, 2}, {4, 5}, {5, 4}, {3, 4}};
        if (!Arrays.equals(summary(6, e1), new int[] {3, 2, 1})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!Arrays.equals(summary(3, new int[][] {{0, 1}, {0, 1}, {1, 0}, {2, 2}}), new int[] {2, 0, 2})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // A hash set stores one copy of a repeated pair, which the count relies on.
        Set<Long> probe = new HashSet<>();
        if (!probe.add(5L) || probe.add(5L)) throw new AssertionError("set");
        // Random graphs: same summary as the oracle, and the condensed graph is acyclic.
        Random rnd = new Random(2509);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            int m = rnd.nextInt(16);
            int[][] edges = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) edges[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n)};
            if (!Arrays.equals(summary(n, edges), oracle(n, edges))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
            if (!isAcyclic(n, edges)) throw new AssertionError("cycle " + t);
        }
    }
}verified

Find Bridges With Low Links

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

One Cable Can Split A Network

A data center connects its servers with network cables. An operator asks which single cable, if it fails, leaves some servers unable to reach others. The first script tests the cables one at a time. It unplugs a cable in memory, checks whether every server still reaches every other server, and plugs the cable back. With 20,000 cables, the script runs 20,000 full reachability checks, and the nightly report takes hours. The question is ordinary, and a much faster answer exists.

Model the servers as vertices numbered 0 to n - 1 and the cables as undirected edges. Two cables may join the same pair of servers, and the input arrays stay unchanged. A connected component is a maximal group of vertices in which every vertex reaches every other vertex along edges. A bridge is an edge whose removal increases the number of connected components. Network operators call such a cable a single point of failure.

This lesson asks how to list every bridge with one traversal of the graph, without testing each edge on its own.

Simple approach

Removing Each Edge And Searching Again

The direct method follows the definition. For each edge, it builds the graph without that edge and runs a breadth-first search from one endpoint. When the search does not reach the other endpoint, the edge is a bridge.

static List<int[]> bridgesByRemoval(int n, int[][] cables) {
    List<int[]> found = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int skip = 0; skip < cables.length; skip++) {
        List<List<Integer>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
        for (int i = 0; i < cables.length; i++) {
            if (i == skip) continue;
            adj.get(cables[i][0]).add(cables[i][1]);
            adj.get(cables[i][1]).add(cables[i][0]);
        }
        boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
        ArrayDeque<Integer> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
        queue.add(cables[skip][0]);
        seen[cables[skip][0]] = true;
        while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
            int cur = queue.poll();
            for (int next : adj.get(cur)) {
                if (!seen[next]) { seen[next] = true; queue.add(next); }
            }
        }
        if (!seen[cables[skip][1]]) found.add(cables[skip]);
    }
    return found;
}

The method is correct, because a search without the edge reaches the far endpoint exactly when another route joins the two endpoints. The cost depends on the number of edges, since each edge triggers a new search.

Predict first: A network has 5,000 servers and 20,000 cables. About how many cable visits does bridgesByRemoval make in total?

It makes about 20,000 searches, and each search visits up to 40,000 adjacency entries, which is two entries for each of the 20,000 cables. The total is near 800 million entry visits, plus the cost of rebuilding the lists 20,000 times. The method has no memory between searches. Every search rediscovers almost the same graph, so the time grows as the product of edges and graph size.

Bottleneck

One Graph Is Searched Again And Again

Each of the E searches explores nearly the entire graph, and the graphs of two searches differ by a single edge. The total cost is O(E * (V + E)), where V is the number of vertices and E is the number of edges. For the network above that is hundreds of millions of steps, and a network ten times larger needs a hundred times more.

Most of this work is wasted, because most edges are not bridges. An edge lies on a cycle in nearly every real network, since operators add redundant cables on purpose. Once one search shows that a cycle covers an edge, the same fact covers every other edge on that cycle, and the method never reuses it.

A single depth-first search already visits every vertex and every edge once. While it runs, it can record how far back each part of the search can reach by a second route. The facts the repeated searches recompute are then collected in one pass of O(V + E) time. The next stage defines what to record.

Key idea

Asking What A Subtree Can Reach

A tree edge from a parent to a child is a bridge exactly when nothing in the child's subtree has another route to the parent or above.

The Depth-First Tree

Run depth-first search from vertex 0 and keep the edge along which each new vertex was first found. These tree edges form a spanning tree of the component, with each vertex except the root joined to one parent. The vertices below a vertex u in the tree, including u, form the subtree of u. Every other edge of the graph is a non-tree edge.

Discovery Time Orders The Vertices

The discovery time disc[u] is the number of vertices the search has discovered before u, so the root has disc 0. A vertex discovered later has a larger value. Discovery times give a cheap test for ancestry. A vertex on the path from the root to u has a smaller discovery time than u.

Every Other Edge Is A Back Edge

In an undirected graph, each non-tree edge joins a vertex to one of its ancestors. This kind of edge is a back edge. The reason is the order of the search, which has three cases when it stands at u and scans an edge to v. First, v is undiscovered, so the edge becomes a tree edge and v becomes a child of u. Second, v lies on the current path from the root to u, so v is an ancestor of u and the edge is a back edge. Third, v is already finished. Then v is a descendant of u, and the edge is the back edge that v scanned from below, so u ignores it. A finished v on another branch cannot occur, because the search would have crossed the edge from v and found u earlier.

The Low Link Value

The low link low[u] is the smallest discovery time that the subtree of u can reach by taking one back edge. The route may start at any vertex of the subtree. The back edge may not be the tree edge that joins u to its parent. Written as a rule, low[u] is the minimum of three kinds of value. The first is disc[u] itself. The second is disc[w] for every back edge from u to an ancestor w. The third is low[c] for every child c of u. The third part passes reach upward from deep vertices to their ancestors.

The Bridge Test

Take the tree edge from u to its child v. The edge is a bridge exactly when low[v] > disc[u]. If the condition holds, every route out of the subtree of v lands on a vertex discovered at time disc[u] + 1 or later. Such a vertex lies inside the subtree of v itself. Removing the edge strands the whole subtree. If the condition fails, some back edge leaves the subtree and lands on u or on an ancestor of u. That edge and the tree path form a second route, and removing the tree edge keeps the graph connected. A non-tree edge is never a bridge, because it closes a cycle with the tree path between its endpoints.

Skipping The Parent By Edge Number

When the search at v scans its edge to the parent u, that edge must not count as a back edge. Otherwise every low[v] would reach disc[u], and no bridge could appear. The safe rule skips the edge by its number in the input and not by the parent vertex. Two cables between the same servers have two numbers. The search skips only the one it arrived by, and the second cable correctly counts as a route back. The second trace below shows what goes wrong with a skip by vertex.

State

Arrays And The Search Clock

The finder reads n and the array cables of pairs [a, b], and it changes neither. It builds an adjacency list in which each entry holds the neighbor and the number of the cable, and it keeps four working values.

  • disc is an int[] of length n; the value -1 marks a vertex that the search has not reached.
  • low is an int[] of length n; it starts equal to disc[u] and only decreases.
  • timer is the next discovery time to give out; it increases by one at each new vertex.
  • parentId is the number of the cable the search used to enter the current vertex; the root receives -1.

The method writes each pair that passes the bridge test to a list as it finds the pair. All values are small nonnegative counts or -1, so int is enough and no overflow guard is needed. The search runs recursively, so the call depth equals the longest path that the search follows. The exercises use n of at most about 1000, so the default stack is enough. A deeper graph needs an explicit stack, as in lessons 05 and 06.

Walkthrough

Two Runs On Small Cable Maps

Two Triangles Joined By A Cable

The first map has seven servers and eight cables. Servers 0, 1 and 2 form a triangle. Servers 3, 4 and 5 form a second triangle. Cable 2-3 joins the triangles, and server 6 hangs from server 5 by cable 5-6. Each cell is a server in order of discovery, and the pointer at marks the server whose values change at this step. The variables are disc, low and flagged, which counts the bridges found so far. At step 4, server 2 sees the back edge to server 0 and low drops to 0. At step 8, server 5 sees the back edge to server 3 and low drops to 3. Server 6 returns with low 6, which exceeds disc 5 of server 5, so cable 5-6 is a bridge. Server 3 later returns with low 3, which exceeds disc 2 of server 2, so cable 2-3 is a bridge. Every other return fails the test, and the run finds exactly two bridges.

Skipping The Parent Vertex Fails

The second map has four servers and four cables. Cable 0-1 and cable 2-3 are single cables, and two cables join servers 1 and 2. Neither of the double cables is a bridge, because the other one still carries traffic. This run uses the wrong rule that skips every edge toward the parent vertex. At server 2, the second cable toward server 1 is skipped, so low of server 2 stays 2. When server 2 returns, the value 2 exceeds disc 1 of server 1, and the run flags the pair 1-2 as a bridge. The correct rule, which skips only the cable of arrival, lets server 2 see the second cable as a back edge to server 1 and sets low to 1. The flagged count ends at 3 where the true count is 2.

Code

Finding Bridges In One Search

The class stores the graph and the two arrays once, and the method visit fills them. The loop in find starts a search from every undiscovered vertex, so a graph with several components also works.

final class BridgeFinder {
    private final List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    private final int[] disc;
    private final int[] low;
    private final List<int[]> bridges = new ArrayList<>();
    private int timer = 0;

    BridgeFinder(int n, int[][] cables) {
        disc = new int[n];
        low = new int[n];
        Arrays.fill(disc, -1);
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
        for (int id = 0; id < cables.length; id++) {
            adj.get(cables[id][0]).add(new int[] {cables[id][1], id});
            adj.get(cables[id][1]).add(new int[] {cables[id][0], id});
        }
    }

    List<int[]> find() {
        for (int s = 0; s < disc.length; s++) {
            if (disc[s] == -1) visit(s, -1);
        }
        return bridges;
    }

    private void visit(int u, int parentId) {
        disc[u] = low[u] = timer++;
        for (int[] step : adj.get(u)) {
            int v = step[0];
            if (step[1] == parentId) continue;
            if (disc[v] == -1) {
                visit(v, step[1]);
                low[u] = Math.min(low[u], low[v]);
                if (low[v] > disc[u]) bridges.add(new int[] {u, v});
            } else {
                low[u] = Math.min(low[u], disc[v]);
            }
        }
    }
}

The order of two lines inside the tree branch matters. The call to visit(v, ...) comes first, because low[v] is final only after the child returns. The bridge test comes after the update of low[u], and it reads low[v], which does not depend on that update. In the other branch, disc[v] is smaller than disc[u] for an ancestor and larger for a descendant already finished. The larger value cannot lower low[u], so the code needs no extra test. The search visits each adjacency entry once, so the time is O(V + E). The adjacency list, the two arrays and the call stack use O(V + E) memory.

When to use it

Recognizing Bridge Problems

Reading The Cue

Use this method when an undirected graph and a question about the loss of one edge appear together. Typical statements ask which cables, roads, or dependencies are critical, or whether a network survives any single failure. The answer is usually a list of edges, a count, or a yes or no for a whole graph. With the same two arrays, the test low[v] >= disc[u] finds articulation points, the vertices whose removal splits the graph.

Checking The Invariant

The invariant is that low[u] is final when visit(u, ...) returns. It equals the smallest discovery time that the subtree of u reaches through one back edge, other than the edge to its parent. It holds because low[u] starts at disc[u], every back edge of u can only lower it, and every child hands up its own final value. Check the cases that break it. Test a graph with two cables between the same pair. Test a graph with no edge at all. Test a root that has several children, where the bridge test works unchanged but the articulation test needs a separate rule.

Avoiding The False Friend

A false friend is a familiar idea that looks right for the problem and fails on it. The first false friend is to delete each edge in turn and recount the components. It is correct, but it costs O(E * (V + E)), as the naive stage shows. The second false friend is the skip by parent vertex, shown in the second trace. It is correct for simple graphs and wrong as soon as a pair of vertices has two edges, because it also skips the second edge. Do not use this method when edges have capacities and the question asks for the cheapest set of edges to cut. That question needs flow, which this chapter does not cover.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Discovery And Low Values

Author exercise

Problem Specification. An undirected connected graph spans the n servers 0, ..., n - 1 and the edges in edges, where edge number i joins edges[i][0] and edges[i][1]. Run a depth-first search from vertex 0. At each vertex, scan its edges in the order of their numbers, and treat a self loop as one edge scanned twice. Return an int[][] of two rows. Row 0 is the discovery time of every vertex. Row 1 is the low link of every vertex, where the edge used to enter a vertex does not count as a back edge, and any other edge does.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 300.
  • Edges satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 1500, and repeated pairs and self loops may occur.
  • Connectivity is guaranteed, so every vertex receives a discovery time.
  • Mutation of edges is not allowed.

Example 1. Input n = 5, edges = [[0,1],[0,2],[1,2],[2,3],[3,4]], output [[0,1,2,3,4],[0,0,0,3,4]].

Example 2. Input n = 3, edges = [[0,1],[0,1],[1,2]], output [[0,1,2],[0,0,2]].

Prerequisites. The variables and the code of this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. The method returns the two arrays and does not test for bridges, so the only work is updating low from child returns and back edges.

Hint

When the search stands at vertex 1 in the second example, does the second cable to vertex 0 count as a way back?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method builds an adjacency list in which every entry stores the neighbor and the number of the edge. It then runs a recursive search from vertex 0. On entry to a vertex, the method gives it the next discovery time and sets its low link equal to that time. For each edge in order, it skips the edge number used to arrive, so a second edge between the same pair still counts. A neighbor that is undiscovered becomes a child, and after the call returns the method lowers the low link of the parent to the child value. A neighbor that is already discovered lowers the low link to its discovery time.

The invariant is that when a vertex returns, its low link is the smallest discovery time reachable from its subtree through one back edge other than the arrival edge. A discovered neighbor with a larger time is a descendant, so the minimum ignores it automatically. A self loop shows the vertex itself, which equals its own time and changes nothing.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(V + E), because each adjacency entry is scanned once and each vertex is entered once.
  • Space is O(V + E) for the adjacency list, the two arrays and a call stack as deep as the longest tree path.
import java.util.*;

public final class LowLinkValues {
    /** Adjacency list: entry {neighbor, edge number}, in edge order. */
    static List<List<int[]>> build(int n, int[][] edges) {
        List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
        // One empty list per vertex.
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
        // Each edge is stored at both ends, so a self loop appears twice at its vertex.
        for (int id = 0; id < edges.length; id++) {
            adj.get(edges[id][0]).add(new int[] {edges[id][1], id});
            adj.get(edges[id][1]).add(new int[] {edges[id][0], id});
        }
        return adj;
    }

    /**
     * Returns {disc, low} for a depth-first search from vertex 0.
     * Time: O(V + E). Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: on return, low[u] is the least discovery time the subtree of u reaches by one back edge.
     */
    static int[][] discAndLow(int n, int[][] edges) {
        List<List<int[]>> adj = build(n, edges);
        int[] disc = new int[n];
        int[] low = new int[n];
        // The value -1 marks a vertex the search has not reached.
        Arrays.fill(disc, -1);
        int[] timer = {0};
        visit(0, -1, adj, disc, low, timer);
        return new int[][] {disc, low};
    }

    static void visit(int u, int parentId, List<List<int[]>> adj, int[] disc, int[] low, int[] timer) {
        // The vertex receives the next time, and its low link starts at that time.
        disc[u] = low[u] = timer[0]++;
        // Each adjacency entry is scanned once, which gives the linear cost.
        for (int[] step : adj.get(u)) {
            int v = step[0];
            // The arrival edge is skipped by number, so a parallel edge still counts.
            if (step[1] == parentId) continue;
            if (disc[v] == -1) {
                // A new vertex becomes a child, and its low link is final after the call.
                visit(v, step[1], adj, disc, low, timer);
                low[u] = Math.min(low[u], low[v]);
            } else {
                // A discovered vertex is an ancestor or a finished descendant; a larger time changes nothing.
                low[u] = Math.min(low[u], disc[v]);
            }
        }
    }

    /** Oracle: an explicit-stack search builds the tree, then low is computed straight from its definition. */
    static int[][] oracle(int n, int[][] edges) {
        List<List<int[]>> adj = build(n, edges);
        int[] disc = new int[n];
        int[] parent = new int[n];
        int[] parentEdge = new int[n];
        Arrays.fill(disc, -1);
        Arrays.fill(parent, -1);
        Arrays.fill(parentEdge, -1);
        int[] next = new int[n];
        Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
        int t = 0;
        // The root is discovered first.
        disc[0] = t++;
        stack.push(0);
        // Each loop pass either descends to a new vertex or finishes the top vertex.
        while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
            int u = stack.peek();
            if (next[u] == adj.get(u).size()) { stack.pop(); continue; }
            int[] step = adj.get(u).get(next[u]++);
            if (disc[step[0]] == -1) {
                disc[step[0]] = t++;
                parent[step[0]] = u;
                parentEdge[step[0]] = step[1];
                stack.push(step[0]);
            }
        }
        int[] low = new int[n];
        // For each vertex, take the minimum over its whole subtree by definition.
        for (int u = 0; u < n; u++) {
            int best = disc[u];
            for (int x = 0; x < n; x++) {
                // x is in the subtree of u when walking up the parents from x reaches u.
                boolean inside = false;
                for (int y = x; y != -1; y = parent[y]) if (y == u) { inside = true; break; }
                if (!inside) continue;
                best = Math.min(best, disc[x]);
                // Every edge leaving x counts, except the edge that enters u from its parent.
                for (int[] step : adj.get(x)) {
                    if (step[1] == parentEdge[u]) continue;
                    best = Math.min(best, disc[step[0]]);
                }
            }
            low[u] = best;
        }
        return new int[][] {disc, low};
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        int[][] a = discAndLow(5, new int[][] {{0,1},{0,2},{1,2},{2,3},{3,4}});
        if (!Arrays.equals(a[0], new int[] {0,1,2,3,4}) || !Arrays.equals(a[1], new int[] {0,0,0,3,4})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        int[][] b = discAndLow(3, new int[][] {{0,1},{0,1},{1,2}});
        if (!Arrays.equals(b[0], new int[] {0,1,2}) || !Arrays.equals(b[1], new int[] {0,0,2})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // A single vertex with a self loop keeps low equal to disc.
        int[][] c = discAndLow(1, new int[][] {{0,0}});
        if (c[0][0] != 0 || c[1][0] != 0) throw new AssertionError("self loop");
        // Random connected multigraphs against the oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(2507);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            List<int[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
            // A random tree first guarantees connectivity.
            for (int v = 1; v < n; v++) list.add(new int[] {rnd.nextInt(v), v});
            int extra = rnd.nextInt(6);
            // Extra edges may repeat a pair or loop on a vertex.
            for (int k = 0; k < extra; k++) list.add(new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n)});
            Collections.shuffle(list, rnd);
            // Vertex 0 may now be a leaf, which is fine because the search still starts there.
            int[][] edges = list.toArray(new int[0][]);
            int[][] got = discAndLow(n, edges);
            int[][] want = oracle(n, edges);
            if (!Arrays.equals(got[0], want[0]) || !Arrays.equals(got[1], want[1])) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Bridge Test

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A depth-first search of a connected undirected graph produced three arrays of length n. Entry parent[v] is the parent of v in the search tree, with parent[0] = -1. Entry disc[v] is the discovery time of v. Entry low[v] is the low link of v. Return an int[] that lists in increasing order every vertex v other than the root whose tree edge to parent[v] is a bridge.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 300.
  • Arrays come from a real search, with low computed by skipping the arrival edge by number.
  • Result is empty when the graph has no bridge.
  • Mutation of the three arrays is not allowed.

Example 1. Input parent = [-1,0,1,2,3,4], disc = [0,1,2,3,4,5], low = [0,0,0,3,4,5], output [3,4,5].

Example 2. Input parent = [-1,0,1], disc = [0,1,2], low = [0,0,0], output [].

Prerequisites. The exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method makes a decision from finished arrays and does not search, and it must use a strict comparison.

Hint

In the second example, low[1] equals disc[0]. Does an equal value mean that the subtree escapes?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method walks the vertices in increasing order and skips the root. For each other vertex v, it compares low[v] with the discovery time of its parent. A strictly larger low link means that the subtree of v has no route to the parent or above. The tree edge is then a bridge, and the method adds v. Because the loop runs in increasing order, the output is already sorted.

The invariant is that low[v] > disc[parent[v]] holds exactly when the subtree of v is attached to the rest of the graph only by its tree edge. The comparison must be strict. An equal value means a back edge lands on the parent itself, which gives a second route.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n), because the method reads each vertex once and compares two numbers.
  • Space is O(1) beyond the output list, since it keeps no other structure.
import java.util.*;

public final class BridgeTestOnArrays {
    /** Adjacency list: entry {neighbor, edge number}, in edge order. */
    static List<List<int[]>> build(int n, int[][] edges) {
        List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
        // One empty list per vertex.
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
        // Each edge is stored at both ends, so a self loop appears twice at its vertex.
        for (int id = 0; id < edges.length; id++) {
            adj.get(edges[id][0]).add(new int[] {edges[id][1], id});
            adj.get(edges[id][1]).add(new int[] {edges[id][0], id});
        }
        return adj;
    }

    /**
     * Returns the vertices whose tree edge to the parent is a bridge, in increasing order.
     * Time: O(n). Space: O(1) beyond the result.
     * Invariant: low[v] > disc[parent[v]] exactly when no back edge leaves the subtree of v upward.
     */
    static int[] bridgeChildren(int[] parent, int[] disc, int[] low) {
        List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
        // Vertex 0 is the root and has no tree edge, so the loop starts at 1.
        for (int v = 1; v < parent.length; v++) {
            // A strict comparison separates a stranded subtree from one with a second route.
            if (low[v] > disc[parent[v]]) out.add(v);
        }
        int[] res = new int[out.size()];
        // Copy the list into the int array that the contract requires.
        for (int i = 0; i < res.length; i++) res[i] = out.get(i);
        return res;
    }

    /** Test helper: arrays from a depth-first search that skips the arrival edge by number. */
    static int[][] arrays(int n, int[][] edges) {
        List<List<int[]>> adj = build(n, edges);
        int[] disc = new int[n];
        int[] low = new int[n];
        int[] parent = new int[n];
        Arrays.fill(disc, -1);
        Arrays.fill(parent, -1);
        go(0, -1, adj, disc, low, parent, new int[] {0});
        return new int[][] {parent, disc, low};
    }

    static void go(int u, int pid, List<List<int[]>> adj, int[] disc, int[] low, int[] parent, int[] timer) {
        disc[u] = low[u] = timer[0]++;
        for (int[] s : adj.get(u)) {
            if (s[1] == pid) continue;
            if (disc[s[0]] == -1) {
                parent[s[0]] = u;
                go(s[0], s[1], adj, disc, low, parent, timer);
                low[u] = Math.min(low[u], low[s[0]]);
            } else low[u] = Math.min(low[u], disc[s[0]]);
        }
    }

    /** Oracle: remove each edge and search again; a bridge maps to its child endpoint. */
    static int[] oracle(int n, int[][] edges, int[] disc) {
        TreeSet<Integer> kids = new TreeSet<>();
        for (int skip = 0; skip < edges.length; skip++) {
            boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
            Deque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
            seen[0] = true;
            st.push(0);
            // Plain reachability from vertex 0 without the skipped edge.
            while (!st.isEmpty()) {
                int x = st.pop();
                for (int i = 0; i < edges.length; i++) {
                    if (i == skip) continue;
                    int y = edges[i][0] == x ? edges[i][1] : edges[i][1] == x ? edges[i][0] : -1;
                    if (y >= 0 && !seen[y]) { seen[y] = true; st.push(y); }
                }
            }
            boolean cut = false;
            for (boolean s : seen) if (!s) cut = true;
            // The child endpoint is the one discovered later.
            if (cut) kids.add(disc[edges[skip][0]] > disc[edges[skip][1]] ? edges[skip][0] : edges[skip][1]);
        }
        int[] res = new int[kids.size()];
        int k = 0;
        for (int v : kids) res[k++] = v;
        return res;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        int[] r1 = bridgeChildren(new int[] {-1,0,1,2,3,4}, new int[] {0,1,2,3,4,5}, new int[] {0,0,0,3,4,5});
        if (!Arrays.equals(r1, new int[] {3,4,5})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        int[] r2 = bridgeChildren(new int[] {-1,0,1}, new int[] {0,1,2}, new int[] {0,0,0});
        if (r2.length != 0) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // The equal case is not a bridge, and one more than the parent time is.
        if (bridgeChildren(new int[] {-1,0}, new int[] {0,1}, new int[] {0,0}).length != 0) throw new AssertionError("equal");
        if (bridgeChildren(new int[] {-1,0}, new int[] {0,1}, new int[] {0,1}).length != 1) throw new AssertionError("greater");
        // Random connected multigraphs: arrays from a real search, answers from edge removal.
        Random rnd = new Random(2508);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            List<int[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int v = 1; v < n; v++) list.add(new int[] {rnd.nextInt(v), v});
            for (int k = rnd.nextInt(5); k > 0; k--) list.add(new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n)});
            Collections.shuffle(list, rnd);
            int[][] edges = list.toArray(new int[0][]);
            int[][] a = arrays(n, edges);
            int[] got = bridgeChildren(a[0], a[1], a[2]);
            int[] want = oracle(n, edges, a[1]);
            if (!Arrays.equals(got, want)) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Root Articulation Rule

Author exercise

Problem Specification. An undirected graph contains vertices 0 to n - 1 and the edges in edges. An articulation point is a vertex whose removal, together with its edges, increases the number of connected components among the remaining vertices. Return an int[] of all articulation points in increasing order. Start a depth-first search from every undiscovered vertex in increasing order. A search root is an articulation point when it has at least two children in its tree. Any other vertex u is one when it has a child c with low[c] >= disc[u].

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 300.
  • Edges satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 1500, and repeated pairs and self loops may occur.
  • Components may be several, and an isolated vertex is never a result.
  • Mutation of edges is not allowed.

Example 1. Input n = 6, edges = [[0,1],[0,2],[0,3],[3,4],[4,5],[5,3]], output [0,3].

Example 2. Input n = 7, edges = [[0,1],[1,2],[2,0],[3,4],[4,5],[5,3],[5,6]], output [5].

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method counts children at the root and uses the nonstrict comparison >= below it, where the bridge test uses a strict one.

Hint

What does the nonroot rule report for a root, and how many children does a root need before its removal splits the graph?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method searches from every undiscovered vertex in increasing order, so each component gets its own root. Inside the recursive call, it counts the children of the current vertex. After each child returns, it lowers the low link of the current vertex. For a vertex that is not a root, one child with low[c] >= disc[u] marks the vertex. The comparison is nonstrict, because a back edge that lands exactly on u still disconnects the child subtree once u is gone. For a root, the method ignores the low links and marks the root only when it has two or more children. A marked vertex goes into a sorted set, so repeats collapse.

The invariant has two parts. A nonroot vertex splits the graph exactly when some child subtree cannot reach a proper ancestor. A root splits it exactly when its tree has two or more child subtrees. In a root with one child, every other vertex of the component sits in that child subtree, so removing the root leaves one piece. The nonroot formula would flag every root with a child, because the root has the smallest discovery time and low[c] >= disc[root] always holds.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(V + E), because the searches together scan each adjacency entry once.
  • Space is O(V + E) for the adjacency list, the arrays, the result set and the call stack.
import java.util.*;

public final class RootArticulationRule {
    /** Adjacency list: entry {neighbor, edge number}, in edge order. */
    static List<List<int[]>> build(int n, int[][] edges) {
        List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
        // One empty list per vertex.
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
        // Each edge is stored at both ends, so a self loop appears twice at its vertex.
        for (int id = 0; id < edges.length; id++) {
            adj.get(edges[id][0]).add(new int[] {edges[id][1], id});
            adj.get(edges[id][1]).add(new int[] {edges[id][0], id});
        }
        return adj;
    }

    /**
     * Returns all articulation points in increasing order.
     * Time: O(V + E). Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: a nonroot u splits the graph when a child c has low[c] >= disc[u]; a root splits it with two or more children.
     */
    static int[] articulation(int n, int[][] edges) {
        List<List<int[]>> adj = build(n, edges);
        int[] disc = new int[n];
        int[] low = new int[n];
        Arrays.fill(disc, -1);
        TreeSet<Integer> found = new TreeSet<>();
        int[] timer = {0};
        // A new search starts at each vertex that earlier searches did not reach.
        for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
            if (disc[s] == -1) visit(s, -1, adj, disc, low, timer, found);
        }
        int[] out = new int[found.size()];
        int k = 0;
        // The tree set already iterates in increasing order.
        for (int v : found) out[k++] = v;
        return out;
    }

    static void visit(int u, int parentId, List<List<int[]>> adj, int[] disc, int[] low, int[] timer, TreeSet<Integer> found) {
        disc[u] = low[u] = timer[0]++;
        int children = 0;
        // Each adjacency entry is scanned once.
        for (int[] step : adj.get(u)) {
            int v = step[0];
            // The arrival edge is skipped by number.
            if (step[1] == parentId) continue;
            if (disc[v] == -1) {
                children++;
                visit(v, step[1], adj, disc, low, timer, found);
                low[u] = Math.min(low[u], low[v]);
                // The nonstrict test applies below the root only.
                if (parentId != -1 && low[v] >= disc[u]) found.add(u);
            } else {
                low[u] = Math.min(low[u], disc[v]);
            }
        }
        // A root is decided by its child count and by nothing else.
        if (parentId == -1 && children >= 2) found.add(u);
    }

    /** Counts components among the vertices that are not removed. */
    static int components(int n, int[][] edges, int removed) {
        boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
        int count = 0;
        for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
            if (s == removed || seen[s]) continue;
            count++;
            Deque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
            seen[s] = true;
            st.push(s);
            // Plain reachability that never enters the removed vertex.
            while (!st.isEmpty()) {
                int x = st.pop();
                for (int[] e : edges) {
                    int y = e[0] == x ? e[1] : e[1] == x ? e[0] : -1;
                    if (y >= 0 && y != removed && !seen[y]) { seen[y] = true; st.push(y); }
                }
            }
        }
        return count;
    }

    /** Oracle: remove each vertex and compare component counts. */
    static int[] oracle(int n, int[][] edges) {
        int base = components(n, edges, -1);
        List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
            boolean alone = true;
            // A vertex with no edge to another vertex is a component by itself.
            for (int[] e : edges) if ((e[0] == v || e[1] == v) && e[0] != e[1]) alone = false;
            int expected = alone ? base - 1 : base;
            if (components(n, edges, v) > expected) out.add(v);
        }
        int[] res = new int[out.size()];
        for (int i = 0; i < res.length; i++) res[i] = out.get(i);
        return res;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        int[] a = articulation(6, new int[][] {{0,1},{0,2},{0,3},{3,4},{4,5},{5,3}});
        if (!Arrays.equals(a, new int[] {0,3})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        int[] b = articulation(7, new int[][] {{0,1},{1,2},{2,0},{3,4},{4,5},{5,3},{5,6}});
        if (!Arrays.equals(b, new int[] {5})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // A root with one child is not an articulation point, and an isolated vertex never is.
        if (articulation(2, new int[][] {{0,1}}).length != 0) throw new AssertionError("root one child");
        if (articulation(3, new int[][] {{0,0}}).length != 0) throw new AssertionError("isolated");
        // A path of three has a middle articulation point.
        if (!Arrays.equals(articulation(3, new int[][] {{0,1},{1,2}}), new int[] {1})) throw new AssertionError("path");
        // Random graphs, possibly disconnected, with repeats and loops, against the oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(2509);
        for (int t = 0; t < 4000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            int m = rnd.nextInt(10);
            int[][] edges = new int[m][];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) edges[i] = new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n)};
            if (!Arrays.equals(articulation(n, edges), oracle(n, edges))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Critical Connections in a Network

LeetCode 1192

Problem Specification. A network has servers 0 to n - 1 and undirected connections in connections. The network is connected. A critical connection is a connection whose removal disconnects the network. Removing a critical connection leaves two components, and its small side is the smaller of the two component sizes. Return an int[] of size two, where index 0 gives the number of critical connections. The second entry is the largest small side over all critical connections, or 0 when none exists.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Servers satisfy 1 <= n <= 1000.
  • Connections satisfy 0 <= connections.length <= 3000, and repeated pairs and self loops may occur.
  • Connectivity is guaranteed.
  • Result has length 2, and the two entries are nonnegative.

Example 1. Input n = 7, connections = [[0,1],[1,2],[2,0],[2,3],[3,4],[4,5],[5,3],[5,6],[6,5]], output [1,3].

Example 2. Input n = 6, connections = [[0,1],[1,2],[2,0],[2,3],[3,4],[4,5]], output [3,3].

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method returns a count and a size and not the list of connections. It must also add up subtree sizes during the same search.

Hint

If the search also returns the size of each subtree, how large is the side below a bridge, and how large is the other side?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method runs one search from server 0 and returns the size of each subtree. When a child v of u returns with low[v] > disc[u], the tree edge is critical. The subtree of v holds size[v] servers, so removing the edge leaves sides of size[v] and n - size[v]. The method counts the edge and keeps the largest value of the smaller side. The standard task lists the edges. This contract needs only a count and a size, so the method stores no edge list.

The invariant is that after a child returns, low[v] and the subtree size of v are final. The size is the number of servers on the side of v when the tree edge is cut. A bridge leaves no other connection between the subtree and the rest. The method skips the arrival edge by number, so a repeated connection is never critical.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(V + E), because the single search scans each adjacency entry once and adds sizes in constant time per vertex.
  • Space is O(V + E) for the adjacency list, the arrays and the recursion, which reaches depth n on a path.
import java.util.*;

public final class CriticalSmallSide {
    /** Adjacency list: entry {neighbor, edge number}, in edge order. */
    static List<List<int[]>> build(int n, int[][] edges) {
        List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
        // One empty list per vertex.
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
        // Each edge is stored at both ends, so a self loop appears twice at its vertex.
        for (int id = 0; id < edges.length; id++) {
            adj.get(edges[id][0]).add(new int[] {edges[id][1], id});
            adj.get(edges[id][1]).add(new int[] {edges[id][0], id});
        }
        return adj;
    }

    static int count;
    static int bestSmall;

    /**
     * Returns {number of critical connections, largest small side}.
     * Time: O(V + E). Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: when a child returns, low[v] and its subtree size are final, so a bridge cuts size[v] from n - size[v].
     */
    static int[] analyze(int n, int[][] connections) {
        List<List<int[]>> adj = build(n, connections);
        int[] disc = new int[n];
        int[] low = new int[n];
        Arrays.fill(disc, -1);
        count = 0;
        bestSmall = 0;
        visit(0, -1, n, adj, disc, low, new int[] {0});
        return new int[] {count, bestSmall};
    }

    static int visit(int u, int parentId, int n, List<List<int[]>> adj, int[] disc, int[] low, int[] timer) {
        disc[u] = low[u] = timer[0]++;
        // The vertex counts itself in its subtree size.
        int size = 1;
        for (int[] step : adj.get(u)) {
            int v = step[0];
            // The arrival edge is skipped by number.
            if (step[1] == parentId) continue;
            if (disc[v] == -1) {
                int below = visit(v, step[1], n, adj, disc, low, timer);
                size += below;
                low[u] = Math.min(low[u], low[v]);
                // A critical connection cuts the child subtree from the rest of the network.
                if (low[v] > disc[u]) {
                    count++;
                    bestSmall = Math.max(bestSmall, Math.min(below, n - below));
                }
            } else {
                low[u] = Math.min(low[u], disc[v]);
            }
        }
        return size;
    }

    /** Oracle: remove each connection, measure the side that contains server 0, and take the smaller side. */
    static int[] oracle(int n, int[][] connections) {
        int cnt = 0;
        int best = 0;
        for (int skip = 0; skip < connections.length; skip++) {
            boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
            Deque<Integer> st = new ArrayDeque<>();
            seen[0] = true;
            st.push(0);
            int reached = 1;
            // Reachability from server 0 without the skipped connection.
            while (!st.isEmpty()) {
                int x = st.pop();
                for (int i = 0; i < connections.length; i++) {
                    if (i == skip) continue;
                    int y = connections[i][0] == x ? connections[i][1] : connections[i][1] == x ? connections[i][0] : -1;
                    if (y >= 0 && !seen[y]) { seen[y] = true; reached++; st.push(y); }
                }
            }
            // Fewer reached servers than n means this connection is critical.
            if (reached < n) { cnt++; best = Math.max(best, Math.min(reached, n - reached)); }
        }
        return new int[] {cnt, best};
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        int[] a = analyze(7, new int[][] {{0,1},{1,2},{2,0},{2,3},{3,4},{4,5},{5,3},{5,6},{6,5}});
        if (!Arrays.equals(a, new int[] {1,3})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        int[] b = analyze(6, new int[][] {{0,1},{1,2},{2,0},{2,3},{3,4},{4,5}});
        if (!Arrays.equals(b, new int[] {3,3})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // A single server and a pure cycle have no critical connection.
        if (!Arrays.equals(analyze(1, new int[0][]), new int[] {0,0})) throw new AssertionError("single");
        if (!Arrays.equals(analyze(4, new int[][] {{0,1},{1,2},{2,3},{3,0}}), new int[] {0,0})) throw new AssertionError("cycle");
        // A path of 1000 servers recurses 1000 deep and still completes with all 999 connections critical.
        int[][] path = new int[999][];
        for (int i = 0; i < 999; i++) path[i] = new int[] {i, i + 1};
        if (!Arrays.equals(analyze(1000, path), new int[] {999,500})) throw new AssertionError("deep path");
        // Random connected multigraphs against the oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(2510);
        for (int t = 0; t < 3000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(9);
            List<int[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int v = 1; v < n; v++) list.add(new int[] {rnd.nextInt(v), v});
            for (int k = rnd.nextInt(6); k > 0; k--) list.add(new int[] {rnd.nextInt(n), rnd.nextInt(n)});
            Collections.shuffle(list, rnd);
            int[][] edges = list.toArray(new int[0][]);
            if (!Arrays.equals(analyze(n, edges), oracle(n, edges))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified

Choose Edges For A Minimum Spanning Tree

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Linking Every Office At Lowest Price

A company has six offices and wants one private network that joins all of them. A telecom provider quotes a price for each cable between two offices, and not every pair of offices has a quote. A cable works in both directions. The company must be able to send data from any office to any other office, possibly through several cables. It wants the lowest total price for the cables it buys.

Model the offices as vertices numbered 0 to n - 1 and each quoted cable as an undirected edge with a weight, which is its price. The input is a connected graph, and the method does not change it. The task is to choose some of the edges so that every office reaches every other office and the sum of the chosen weights is as small as possible. Weights are whole numbers and may be zero, and the inputs of these lessons never use negative weights.

This lesson asks how to decide that an edge belongs in the purchase without comparing every possible purchase, and it asks which of two standard methods fits a given input.

Simple approach

Buying The Shortest Routes To Headquarters

A natural first plan is to treat office 0 as headquarters and give every other office its cheapest route to it. The engineer runs Dijkstra's method from office 0, remembers for each office the cable that last improved its distance, and buys exactly those cables. Every office then reaches headquarters, so the network is connected. The method sums the weights of the bought cables.

static long routesToHeadquarters(int n, int[][] cables) {
    List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int[] c : cables) {
        adj.get(c[0]).add(new int[] {c[1], c[2]});
        adj.get(c[1]).add(new int[] {c[0], c[2]});
    }
    long[] dist = new long[n];
    int[] lastPrice = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(dist, Long.MAX_VALUE);
    dist[0] = 0;
    PriorityQueue<long[]> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Long.compare(a[0], b[0]));
    heap.add(new long[] {0, 0});
    while (!heap.isEmpty()) {
        long[] top = heap.poll();
        int u = (int) top[1];
        if (top[0] > dist[u]) continue;
        for (int[] e : adj.get(u)) {
            if (top[0] + e[1] < dist[e[0]]) {
                dist[e[0]] = top[0] + e[1];
                lastPrice[e[0]] = e[1];
                heap.add(new long[] {dist[e[0]], e[0]});
            }
        }
    }
    long total = 0;
    for (int v = 1; v < n; v++) total += lastPrice[v];
    return total;
}

The method connects all offices and runs in O(E log E) time. The open question is whether the sum it returns is the lowest possible.

Predict first: Three offices have the cables 0 to 1 at price 5, 0 to 2 at price 5, and 1 to 2 at price 1. What total does routesToHeadquarters return, and is it the lowest?

It returns 10, which is not the lowest. Office 1 and office 2 each reach headquarters most cheaply by their own direct cable, so the method buys both cables of price 5. Buying the cable of price 1 together with one cable of price 5 also connects all three offices, and the total is only 6. The method minimizes the distance of each office to headquarters. The company wants the smallest sum of prices, which is a different quantity.

Bottleneck

Shortest Routes Ignore The Total Price

Distance Is Not Price

Dijkstra's method judges a cable by how much it shortens the trip to office 0. The company does not care about trips. It pays once for each cable it buys, no matter how much data travels over it. Take a cable of price 1 between two offices that are both far from headquarters. It never improves a distance from headquarters, so the method ignores it. That cable is exactly the one that lowers the bill.

Trying Every Purchase Is Too Slow

The honest alternative is to try every purchase. A purchase that connects n offices needs at least n - 1 cables. The method could test every subset of n - 1 cables for connection and keep the cheapest. With E cables that is C(E, n - 1) subsets, which is O(2^E) in the worst case. The values E = 40 and n = 10 already give more than 270 million subsets to test.

A Test For One Edge

The gap is a local rule. The method needs a test that looks at one edge and says that this edge is safe to buy, meaning that some cheapest purchase contains it. If such a test exists and can be applied repeatedly, the method builds the answer edge by edge and never reconsiders a decision. The test must also be cheap to evaluate, so that the total work stays close to one pass over the edges plus the price of ordering them.

Key idea

Lightest Edge Across A Split Is Safe

The method buys the lightest edge that joins two groups of offices, because some cheapest purchase always contains it.

A Network With No Spare Cable

A spanning tree of a connected graph is a set of exactly n - 1 edges that connects all n vertices and contains no cycle. A cycle is a closed chain of edges, and in a cycle any one cable is spare, because the other cables still connect its two ends. A purchase with the lowest total weight never keeps a spare cable when weights are positive. When weights are zero a spare cable costs nothing, so the method can drop it without changing the total. A minimum spanning tree is a spanning tree whose total weight is the smallest among all spanning trees. The graph may have several of them.

Splitting The Offices Into Two Groups

A cut is a division of the vertices into two nonempty groups. A crossing edge of a cut is an edge with one endpoint in each group. A set of chosen edges respects a cut when none of them is a crossing edge. For any cut of a connected graph, at least one crossing edge exists, because otherwise the two groups would have no connection at all.

Why The Lightest Crossing Edge Is Safe

The cut property states the rule. Let A be a set of edges that lies inside some minimum spanning tree T, and let a cut respect A. Let e be a crossing edge of the lowest weight. Then A plus e lies inside some minimum spanning tree.

The proof exchanges one edge. If T already contains e, nothing remains to show. Otherwise adding e to T creates exactly one cycle. That cycle enters one group through e, so it must return through a second crossing edge f that belongs to T. The edge f is not in A, because A respects the cut. Since e is lightest among the crossing edges, its weight is at most the weight of f. Removing f and keeping e gives a spanning tree whose weight is no larger than the weight of T. That tree is also minimum, and it contains A and e. The argument never uses the sign of a weight, so negative weights are allowed, in contrast to Dijkstra's method.

Two Ways To Pick The Cut

The rule needs a cut that the chosen edges respect, and two choices appear in practice. Prim's method keeps one growing tree and uses the cut between the tree's vertices and all other vertices. Kruskal's method looks at the lightest remaining edge in the whole graph. Suppose that edge joins two different components of the chosen edges. Then the cut between one of those components and the rest is respected, and the edge is the lightest across it. Both methods add safe edges only, so both end with a minimum spanning tree. They differ in the data they need, and the applicability stage compares them.

State

Tree Membership And Best Weights

This lesson uses the array form of Prim. It keeps best[] and the matrix price[][] and scans all vertices in each round, with no heap. That form fits a dense graph, because the matrix already holds up to V^2 / 2 distinct prices and the scans cost O(V^2), which is about the size of the input.

The input is n, an array cables of triples [a, b, weight], and nothing else. The methods read it and change nothing. Prim uses four variables, and Kruskal uses three more.

  • inTree is a boolean[] of length n that marks the vertices already joined to the growing tree.
  • best is a long[] where best[v] is the weight of the lightest known edge from v to a vertex in the tree; Long.MAX_VALUE means no such edge is known.
  • price is a long[][] where price[u][v] is the lightest cable between u and v; Long.MAX_VALUE means no cable.
  • total is a long that sums the weights of the edges bought so far, and both methods use it.
  • sorted is a copy of cables ordered by weight for Kruskal.
  • bought is the number of edges Kruskal has accepted, and it must reach n - 1.
  • root is an int[] for Kruskal in which two vertices share a root exactly when the chosen edges already connect them.

The sum is a long, because a thousand edges of weight near two billion exceed the range of int. Vertex u is picked by comparing best entries with Long.compare and never by subtraction. The marker Long.MAX_VALUE is only compared and never added to anything. Both methods return -1 to signal that fewer than n vertices joined, and the value is not a cost. Negative weights would also give a correct minimum spanning tree, because the proof never uses the sign, but these lessons do not assume them in their inputs.

Walkthrough

Two Runs On Small Cable Maps

Prim On Six Offices

The first map has six offices and eight cables. The cables are 0 to 1 at 4, 0 to 2 at 3, 1 to 2 at 1, 1 to 3 at 2, 2 to 3 at 4, 3 to 4 at 2, 4 to 5 at 6 and 3 to 5 at 5. Each cell is one office in the order it joins the tree, written as the office and the weight of the edge that joined it. The pointer add marks the office that joins at that step. The variable weight is that edge weight, total is the sum so far, and improved counts the entries of best that drop at this step. At the second step, office 2 joins at weight 3 and lowers the best weights of offices 1 and 3. At the third step, office 1 joins at weight 1, which is cheaper than its direct cable of weight 4. At the fifth step, office 4 joins and lowers nothing, because office 5 already has a cable of weight 5 and the cable from office 4 costs 6. The final total is 13.

Shortest Routes Overpay

The second map has five offices and six cables. The cables are 0 to 1 at 5, 0 to 2 at 5, 1 to 2 at 1, 2 to 3 at 2, 3 to 4 at 1 and 0 to 4 at 9. This run is the method of the naive stage. Each cell is an office in the order Dijkstra's method finalizes it, written as the office and its distance from office 0. The pointer settle marks that office. The variable distance is the distance, cable is the weight of the last cable on the route, and total adds those cables. Offices 1 and 2 each buy a cable of weight 5, and the cable of weight 1 between them is never bought. The tree totals 13, while the cheapest tree totals 9.

Code

Prim And Kruskal In Java

The first method is dense Prim. It builds a matrix of lightest weights, so parallel cables keep only the cheapest one. It returns -1 when the graph is not connected, which the entry contract excludes but a caller can still pass by mistake.

static long densePrim(int n, int[][] cables) {
    long[][] price = new long[n][n];
    for (long[] row : price) Arrays.fill(row, Long.MAX_VALUE);
    for (int[] c : cables) {
        price[c[0]][c[1]] = Math.min(price[c[0]][c[1]], c[2]);
        price[c[1]][c[0]] = price[c[0]][c[1]];
    }
    boolean[] inTree = new boolean[n];
    long[] best = new long[n];
    Arrays.fill(best, Long.MAX_VALUE);
    best[0] = 0;
    long total = 0;
    for (int round = 0; round < n; round++) {
        int u = -1;
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
            if (!inTree[v] && (u == -1 || Long.compare(best[v], best[u]) < 0)) u = v;
        }
        if (best[u] == Long.MAX_VALUE) return -1;
        inTree[u] = true;
        total += best[u];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
            if (!inTree[v] && price[u][v] < best[v]) best[v] = price[u][v];
        }
    }
    return total;
}

Each round scans all vertices twice, so dense Prim runs in O(V^2) time and uses O(V^2) memory for the matrix. The tree edge is safe because best[u] is the lightest edge across the cut between the tree and the other vertices.

The second method is Kruskal. It sorts a copy of the edges, so the input array keeps its order. The sort compares with Integer.compare, because the difference of two int weights can overflow.

static long kruskal(int n, int[][] cables) {
    int[][] sorted = cables.clone();
    Arrays.sort(sorted, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[2], b[2]));
    int[] root = new int[n];
    for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) root[v] = v;
    long total = 0;
    int bought = 0;
    for (int[] c : sorted) {
        int ra = find(root, c[0]);
        int rb = find(root, c[1]);
        if (ra != rb) {
            root[ra] = rb;
            total += c[2];
            bought++;
        }
    }
    return bought == n - 1 ? total : -1;
}

static int find(int[] root, int x) {
    while (root[x] != x) {
        root[x] = root[root[x]];
        x = root[x];
    }
    return x;
}

Sorting costs O(E log E), and the loop makes E calls to find, and path halving alone gives amortized O(log V) per call. The loop costs O(E log V), so the sort dominates. The method uses O(V) memory beyond the cloned array. The edge c is safe whenever its endpoints have different roots, since it is the lightest edge that leaves the component of ra.

When to use it

Recognizing Minimum Connection Problems

Reading The Cue

Use these methods when the statement asks for the lowest total weight that keeps every vertex connected to every other. Laying cable, building roads between towns, and wiring chips are typical cases. The word total matters. A request for the shortest route from one source is a different problem.

Checking The Invariant

An invariant is a property that every step of the method must preserve. Here the invariant is that the chosen edges always lie inside some minimum spanning tree. Each step keeps it by adding the lightest edge across a cut that the chosen edges respect. For Prim the cut is the tree against the rest. For Kruskal it is a component against the rest. Test a graph with equal weights, where two different edges are both lightest and either may be taken. Test a single vertex, which needs zero edges.

Choosing Between Prim And Kruskal

The decision follows the density and the form of the input. A dense graph has close to V^2 / 2 edges. When the input is a list of edges, Kruskal sorts it at O(E log E). Suppose the input is a matrix, or the weights come from a formula for every pair, such as distances between points. Then dense Prim runs at O(V^2) and never stores an edge list. A sparse graph with an edge list favors Kruskal, and a sparse graph held as an adjacency list can use Prim with a heap. Kruskal also handles a graph that is not connected, because it ends with fewer than n - 1 accepted edges and returns -1.

Avoiding The False Friend

A false friend is a method that resembles the correct one and still returns wrong answers. The false friend here is the shortest-path tree, which the first example of this lesson shows. A second false friend is to give every vertex its own lightest edge. That choice can close a cycle and leave the offices in separate groups. Equal weights are a third trap, since they allow several correct answers and a test must accept any of them.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Distinguish MST From Shortest Paths

Author exercise

Problem Specification. An undirected connected graph joins n offices labelled 0, ..., n - 1 and has edges [a, b, w]. Let dist[v] be the least total weight of a path from vertex 0 to v. The shortest-path tree buys, for each vertex v other than 0, the lightest edge [u, v, w] that satisfies dist[u] + w == dist[v]. Return a long[] with exactly two entries. Entry 0 is the total weight of the shortest-path tree. The second entry is the total weight of a minimum spanning tree.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 200.
  • Edges satisfy n - 1 <= edges.length <= 2000, and repeats may occur.
  • Weight satisfies 0 <= w <= 1000000000.
  • Mutation of the input is not allowed.

Example 1. Input n = 4, edges = [[0,1,3],[1,2,1],[0,2,3],[2,3,2]], output [8,6].

Example 2. Input n = 4, edges = [[0,1,2],[1,2,3],[2,3,4]], output [9,9].

Prerequisites. The naive method and the Prim method of this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. The method scores each tree by its total weight, so a cable that is cheap but never lies on a shortest route can still enter the cheapest tree.

Hint

When a graph has only one spanning tree, what must the two totals be?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method computes two totals on the same graph. For the first total it runs Dijkstra's method from vertex 0 with long distances. It then scans the edges once more. For each vertex other than 0 it keeps the lightest edge that satisfies dist[u] + w == dist[v], in either direction, and it sums those weights. For the second total it sorts a copy of the edges and runs Kruskal's method with a union-find array, adding an edge when its endpoints have different roots.

The invariant of the second total is that the chosen edges always lie inside some minimum spanning tree, because each added edge is the lightest one that leaves a component. The first total follows a different rule, which buys edges by distance from vertex 0. The two totals agree when the graph has a single spanning tree and differ when a cheap edge lies off every shortest route. The exercise relies on a connected graph, so the distances are all finite.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(E log E), because Dijkstra's heap, the sort for Kruskal and the single scan of the edges each cost at most that much.
  • Space is O(V + E) for the adjacency list, the heap, the distance array and the copied edge list.
import java.util.*;

public final class MstSptVsMst {
    /**
     * Returns the shortest-path tree weight and the minimum spanning tree weight.
     * Time: O(E log E). Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: Kruskal's chosen edges always lie inside some minimum spanning tree.
     */
    static long[] compare(int n, int[][] edges) {
        // Build an undirected adjacency list; each edge appears twice.
        List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            adj.get(e[0]).add(new int[] {e[1], e[2]});
            adj.get(e[1]).add(new int[] {e[0], e[2]});
        }
        // Distances are long, because a path of many heavy edges exceeds int.
        long[] dist = new long[n];
        Arrays.fill(dist, Long.MAX_VALUE);
        dist[0] = 0;
        PriorityQueue<long[]> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Long.compare(a[0], b[0]));
        heap.add(new long[] {0, 0});
        // Each pass removes one entry; each successful relaxation adds one.
        while (!heap.isEmpty()) {
            long[] top = heap.poll();
            int u = (int) top[1];
            // A stale entry has a cost above the current distance and is skipped.
            if (top[0] > dist[u]) continue;
            for (int[] e : adj.get(u)) {
                // Only a strictly cheaper route creates a new entry.
                if (top[0] + e[1] < dist[e[0]]) {
                    dist[e[0]] = top[0] + e[1];
                    heap.add(new long[] {dist[e[0]], e[0]});
                }
            }
        }
        // For each vertex keep the lightest edge that lies on some shortest route into it.
        long[] tight = new long[n];
        Arrays.fill(tight, Long.MAX_VALUE);
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            // Test both directions of the undirected edge.
            if (dist[e[0]] + e[2] == dist[e[1]]) tight[e[1]] = Math.min(tight[e[1]], e[2]);
            if (dist[e[1]] + e[2] == dist[e[0]]) tight[e[0]] = Math.min(tight[e[0]], e[2]);
        }
        long sptTotal = 0;
        // Vertex 0 buys nothing, so the sum starts at vertex 1.
        for (int v = 1; v < n; v++) sptTotal += tight[v];
        // Kruskal on a sorted copy keeps the input order unchanged.
        int[][] sorted = edges.clone();
        Arrays.sort(sorted, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[2], b[2]));
        int[] root = new int[n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) root[v] = v;
        long mstTotal = 0;
        for (int[] e : sorted) {
            int ra = find(root, e[0]);
            int rb = find(root, e[1]);
            // An edge between two different components is the lightest across that cut.
            if (ra != rb) {
                root[ra] = rb;
                mstTotal += e[2];
            }
        }
        return new long[] {sptTotal, mstTotal};
    }

    /** Finds the root of x with path halving. */
    static int find(int[] root, int x) {
        // Each step halves the path, so repeated calls stay close to constant.
        while (root[x] != x) {
            root[x] = root[root[x]];
            x = root[x];
        }
        return x;
    }

    /** Oracle: Floyd-Warshall distances and a search over every subset of n - 1 edges. */
    static long[] oracle(int n, int[][] edges) {
        long inf = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4;
        long[][] d = new long[n][n];
        for (long[] row : d) Arrays.fill(row, inf);
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) d[v][v] = 0;
        for (int[] e : edges) {
            d[e[0]][e[1]] = Math.min(d[e[0]][e[1]], e[2]);
            d[e[1]][e[0]] = Math.min(d[e[1]][e[0]], e[2]);
        }
        for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
                for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) d[i][j] = Math.min(d[i][j], d[i][k] + d[k][j]);
        long spt = 0;
        for (int v = 1; v < n; v++) {
            long best = inf;
            for (int[] e : edges) {
                if (e[1] == v && d[0][e[0]] + e[2] == d[0][v]) best = Math.min(best, e[2]);
                if (e[0] == v && d[0][e[1]] + e[2] == d[0][v]) best = Math.min(best, e[2]);
            }
            spt += best;
        }
        long mst = inf;
        int m = edges.length;
        // Try every subset of edges with exactly n - 1 members.
        for (int mask = 0; mask < (1 << m); mask++) {
            if (Integer.bitCount(mask) != n - 1) continue;
            int[] p = new int[n];
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) p[v] = v;
            long sum = 0;
            boolean tree = true;
            for (int i = 0; i < m && tree; i++) {
                if ((mask >> i & 1) == 0) continue;
                int ra = find(p, edges[i][0]);
                int rb = find(p, edges[i][1]);
                if (ra == rb) tree = false;
                else { p[ra] = rb; sum += edges[i][2]; }
            }
            if (tree) mst = Math.min(mst, sum);
        }
        return new long[] {spt, mst};
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (!Arrays.equals(compare(4, new int[][] {{0, 1, 3}, {1, 2, 1}, {0, 2, 3}, {2, 3, 2}}), new long[] {8, 6})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!Arrays.equals(compare(4, new int[][] {{0, 1, 2}, {1, 2, 3}, {2, 3, 4}}), new long[] {9, 9})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // A single vertex buys nothing.
        if (!Arrays.equals(compare(1, new int[][] {}), new long[] {0, 0})) throw new AssertionError("single");
        // Java fact: the difference of two int weights can overflow, so the sort uses Integer.compare.
        if (Integer.MIN_VALUE - 1 <= 0) throw new AssertionError("subtraction overflow");
        if (Integer.compare(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 1) >= 0) throw new AssertionError("compare");
        // Java fact: heavy edges exceed int range, so the totals are long.
        long[] big = compare(3, new int[][] {{0, 1, 1_000_000_000}, {1, 2, 1_000_000_000}, {0, 2, 1_000_000_000}});
        if (big[0] != 2_000_000_000L || big[1] != 2_000_000_000L) throw new AssertionError("big");
        if ((int) (big[0] + big[1]) == big[0] + big[1]) throw new AssertionError("int would overflow");
        // The input array order is unchanged by the call.
        int[][] keep = {{0, 1, 9}, {1, 2, 1}, {0, 2, 5}};
        compare(3, keep);
        if (keep[0][2] != 9 || keep[1][2] != 1 || keep[2][2] != 5) throw new AssertionError("mutated");
        // Random connected graphs of at most six vertices and nine edges, with ties and parallel edges.
        Random rnd = new Random(2581);
        for (int t = 0; t < 1500; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            int m = Math.max(n - 1, 0) + rnd.nextInt(10 - Math.max(n - 1, 0));
            m = Math.min(m, 9);
            int[][] edges = new int[m][];
            // The first n - 1 edges link vertex i to an earlier vertex, which keeps the graph connected.
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) {
                int a = i < n - 1 ? i + 1 : rnd.nextInt(n);
                int b = i < n - 1 ? rnd.nextInt(i + 1) : rnd.nextInt(n);
                edges[i] = new int[] {a, b, rnd.nextInt(4)};
            }
            if (!Arrays.equals(compare(n, edges), oracle(n, edges))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Cut-Safe Choice

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A graph has n vertices, labelled from 0 and has undirected edges [a, b, w] listed by index. The array side of length n assigns each vertex to group true or group false. An edge crosses the cut when its endpoints lie in different groups. Return the index of the crossing edge of the lowest weight, using the lowest index among equal weights. Return -1 when no edge crosses.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 2 <= n <= 1000.
  • Edges satisfy 0 <= edges.length <= 5000, and repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Weight satisfies 0 <= w <= 1000000000.
  • Side contains both true and false.

Example 1. Input n = 4, edges = [[0,1,4],[1,2,2],[2,3,7],[0,3,2]], side = [true,true,false,false], output 1.

Example 2. Input n = 3, edges = [[0,1,1]], side = [true,true,false], output -1.

Prerequisites. The exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method returns an edge index for a supplied cut and builds no tree, so only the crossing test and the tie rule remain.

Hint

Which edges does the test skip, and what decides between two crossing edges of equal weight?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)
