Approach. The method scans the edges in index order and keeps the index of the lightest crossing edge seen so far. An edge crosses when side[a] != side[b], so self loops never qualify. The scan replaces the stored index only when the new weight is strictly smaller. That strict comparison keeps the lowest index among equal weights, because the scan visits indices in increasing order.

The invariant is that after each edge the stored index points to the lightest crossing edge among the edges seen so far. The empty set of chosen edges respects every cut. So the cut property of the lesson places the returned edge inside some minimum spanning tree when the graph is connected. The oracle checks that claim on connected random graphs by searching all spanning trees that contain the returned edge.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(E), because the scan reads each edge once.
  • Space is O(1), because it keeps one index and allocates nothing else.
import java.util.*;

public final class MstCutSafeChoice {
    /**
     * Returns the index of the lightest crossing edge, or -1 when none crosses.
     * Time: O(E). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: best holds the lightest crossing edge among the edges scanned so far.
     */
    static int lightestCrossing(int n, int[][] edges, boolean[] side) {
        int best = -1;
        // One pass over the edges; each edge costs a constant number of reads.
        for (int i = 0; i < edges.length; i++) {
            // Skip edges whose endpoints lie in the same group, including self loops.
            if (side[edges[i][0]] == side[edges[i][1]]) continue;
            // Strictly smaller weight only, so the lowest index wins a tie.
            if (best == -1 || edges[i][2] < edges[best][2]) best = i;
        }
        return best;
    }

    /** Smallest total weight of a spanning tree that contains edge pick, or -1 when none exists. */
    static long cheapestTreeWith(int n, int[][] edges, int pick) {
        long best = Long.MAX_VALUE;
        int m = edges.length;
        // Try every subset of edges with exactly n - 1 members that contains the pick.
        for (int mask = 0; mask < (1 << m); mask++) {
            if (Integer.bitCount(mask) != n - 1 || (mask >> pick & 1) == 0) continue;
            int[] p = new int[n];
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) p[v] = v;
            long sum = 0;
            boolean tree = true;
            for (int i = 0; i < m && tree; i++) {
                if ((mask >> i & 1) == 0) continue;
                int ra = find(p, edges[i][0]);
                int rb = find(p, edges[i][1]);
                if (ra == rb) tree = false;
                else { p[ra] = rb; sum += edges[i][2]; }
            }
            if (tree) best = Math.min(best, sum);
        }
        return best == Long.MAX_VALUE ? -1 : best;
    }

    static int find(int[] p, int x) {
        while (p[x] != x) x = p[x];
        return x;
    }

    /** Oracle for the index: sort the crossing indices by weight and then by index. */
    static int oracleIndex(int n, int[][] edges, boolean[] side) {
        List<Integer> cross = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < edges.length; i++) if (side[edges[i][0]] != side[edges[i][1]]) cross.add(i);
        if (cross.isEmpty()) return -1;
        cross.sort((x, y) -> edges[x][2] != edges[y][2] ? Integer.compare(edges[x][2], edges[y][2]) : Integer.compare(x, y));
        return cross.get(0);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        int[][] e1 = {{0, 1, 4}, {1, 2, 2}, {2, 3, 7}, {0, 3, 2}};
        if (lightestCrossing(4, e1, new boolean[] {true, true, false, false}) != 1) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (lightestCrossing(3, new int[][] {{0, 1, 1}}, new boolean[] {true, true, false}) != -1) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // A self loop never crosses, even when it is the lightest edge.
        if (lightestCrossing(2, new int[][] {{0, 0, 0}, {0, 1, 5}}, new boolean[] {true, false}) != 1) throw new AssertionError("loop");
        // Java fact: a boolean array defaults to false, so a fresh array puts every vertex in one group.
        if (lightestCrossing(2, new int[][] {{0, 1, 5}}, new boolean[2]) != -1) throw new AssertionError("default");
        // Random graphs compared with the sorted oracle, and the cut property on connected graphs.
        Random rnd = new Random(2582);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            int n = 2 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            int m = n - 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            int[][] edges = new int[m][];
            // The first n - 1 edges link vertex i + 1 to an earlier vertex, so the graph is connected.
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) {
                int a = i < n - 1 ? i + 1 : rnd.nextInt(n);
                int b = i < n - 1 ? rnd.nextInt(i + 1) : rnd.nextInt(n);
                edges[i] = new int[] {a, b, rnd.nextInt(4)};
            }
            boolean[] side = new boolean[n];
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) side[v] = rnd.nextBoolean();
            side[0] = true;
            side[n - 1] = false;
            int got = lightestCrossing(n, edges, side);
            if (got != oracleIndex(n, edges, side)) throw new AssertionError("index " + t);
            // A connected graph has a crossing edge, and the picked edge fits in some cheapest tree.
            if (got == -1) throw new AssertionError("connected graph must cross " + t);
            long overall = Long.MAX_VALUE;
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) {
                long w = cheapestTreeWith(n, edges, i);
                if (w != -1) overall = Math.min(overall, w);
            }
            if (cheapestTreeWith(n, edges, got) != overall) throw new AssertionError("cut property " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Multiple Valid MSTs

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A connected undirected graph uses vertex labels 0 through n - 1 and has edges [a, b, w]. Return a long[] of two values. The first value is the total weight of a minimum spanning tree. The second entry is 1 when exactly one minimum spanning tree exists, where trees are different when their edge sets differ, and 0 otherwise. Parallel edges are different edges.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Vertices satisfy 1 <= n <= 1000.
  • Edges satisfy n - 1 <= edges.length <= 5000, and repeats and self loops may occur.
  • Weight satisfies 0 <= w <= 1000000000.
  • Sums may exceed the range of int, and the result uses long.

Example 1. Input n = 3, edges = [[0,1,1],[1,2,1],[0,2,1]], output [2,0].

Example 2. Input n = 4, edges = [[0,1,2],[1,2,2],[2,3,1]], output [5,1].

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The method inspects every group of equal weights, because equal weights alone do not make a tree ambiguous, but two edges that compete for the same connection do.

Hint

Among the edges of one weight, which ones could the method buy, and how many does it actually buy?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method sorts a copy of the edges by weight and walks through each group of equal weight. At the start of a group it finds every edge whose endpoints have different roots, and it counts them as candidates. It then adds the candidates to the union-find structure one by one, and it counts how many actually join two components. After the group it compares the two counts. When some candidate was rejected, that candidate closes a cycle with other edges of the same weight, so swapping them gives a second tree of equal total weight.

The invariant is that before each group the structure holds exactly the components of all lighter edges, and every minimum spanning tree contains a spanning forest of those components. Within a group the number of edges added is fixed, but the choice among candidates is free exactly when some candidate is rejected. Equal weights alone do not create a tie, because a group whose candidates all join separate components has only one choice. Self loops never become candidates, and parallel equal edges do.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(E log E), because the sort dominates and the two passes over each group make E calls to find in total.
  • Space is O(V + E) for the roots and the copied edge list.
import java.util.*;

public final class MstMultipleTrees {
    /**
     * Returns the minimum spanning tree weight and 1 when that tree is unique, else 0.
     * Time: O(E log E). Space: O(V + E).
     * Invariant: before each weight group the roots describe the components of all lighter edges.
     */
    static long[] weightAndUniqueness(int n, int[][] edges) {
        // Sort a copy so the caller's array keeps its order.
        int[][] sorted = edges.clone();
        Arrays.sort(sorted, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[2], b[2]));
        int[] root = new int[n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) root[v] = v;
        long total = 0;
        boolean unique = true;
        int i = 0;
        // Each pass handles one group of edges that share a weight.
        while (i < sorted.length) {
            int j = i;
            while (j < sorted.length && sorted[j][2] == sorted[i][2]) j++;
            // Count candidates before any union of this group changes the roots.
            int candidates = 0;
            for (int k = i; k < j; k++) {
                if (find(root, sorted[k][0]) != find(root, sorted[k][1])) candidates++;
            }
            // Add edges and count the ones that really join two components.
            int added = 0;
            for (int k = i; k < j; k++) {
                int ra = find(root, sorted[k][0]);
                int rb = find(root, sorted[k][1]);
                if (ra != rb) {
                    root[ra] = rb;
                    total += sorted[k][2];
                    added++;
                }
            }
            // A rejected candidate means two equal edges compete for the same connection.
            if (added != candidates) unique = false;
            i = j;
        }
        return new long[] {total, unique ? 1 : 0};
    }

    static int find(int[] root, int x) {
        // Path halving keeps later calls close to constant.
        while (root[x] != x) {
            root[x] = root[root[x]];
            x = root[x];
        }
        return x;
    }

    /** Oracle: enumerate every subset of n - 1 edges and count the trees of least weight. */
    static long[] oracle(int n, int[][] edges) {
        long best = Long.MAX_VALUE;
        int count = 0;
        int m = edges.length;
        for (int mask = 0; mask < (1 << m); mask++) {
            if (Integer.bitCount(mask) != n - 1) continue;
            int[] p = new int[n];
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) p[v] = v;
            long sum = 0;
            boolean tree = true;
            for (int i = 0; i < m && tree; i++) {
                if ((mask >> i & 1) == 0) continue;
                int ra = find(p, edges[i][0]);
                int rb = find(p, edges[i][1]);
                if (ra == rb) tree = false;
                else { p[ra] = rb; sum += edges[i][2]; }
            }
            if (!tree) continue;
            if (sum < best) { best = sum; count = 1; }
            else if (sum == best) count++;
        }
        return new long[] {best, count == 1 ? 1 : 0};
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (!Arrays.equals(weightAndUniqueness(3, new int[][] {{0, 1, 1}, {1, 2, 1}, {0, 2, 1}}), new long[] {2, 0})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!Arrays.equals(weightAndUniqueness(4, new int[][] {{0, 1, 2}, {1, 2, 2}, {2, 3, 1}}), new long[] {5, 1})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // A single vertex has exactly one empty tree.
        if (!Arrays.equals(weightAndUniqueness(1, new int[][] {{0, 0, 7}}), new long[] {0, 1})) throw new AssertionError("single");
        // Two parallel equal edges are two different trees.
        if (!Arrays.equals(weightAndUniqueness(2, new int[][] {{0, 1, 3}, {1, 0, 3}}), new long[] {3, 0})) throw new AssertionError("parallel");
        // Java fact: the total of many heavy edges exceeds int range, so the result is long.
        int[][] heavy = new int[3][];
        for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) heavy[i] = new int[] {i, i + 1, 1_000_000_000};
        long[] big = weightAndUniqueness(4, heavy);
        if (big[0] != 3_000_000_000L || (int) big[0] == big[0]) throw new AssertionError("big");
        // Random connected graphs with many ties, compared with the exhaustive count.
        Random rnd = new Random(2583);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2500; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            int m = (n - 1) + rnd.nextInt(Math.min(4, 10 - n));
            int[][] edges = new int[m][];
            // The first n - 1 edges link vertex i + 1 to an earlier vertex, which keeps the graph connected.
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) {
                int a = i < n - 1 ? i + 1 : rnd.nextInt(n);
                int b = i < n - 1 ? rnd.nextInt(i + 1) : rnd.nextInt(n);
                edges[i] = new int[] {a, b, rnd.nextInt(3)};
            }
            if (!Arrays.equals(weightAndUniqueness(n, edges), oracle(n, edges))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Min Cost to Connect All Points

LeetCode 1584

Problem Specification. Points points[i] = [x, y] lie on a plane. A cable between points i and j costs |xi - xj| + |yi - yj|. The list built holds pairs [i, j] that already have a cable at cost 0. Return a long[] of length 2. The first entry is the least total cost of new cables that connect all points. The second entry is the number of new cables laid.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Points satisfy 1 <= n <= 1000, and all points are different.
  • Coordinates satisfy -1000000000 <= x, y <= 1000000000.
  • Built satisfies 0 <= built.length <= 5000, and repeats and cycles may occur.
  • Total may exceed the range of int, and the result uses long.

Example 1. Input points = [[0,0],[1,3],[4,0],[4,4]], built = [[0,3]], output [8,2].

Example 2. Input points = [[0,0],[0,1],[0,2]], built = [[0,1],[1,2]], output [0,0].

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. Existing cables cost zero, so the method counts only the cables that cost something. Up to half a million pairs exist, so the form of Prim without an edge list fits.

Hint

How many candidate cables exist, and does the method need to store them?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Every pair of points is a possible cable, so the graph is complete and has about n squared over two edges. The method therefore uses dense Prim and never builds an edge list. It first marks the built pairs in a boolean matrix. The price of a pair is zero when the matrix marks it, and the Manhattan distance otherwise. The method grows a tree from point 0. In each round it picks the outside point with the smallest best value, adds that value to the total, and counts a new cable when the value is positive. Then it updates best for the remaining points from the price to the new tree point.

The invariant is that best[v] is the lightest price from v to any point in the tree. The picked point therefore has the lightest edge across the cut between the tree and the rest. Points are distinct, so every unbuilt pair costs at least 1, and a positive pick is exactly a new cable. A zero pick follows a built cable. Because the picks reach every point, the number of new cables equals the number of components of the built cables minus one. The total uses long, because a single cable between far corners costs more than the range of int.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n^2 + B), because each of n rounds scans n points twice and the built list is read once.
  • Space is O(n^2) for the boolean matrix of built pairs, which is the only quadratic structure.
import java.util.*;

public final class MstConnectPoints {
    /**
     * Returns the least cost of new cables and the number of new cables.
     * Time: O(n^2 + B). Space: O(n^2).
     * Invariant: best[v] is the lightest price from v to any point already in the tree.
     */
    static long[] connect(int[][] points, int[][] built) {
        int n = points.length;
        // Mark each built pair in both directions; repeats just set the same cell again.
        boolean[][] free = new boolean[n][n];
        for (int[] b : built) {
            free[b[0]][b[1]] = true;
            free[b[1]][b[0]] = true;
        }
        boolean[] inTree = new boolean[n];
        long[] best = new long[n];
        // Long.MAX_VALUE marks a point whose price is not yet known; it is only compared.
        Arrays.fill(best, Long.MAX_VALUE);
        best[0] = 0;
        long total = 0;
        long cables = 0;
        // Each round adds one point, so there are n rounds.
        for (int round = 0; round < n; round++) {
            int u = -1;
            // Scan for the outside point with the smallest known price.
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
                if (!inTree[v] && (u == -1 || best[v] < best[u])) u = v;
            }
            inTree[u] = true;
            total += best[u];
            // A positive price is a new cable; a zero price follows a built cable.
            if (best[u] > 0) cables++;
            // Lower the price of every outside point using the new tree point.
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {
                if (inTree[v]) continue;
                long price = free[u][v] ? 0 : Math.abs((long) points[u][0] - points[v][0]) + Math.abs((long) points[u][1] - points[v][1]);
                if (price < best[v]) best[v] = price;
            }
        }
        return new long[] {total, cables};
    }

    /** Oracle: Kruskal on the explicit list of all pairs, with built pairs at price 0. */
    static long[] oracle(int[][] points, int[][] built) {
        int n = points.length;
        Set<Long> free = new HashSet<>();
        for (int[] b : built) {
            free.add((long) Math.min(b[0], b[1]) * 10_000 + Math.max(b[0], b[1]));
        }
        List<long[]> all = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            for (int j = i + 1; j < n; j++) {
                long price = free.contains((long) i * 10_000 + j) ? 0 : Math.abs((long) points[i][0] - points[j][0]) + Math.abs((long) points[i][1] - points[j][1]);
                all.add(new long[] {price, i, j});
            }
        }
        all.sort((a, b) -> Long.compare(a[0], b[0]));
        int[] p = new int[n];
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) p[v] = v;
        long total = 0, cables = 0;
        for (long[] e : all) {
            int ra = find(p, (int) e[1]);
            int rb = find(p, (int) e[2]);
            if (ra != rb) {
                p[ra] = rb;
                total += e[0];
                if (e[0] > 0) cables++;
            }
        }
        return new long[] {total, cables};
    }

    static int find(int[] p, int x) {
        while (p[x] != x) x = p[x];
        return x;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        if (!Arrays.equals(connect(new int[][] {{0, 0}, {1, 3}, {4, 0}, {4, 4}}, new int[][] {{0, 3}}), new long[] {8, 2})) throw new AssertionError("ex1");
        if (!Arrays.equals(connect(new int[][] {{0, 0}, {0, 1}, {0, 2}}, new int[][] {{0, 1}, {1, 2}}), new long[] {0, 0})) throw new AssertionError("ex2");
        // One point needs no cable.
        if (!Arrays.equals(connect(new int[][] {{5, 5}}, new int[][] {}), new long[] {0, 0})) throw new AssertionError("single");
        // Java fact: two coordinate differences of 2 billion sum to a negative int, so the code widens to long first.
        int far = 1_000_000_000;
        int diff = far - (-far);
        if (diff + diff >= 0) throw new AssertionError("int sum should wrap");
        // Extreme corners: one cable costs 4 billion, which is above the int range.
        int[][] corners = {{-far, -far}, {far, far}, {far, -far}};
        long[] big = connect(corners, new int[][] {});
        if (!Arrays.equals(big, new long[] {4_000_000_000L, 2}) || !Arrays.equals(big, oracle(corners, new int[][] {}))) throw new AssertionError("corners");
        if (big[0] <= Integer.MAX_VALUE) throw new AssertionError("sum should exceed int");
        // Random small point sets with random built pairs, including repeats and cycles.
        Random rnd = new Random(2584);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            Set<String> seen = new HashSet<>();
            List<int[]> pts = new ArrayList<>();
            while (pts.size() < n) {
                int x = rnd.nextInt(7) - 3, y = rnd.nextInt(7) - 3;
                if (seen.add(x + "," + y)) pts.add(new int[] {x, y});
            }
            int[][] points = pts.toArray(new int[0][]);
            int bcount = n < 2 ? 0 : rnd.nextInt(6);
            int[][] built = new int[bcount][];
            for (int i = 0; i < bcount; i++) {
                int a = rnd.nextInt(n);
                int b = (a + 1 + rnd.nextInt(n - 1)) % n;
                built[i] = new int[] {a, b};
            }
            if (!Arrays.equals(connect(points, built), oracle(points, built))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified

Unlocked Combinations

A planner asks for the cheapest cable plan, the operations team asks which single links split the network, and a dispatcher asks for a tour that drives every link once. All three questions use the same map, and a shortest path search answers none of them correctly. This chapter releases one pairing, where a pairing is a lesson that joins earlier topics into one decision, and it leaves one pairing for later.

Pairing Taught In This Chapter

The lesson Choose A Method For Each Graph Goal joins graph optimization with union-find and the heap. The heap and the sorted edge list come from earlier lessons, union-find supplies the group test, and the chapter methods supply low links, the distance table and the trail walk. The lesson adds one idea: the goal of the statement decides which fact the program must keep true, and a method that keeps a different fact gives a wrong or slow answer. Its four exercises cover a cable plan with a group target, bridges filtered by size, a city count with a strict limit, and an itinerary in dictionary order.

Pairing That Waits For A Later Chapter

Flow and matching need a residual graph, which records how much capacity each edge still has, and an invariant about augmenting paths that grow a flow step by step. No lesson of this book teaches that invariant yet, so this chapter assigns no exercises for it. The owner is a future chapter on flow and matching, and until it exists the topic stays outside the active curriculum.

Choose A Method For Each Graph Goal

ProblemWhat Each Technique AddsSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why One Tool Fails Four Requests

A regional telecom keeps one map of its sites and links, and four requests reach the data team in the same week. Planners want the cheapest set of new cables that connects every site. Operators want the list of single links whose failure splits the network. A warehouse team wants the site that reaches the fewest other sites within a travel limit. A dispatch team wants one tour that drives every link exactly once.

An engineer owns a tested shortest path routine with a heap and tries it on all four. For the cabling request, the routine returns a plan that costs more than the cheapest set. For the single-link request, it produces no output that answers the question. For the tour request, it never says which link to drive next. Only the warehouse request fits the tool.

The four requests ask for four different kinds of answer. Each answer stays correct only while a different fact holds during the computation. This lesson asks how to read the goal in a statement and pick the method whose fact matches it.

What Each Technique Adds

What Six Earlier Lessons Add

What Spanning Tree Methods Supply

The Prim lesson supplies a heap of edges that cross from the built tree to a vertex outside it. The Kruskal lesson supplies a sorted edge list together with union-find. Union-find stores one parent entry per vertex, and find returns the root that names a group. Two endpoints with equal roots lie in one group, so the edge between them would close a cycle. The MST decisions lesson explains when each method fits the input better. Both methods sum edge weights in a long, because a total can pass the int range.

What Low Links Supply

The low links lesson supplies a depth-first search that records two numbers per vertex. The number disc[u] is the order in which the search first reaches u. The number low[u] is the smallest discovery time that the subtree of u reaches through one edge other than the edge to its parent. A tree edge from u to a child v is a bridge when low[v] > disc[u]. A bridge is an edge whose removal disconnects the graph.

What Floyd-Warshall And Hierholzer Supply

The Floyd-Warshall lesson supplies a table dist[i][j] of best distances for every pair, and the rule that the sentinel Long.MAX_VALUE never enters an addition. The Hierholzer lesson supplies a walk that deletes each link when it uses it. The walk writes a vertex to the output only when no unused link leaves that vertex, so the output lists the trail backward.

What The Combination Adds

The methods of these six lessons do not compete, because each keeps a different fact true. The combination adds a step before any code. Read the goal in the statement, name the fact that a correct program must keep true, and choose the method that keeps it. The nearest false friend is the heap shortest path routine of the previous chapter. It is a strong tool, yet it answers only questions about routes from one source.

Simple approach

Reusing The Shortest Path Routine

The direct plan treats every request as a route question. For the cabling request it runs a shortest path search from site 0 over the undirected links. It buys the last link of each shortest route, so every site has its cheapest route from site 0 inside the bought set. The method below returns the total cost of the bought links. It returns -1 when some site has no route from site 0.

static long shortestTreeCost(int n, int[][] links) {
    long[] dist = new long[n];
    int[] lastLink = new int[n];
    boolean[] done = new boolean[n];
    Arrays.fill(dist, Long.MAX_VALUE);
    dist[0] = 0;
    long total = 0;
    for (int round = 0; round < n; round++) {
        int u = -1;
        for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) {                      // pick the unfinished site with the smallest distance
            if (!done[v] && dist[v] != Long.MAX_VALUE && (u == -1 || dist[v] < dist[u])) u = v;
        }
        if (u == -1) return -1;
        done[u] = true;
        total += lastLink[u];                               // buy the link that gave u its best distance
        for (int[] e : links) {
            int other = e[0] == u ? e[1] : (e[1] == u ? e[0] : -1);
            if (other >= 0 && !done[other] && dist[u] + e[2] < dist[other]) {
                dist[other] = dist[u] + e[2];
                lastLink[other] = e[2];
            }
        }
    }
    return total;
}

The method is a correct shortest path search, so every site gets its cheapest route from site 0. The open question is whether those routes also give the cheapest set of cables.

Predict first: Three sites 0, 1 and 2 have the links 0 to 1 at cost 4, 0 to 2 at cost 5 and 1 to 2 at cost 2. What does shortestTreeCost return, and what is the cheapest set of cables that connects the three sites?

The method returns 9. Site 1 is reached by the link of cost 4. Site 2 is reached directly for 5, because the route through site 1 costs 6. The bought links are 0 to 1 and 0 to 2. The cheapest connected set uses the links of cost 2 and 4, which total 6. The routine optimizes each route from site 0, not the sum of the bought links.

Bottleneck

Wrong Answers And Slow Searches

The Cabling Answer Is Wrong

The triangle shows the first failure. A shortest path search fixes the cost of every route that starts at one site. The cabling request asks for the smallest sum over the bought links, which is a different quantity. The two answers differ whenever a direct link beats a route through a cheap detour. No change to the loop repairs this, because the loop is correct for its own question.

The Other Requests Cost Too Much

The bridge request fails for another reason. A shortest path search reports distances, and a bridge has no distance. A direct plan deletes each link in turn and searches again from one endpoint. With V sites and E links that costs O(E * (V + E)) time, which is about 10^12 steps for 200000 sites and one million links.

The tour request has no distance at all. A direct plan tries the orders of the links and backtracks, and the number of orders grows exponentially with the number of links. Even the warehouse request, which the routine can answer, costs O(V * (V + E) log V) when the routine starts from every site. On a dense map a table of all pairs costs O(V^3) and is simpler to write.

A Plan That Names The Need

A better plan names what each request needs and uses one method per need. The next stage lists the four matches.

Key idea

Match The Method To The Goal

Every request has an objective, which is the quantity or property that the final answer must optimize or satisfy. The objective decides which fact the program keeps true. A total over a chosen set of links points to a spanning tree method. A question about what one failure disconnects points to low links. A question about the distance between every pair points to a table that grows one intermediate vertex at a time. A requirement to use every link exactly once points to a trail.

Cabling Follows The Cut Property

A cut splits the vertices into two sets. The cut property says that the lightest edge crossing a cut belongs to some minimum spanning tree, when no chosen edge crosses that cut. Kruskal applies it to the cut between one group and the rest. Prim applies it to the cut between the tree and the rest. Each accepted edge is therefore safe, and the chosen edges always form a forest. Stopping after n - g accepted edges leaves exactly g groups with the least total cost.

Bridges Follow Low Links

A low link is the value low[u] from the low links lesson. When low[v] > disc[u] for a child v, no edge from the subtree of v climbs to u or above, except the tree edge itself. That tree edge is then the only connection between the subtree and the rest, so it is a bridge. The test uses discovery times and never uses weights. The same search can also add up the vertices below v, which gives the size of one side of each bridge.

Distances Follow An Intermediate Limit

An intermediate is a vertex that a route passes through between its first and last vertex. After the round for k, the entry dist[i][j] holds the best route whose intermediates all have an index of at most k. Round k only compares the current entry with dist[i][k] + dist[k][j]. After the last round every pair has its final distance, so a question about every pair reads the table once.

Trails Follow Postorder

A postorder write happens after the walk has finished everything reachable from a vertex through unused links. Hierholzer writes a vertex only at that moment. The output read backward is a trail that uses each link once. Taking the smallest unused destination first at every vertex makes the trail the smallest in dictionary order.

State

What Each Method Keeps

Every method reads its input and never changes it. Each one keeps a small piece of state that carries its fact, and the names below are the ones that the code uses.

  • parent is an int[] of union-find links, and find(x) returns the root of the group of x.
  • disc and low are int[] arrays of discovery times and low links, with -1 in disc meaning not yet reached and the clock starting at 0, as in the low links lesson.
  • dist is a long[][] table of pair distances, and Long.MAX_VALUE means no route is known.
  • unused holds one min-ordered queue of destinations per vertex, and the walk polls from it.
  • groups counts the groups that remain, and the cabling loop stops when it reaches the target.

Every addition first checks the sentinel Long.MAX_VALUE, because the sum of two large values wraps to a negative number in Java.

Walkthrough

Two Small Networks Step By Step

Cabling With Union-Find

Five sites sit at the points (0,0), (1,3), (3,2), (2,6) and (6,5), and a cable between two sites costs the sum of the horizontal and vertical gaps. Each cell is one of the ten possible cables, written a-b@cost and sorted by cost. The pointer edge marks the cable under review. The variable sets lists the groups of sites, groups counts them, and total is the cost bought so far. At cable 0-2@5 the sites 0 and 2 already share a group through site 1, so the loop skips it. The loop stops at cable 3-4@5 because one group remains, and it never reads the last four cables.

The total is 16. Notice that the skipped cables are exactly the ones whose endpoints already share a root.

Shortest Routes Cost More

The second network has four sites. Its links are 0 to 1 at cost 4, 0 to 2 at cost 5, 1 to 2 at cost 2 and 2 to 3 at cost 2. This trace runs the shortest path routine from site 0, which is the false friend for the cabling request. Each cell is a site, and the pointer site marks the site that the routine finishes. The variable dist lists the distances from site 0, and bought is the cost of the links that the routine has bought. The cheap link 1 to 2 never enters the bought set, because the direct link 0 to 2 gives site 2 a shorter route than the detour through site 1.

The routine pays 11. A cheapest cable set for this four-site network buys the links of cost 2, 2 and 4, for a total of 8, so the routine overpays by 3.

Code

Four Methods In Short Form

Cabling And Distances

The first method is Kruskal with union-find. It sorts a copy of the edges, so the caller's array keeps its order. It returns -1 when the sites do not end in one group.

static long spanningCost(int n, int[][] edges) {
    int[][] sorted = edges.clone();
    Arrays.sort(sorted, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[2], b[2]));   // cheapest edge first
    int[] parent = new int[n];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) parent[i] = i;
    long total = 0;
    int groups = n;
    for (int[] e : sorted) {
        if (groups == 1) break;                                     // one group left: stop early
        int a = find(parent, e[0]), b = find(parent, e[1]);
        if (a == b) continue;                                       // equal roots: the edge would close a cycle
        parent[a] = b;
        total += e[2];
        groups--;
    }
    return groups == 1 ? total : -1;
}

static int find(int[] parent, int x) {
    while (parent[x] != x) {
        parent[x] = parent[parent[x]];                              // path halving keeps later finds short
        x = parent[x];
    }
    return x;
}

The second method counts, for every city, the other cities within a limit. The guard before the addition keeps the sentinel out of the arithmetic.

static int[] countWithin(int n, int[][] roads, long limit) {
    long[][] dist = new long[n][n];
    for (long[] row : dist) Arrays.fill(row, Long.MAX_VALUE);
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dist[i][i] = 0;
    for (int[] r : roads) {
        dist[r[0]][r[1]] = Math.min(dist[r[0]][r[1]], r[2]);
        dist[r[1]][r[0]] = Math.min(dist[r[1]][r[0]], r[2]);
    }
    for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)                                     // the intermediate vertex is the outer loop
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++)
                if (dist[i][k] != Long.MAX_VALUE && dist[k][j] != Long.MAX_VALUE
                        && dist[i][k] + dist[k][j] < dist[i][j])
                    dist[i][j] = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j];
    int[] near = new int[n];
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
        for (int j = 0; j < n; j++)
            if (i != j && dist[i][j] <= limit) near[i]++;           // the sentinel is larger than any limit
    return near;
}

Bridges And Trails

The third method counts bridges with one depth-first search. It skips the parent edge by edge number, so repeated links between two sites would still count as back edges.

static int countBridges(int n, int[][] links) {
    List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int id = 0; id < links.length; id++) {
        adj.get(links[id][0]).add(new int[] {links[id][1], id});
        adj.get(links[id][1]).add(new int[] {links[id][0], id});
    }
    int[] disc = new int[n], low = new int[n], state = new int[2];  // state: {clock, bridges}
    Arrays.fill(disc, -1);                                          // -1 marks an unreached vertex
    for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) if (disc[s] == -1) visit(s, -1, adj, disc, low, state);
    return state[1];
}

static void visit(int u, int viaId, List<List<int[]>> adj, int[] disc, int[] low, int[] state) {
    disc[u] = low[u] = state[0]++;
    for (int[] e : adj.get(u)) {
        if (e[1] == viaId) continue;                                // never reuse the edge that entered u
        if (disc[e[0]] == -1) {
            visit(e[0], e[1], adj, disc, low, state);
            low[u] = Math.min(low[u], low[e[0]]);
            if (low[e[0]] > disc[u]) state[1]++;                    // no way around the tree edge
        } else {
            low[u] = Math.min(low[u], disc[e[0]]);                  // a back edge lowers the low link
        }
    }
}

The fourth method returns an Eulerian trail from a given start that takes the smallest unused destination first. It assumes that a trail exists.

static List<Integer> trailFrom(int n, int[][] links, int start) {
    List<PriorityQueue<Integer>> unused = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) unused.add(new PriorityQueue<>());
    for (int[] l : links) unused.get(l[0]).add(l[1]);
    Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    LinkedList<Integer> trail = new LinkedList<>();
    stack.push(start);
    while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
        PriorityQueue<Integer> q = unused.get(stack.peek());
        if (q.isEmpty()) trail.addFirst(stack.pop());               // dead end: the postorder write
        else stack.push(q.poll());                                  // consume the smallest unused link
    }
    return trail;
}

Each method costs what its lesson states. The sorted edges take O(E log E), the table takes O(V^3), the search takes O(V + E), and the trail takes O(V + E) as in lesson 05. The trail code here uses priority queues for the smallest-first rule, which adds a log factor and gives O(E log E). The log factor comes only from sorting and priority queues.

When to use it

Choosing Among The Graph Methods

Reading The Goal

Read the sentence that states what the answer must be. A smallest total cost over links that connect everything calls for a spanning tree method, and a count of groups to leave behind changes only the stopping rule. A request for the links that disconnect the network calls for low links. A request that compares every pair of sites with a distance limit calls for the table, as long as the map is small enough for a cubic loop. A request to use every link once and to print the order calls for the trail walk. A route from one source with a cost per step calls for the heap search from the previous chapter. Shortest paths with negative edges and strong components of a directed graph lie outside these four goal families. Lesson 01 covers the first, and lesson 06 covers the second.

Checking The Invariant

An invariant is a fact that holds before and after every step of a loop. For the spanning tree loops, the chosen edges always lie inside some minimum spanning tree. For low links, low[u] is final once the visit of u returns. For the table, every entry is the best route with the allowed intermediates. For the trail, the output read backward is a valid tail of the trail. Test each method on a single vertex, on a disconnected map, and on repeated links between two sites.

Avoiding The False Friend

A false friend is a method that fits the wording of a statement but keeps a different fact true. The shortest path routine is the false friend for cabling, because it fixes routes from one site and never minimizes the sum. A spanning tree is the false friend for distances, because the path between two sites inside a minimum tree can be longer than their shortest route. Deleting links one at a time is a false friend for bridges, because it works and costs far too much. Check the answer type before the method type, since one structure does not solve every objective.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Cheapest Cables For Exactly g Groups

LeetCode 1584

Problem Specification. Each entry [x, y] of points is a distinct site on a plane. A cable between two sites costs |x1 - x2| + |y1 - y2|. A set of cables leaves the sites in groups, and two sites share a group when a chain of cables joins them. Given g, return the smallest total cost of a set of cables that leaves exactly g groups, as a long.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Sites satisfy 1 <= points.length <= 400, and all points differ.
  • Coordinates are integers with |x|, |y| <= 1000000.
  • Groups satisfy 1 <= g <= points.length.
  • Result is a long, and g = points.length gives 0.
  • Input stays unchanged.

Example 1. Input points = [[1,1],[3,4],[8,1],[6,6],[2,7]], g = 2, output 14.

Example 2. Input points = [[1,1],[3,4],[8,1],[6,6],[2,7]], g = 5, output 0.

Prerequisites. Kruskal with union-find from this chapter.

What This Exercise Changes. The loop stops after n - g accepted cables instead of n - 1. The result is then a forest with g trees, not one spanning tree.

Hint

How many cables must a loop accept to turn n single-site groups into g groups?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method lists the cost of every pair of sites as a triple of cost and two indexes, and it sorts the triples by cost. It then scans them with union-find. A triple whose two sites have equal roots would close a cycle, so the scan skips it. Any other triple merges two groups, adds its cost to a long total and lowers the group counter by one. The scan stops as soon as the counter equals g.

The invariant is that the accepted cables form a forest, and the forest is a cheapest forest with its current number of groups. Each accepted cable is the lightest one that leaves its group, which is the cut property for the cut between that group and the rest. Stopping at g groups means that exactly n - g cables are accepted. The total cannot be lower, because every forest with g groups has n - g cables and the scan took the cheapest safe ones first. The total uses long, because an int sum wraps past 2147483647.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n^2 log n), because the list holds n(n - 1) / 2 pairs and the sort dominates the near-constant union-find work.
  • Space is O(n^2) for the pair list and O(n) for the parent array.
import java.util.*;

public final class ChooseCableGroups {
    /**
     * Returns the least total cost of cables that leave exactly g groups of sites.
     * Time: O(n^2 log n). Space: O(n^2).
     * Invariant: the accepted cables form a cheapest forest for the current group count.
     */
    static long cheapestForGroups(int[][] points, int g) {
        int n = points.length;
        // One triple {cost, i, j} per pair of sites; the list has n(n-1)/2 entries.
        List<int[]> pairs = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            for (int j = i + 1; j < n; j++) {
                int cost = Math.abs(points[i][0] - points[j][0]) + Math.abs(points[i][1] - points[j][1]);
                pairs.add(new int[] {cost, i, j});
            }
        }
        // Sorting by cost makes the first usable cable of each scan the lightest one.
        pairs.sort((a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[0], b[0]));
        int[] parent = new int[n];
        // Every site starts as the root of its own group.
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) parent[i] = i;
        int groups = n;
        long total = 0;
        for (int[] p : pairs) {
            // Reaching g groups ends the scan early, so the later pairs cost nothing.
            if (groups == g) break;
            int a = find(parent, p[1]), b = find(parent, p[2]);
            // Equal roots mean the cable would close a cycle, so it is skipped.
            if (a == b) continue;
            parent[a] = b;
            // The sum is a long because a total of int values can pass the int range.
            total += p[0];
            groups--;
        }
        return total;
    }

    /** Finds the root of x with path halving. Time: near constant per call. Space: O(1). */
    static int find(int[] parent, int x) {
        // The loop climbs until a site is its own parent.
        while (parent[x] != x) {
            // Pointing x at its grandparent shortens later climbs.
            parent[x] = parent[parent[x]];
            x = parent[x];
        }
        return x;
    }

    /** Oracle: tries every subset of cables and keeps the cheapest one with exactly g groups. */
    static long oracle(int[][] points, int g) {
        int n = points.length;
        List<int[]> pairs = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
            for (int j = i + 1; j < n; j++)
                pairs.add(new int[] {Math.abs(points[i][0] - points[j][0]) + Math.abs(points[i][1] - points[j][1]), i, j});
        long best = Long.MAX_VALUE;
        for (int mask = 0; mask < (1 << pairs.size()); mask++) {
            int[] parent = new int[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) parent[i] = i;
            int groups = n;
            long cost = 0;
            for (int k = 0; k < pairs.size(); k++) {
                if ((mask >> k & 1) == 0) continue;
                int a = find(parent, pairs.get(k)[1]), b = find(parent, pairs.get(k)[2]);
                if (a != b) {
                    parent[a] = b;
                    groups--;
                }
                cost += pairs.get(k)[0];
            }
            if (groups == g) best = Math.min(best, cost);
        }
        return best;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        int[][] sites = {{1, 1}, {3, 4}, {8, 1}, {6, 6}, {2, 7}};
        if (cheapestForGroups(sites, 2) != 14) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        if (cheapestForGroups(sites, 5) != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // A single site has one group and costs nothing.
        if (cheapestForGroups(new int[][] {{0, 0}}, 1) != 0) throw new AssertionError("single site");
        // The claim about int sums: one more than the largest int wraps to a negative number.
        if (Integer.MAX_VALUE + 1 >= 0) throw new AssertionError("int wrap");
        // Random inputs with distinct points are checked against the subset oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(7);
        for (int iter = 0; iter < 300; iter++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            Set<Long> seen = new HashSet<>();
            int[][] pts = new int[n][];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                int x, y;
                do {
                    x = rnd.nextInt(11) - 5;
                    y = rnd.nextInt(11) - 5;
                } while (!seen.add(x * 100L + y));
                pts[i] = new int[] {x, y};
            }
            int[][] copy = Arrays.stream(pts).map(int[]::clone).toArray(int[][]::new);
            int g = 1 + rnd.nextInt(n);
            if (cheapestForGroups(pts, g) != oracle(pts, g)) throw new AssertionError("random " + iter);
            // The input array stays unchanged.
            if (!Arrays.deepEquals(pts, copy)) throw new AssertionError("mutated");
        }
        System.out.println("ok");
    }
}verified
Variation

Bridges With Large Sides

LeetCode 1192

Problem Specification. A network has servers 0 to n - 1 and undirected connections [a, b] in connections. The network is connected, and no connection repeats or joins a server to itself. A critical connection is a connection whose removal leaves two components. Given s, return every critical connection whose two components each hold at least s servers. Write each connection as [smaller, larger] and sort the list by the first entry, then by the second.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Servers satisfy 2 <= n <= 1000.
  • Connections satisfy n - 1 <= connections.length <= 3000.
  • Size bound satisfies 1 <= s <= n.
  • Result is an empty list when no connection qualifies.
  • Input stays unchanged.

Example 1. Input n = 8, connections = [[0,1],[1,2],[2,0],[1,3],[3,4],[4,5],[5,3],[3,6],[6,7]], s = 2, output [[1,3],[3,6]].

Example 2. Input n = 4, connections = [[0,1],[1,2],[2,3]], s = 2, output [[1,2]].

Prerequisites. The low links lesson and the exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The search also sums subtree sizes. The method reports a bridge only when its subtree size and n minus that size both reach s. It then sorts the output.

Hint

When the search returns from a child v, which count gives the number of servers on the far side of the tree edge?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method builds an adjacency list in which each entry holds the neighbor and the number of the connection. It runs one depth-first search from server 0. On entry to a server u, the search sets disc[u] and low[u] to the next clock value and starts the size sz[u] at 1. It skips the connection that led to u by number, so a parent edge never counts as a back edge. A neighbor already reached lowers low[u] to the neighbor's discovery time. A new neighbor v is visited, and then low[u] takes the smaller value, and sz[u] grows by sz[v].

The invariant is that low[v] is final when visit(v) returns, and sz[v] is the number of servers in the subtree of v. The tree edge from u to v is a bridge exactly when low[v] > disc[u]. Removing it leaves the subtree of v with sz[v] servers and the rest with n - sz[v]. The method keeps the bridge when both numbers reach s. It stores the pair as smaller then larger and sorts the final list, because the search order is not the requested order.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n + m + b log b) for m connections and b reported bridges. The search reads each connection twice, and the sort handles only the reported pairs.
  • Space is O(n + m) for the adjacency list and the arrays, plus recursion depth up to n.
import java.util.*;

public final class ChooseBridgesBySize {
    /**
     * Returns the bridges whose two sides each hold at least s servers, as sorted [smaller, larger] pairs.
     * Time: O(n + m + b log b). Space: O(n + m).
     * Invariant: low[v] and sz[v] are final when the visit of v returns.
     */
    static List<List<Integer>> bridgesBySize(int n, int[][] connections, int s) {
        // Each adjacency entry holds {neighbor, connection number}.
        List<List<int[]>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
        for (int id = 0; id < connections.length; id++) {
            adj.get(connections[id][0]).add(new int[] {connections[id][1], id});
            adj.get(connections[id][1]).add(new int[] {connections[id][0], id});
        }
        int[] disc = new int[n], low = new int[n], sz = new int[n];
        Arrays.fill(disc, -1);
        int[] clock = {0};
        List<List<Integer>> out = new ArrayList<>();
        // The network is connected, so one search from server 0 reaches everything.
        visit(0, -1, n, s, adj, disc, low, sz, clock, out);
        // Pairs are compared by the first entry and then by the second entry.
        out.sort((a, b) -> a.get(0).equals(b.get(0)) ? Integer.compare(a.get(1), b.get(1)) : Integer.compare(a.get(0), b.get(0)));
        return out;
    }

    /** Depth-first visit that fills disc, low and sz. Time: O(deg(u)) per call. Space: O(1) per frame. */
    static void visit(int u, int viaId, int n, int s, List<List<int[]>> adj, int[] disc, int[] low, int[] sz,
                      int[] clock, List<List<Integer>> out) {
        // The first discovery sets both numbers; -1 stays the marker of an unreached server.
        disc[u] = low[u] = clock[0]++;
        sz[u] = 1;
        for (int[] e : adj.get(u)) {
            // The entering connection is skipped by number, so it never acts as a back edge.
            if (e[1] == viaId) continue;
            int v = e[0];
            if (disc[v] == -1) {
                visit(v, e[1], n, s, adj, disc, low, sz, clock, out);
                // The child's low link may reach above u, and its size joins the subtree of u.
                low[u] = Math.min(low[u], low[v]);
                sz[u] += sz[v];
                // No edge climbs above u from the subtree of v, so the tree edge is a bridge.
                if (low[v] > disc[u] && sz[v] >= s && n - sz[v] >= s) {
                    out.add(List.of(Math.min(u, v), Math.max(u, v)));
                }
            } else {
                // A back edge to an earlier server lowers the low link of u.
                low[u] = Math.min(low[u], disc[v]);
            }
        }
    }

    /** Oracle: removes each connection and counts the servers reachable from one endpoint. */
    static List<List<Integer>> oracle(int n, int[][] connections, int s) {
        List<List<Integer>> out = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int skip = 0; skip < connections.length; skip++) {
            boolean[] seen = new boolean[n];
            Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
            seen[connections[skip][0]] = true;
            stack.push(connections[skip][0]);
            int count = 1;
            while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
                int u = stack.pop();
                for (int id = 0; id < connections.length; id++) {
                    if (id == skip) continue;
                    int other = connections[id][0] == u ? connections[id][1] : connections[id][1] == u ? connections[id][0] : -1;
                    if (other >= 0 && !seen[other]) {
                        seen[other] = true;
                        count++;
                        stack.push(other);
                    }
                }
            }
            int a = connections[skip][0], b = connections[skip][1];
            if (!seen[b] && count >= s && n - count >= s) out.add(List.of(Math.min(a, b), Math.max(a, b)));
        }
        out.sort((x, y) -> x.get(0).equals(y.get(0)) ? Integer.compare(x.get(1), y.get(1)) : Integer.compare(x.get(0), y.get(0)));
        return out;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        int[][] c1 = {{0, 1}, {1, 2}, {2, 0}, {1, 3}, {3, 4}, {4, 5}, {5, 3}, {3, 6}, {6, 7}};
        if (!bridgesBySize(8, c1, 2).toString().equals("[[1, 3], [3, 6]]")) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        int[][] c2 = {{0, 1}, {1, 2}, {2, 3}};
        if (!bridgesBySize(4, c2, 2).toString().equals("[[1, 2]]")) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Boundary: a size bound above half of n leaves no bridge.
        if (!bridgesBySize(4, c2, 3).isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("large bound");
        // Random connected simple graphs are checked against the removal oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(11);
        for (int iter = 0; iter < 400; iter++) {
            int n = 2 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            List<int[]> edges = new ArrayList<>();
            Set<Integer> used = new HashSet<>();
            // A random tree makes the graph connected.
            for (int v = 1; v < n; v++) {
                int u = rnd.nextInt(v);
                edges.add(new int[] {u, v});
                used.add(u * 100 + v);
            }
            // Extra connections, without repeats or self loops, create cycles.
            for (int extra = rnd.nextInt(6); extra > 0; extra--) {
                int a = rnd.nextInt(n), b = rnd.nextInt(n);
                if (a == b || !used.add(Math.min(a, b) * 100 + Math.max(a, b))) continue;
                edges.add(new int[] {a, b});
            }
            Collections.shuffle(edges, rnd);
            int[][] conn = edges.toArray(new int[0][]);
            int s = 1 + rnd.nextInt(n);
            if (!bridgesBySize(n, conn, s).equals(oracle(n, conn, s))) throw new AssertionError("random " + iter);
        }
        System.out.println("ok");
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

City With Fewest Strictly Near Cities

LeetCode 1334

Problem Specification. There are n cities numbered from 0, and roads lists undirected roads [a, b, w] of length w. Given limit, a city y is near a city x when y != x and the shortest distance from x to y is strictly less than limit. Return the city with the fewest near cities. When several cities tie, return the one with the greatest index.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Cities satisfy 2 <= n <= 100.
  • Roads satisfy 1 <= roads.length <= n * (n - 1) / 2, and each pair of cities appears at most once.
  • Lengths are integers with 1 <= w <= 10000.
  • Limit satisfies 1 <= limit <= 10000.

Example 1. Input n = 4, roads = [[0,1,3],[1,2,1],[1,3,4],[2,3,1]], limit = 5, output 3.

Example 2. Input n = 4, roads = [[0,1,3],[1,2,1],[1,3,4],[2,3,1]], limit = 3, output 0.

Prerequisites. The Floyd-Warshall lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. Near means strictly below the limit, and the final scan replaces the best city on a tie, so the greatest index wins.

Hint

A city at distance exactly limit is not near. Which comparison operator must the final count use?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The cue is a question about distances between all pairs on a map with at most 100 cities. The method fills a long[][] table with Long.MAX_VALUE as the no-route sentinel and 0 on the diagonal. Each undirected road writes its length into both directions and keeps the smaller value if a pair repeats. Three loops then run with the intermediate city k as the outermost loop. Before any addition, the method tests that both parts differ from the sentinel, because the sum of two copies of Long.MAX_VALUE wraps to a negative number.

The invariant is that after the round for k, each entry is the best distance whose intermediates all have an index of at most k. After the last round the table holds the shortest distances. A final scan counts, for each city, the other cities whose distance is strictly below limit. The sentinel never counts, because it exceeds every limit. The scan uses <= when it compares counts, so a later city with an equal count replaces the earlier one, and the greatest index wins. Counting with <= against the limit instead of < would put a city at distance exactly limit into the count, which is the boundary error.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n^3) for the three loops, plus O(n^2) for the final scan.
  • Space is O(n^2) for the distance table.
import java.util.*;

public final class ChooseCityStrict {
    /**
     * Returns the city with the fewest cities strictly closer than limit, preferring the greatest index.
     * Time: O(n^3). Space: O(n^2).
     * Invariant: after the round for k, dist[i][j] is the best distance with intermediates 0..k.
     */
    static int fewestNearCity(int n, int[][] roads, int limit) {
        long[][] dist = new long[n][n];
        // Long.MAX_VALUE marks a pair with no known route.
        for (long[] row : dist) Arrays.fill(row, Long.MAX_VALUE);
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) dist[i][i] = 0;
        for (int[] r : roads) {
            // A road runs both ways, and the smaller length wins if a pair repeats.
            dist[r[0]][r[1]] = Math.min(dist[r[0]][r[1]], r[2]);
            dist[r[1]][r[0]] = Math.min(dist[r[1]][r[0]], r[2]);
        }
        // The intermediate city is the outermost loop, which the recurrence requires.
        for (int k = 0; k < n; k++) {
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                // Skipping an unreachable first half saves work and avoids sentinel arithmetic.
                if (dist[i][k] == Long.MAX_VALUE) continue;
                for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                    if (dist[k][j] == Long.MAX_VALUE) continue;
                    long through = dist[i][k] + dist[k][j];
                    if (through < dist[i][j]) dist[i][j] = through;
                }
            }
        }
        int bestCity = -1, bestCount = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            int near = 0;
            for (int j = 0; j < n; j++) {
                // Strictly below the limit counts as near, and the sentinel is never below it.
                if (i != j && dist[i][j] < limit) near++;
            }
            // A later city with an equal count replaces the earlier one, so the greatest index wins.
            if (near <= bestCount) {
                bestCount = near;
                bestCity = i;
            }
        }
        return bestCity;
    }

    /** Oracle: Bellman-Ford style passes from every source instead of the table. */
    static int oracle(int n, int[][] roads, int limit) {
        int bestCity = -1, bestCount = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
        for (int s = 0; s < n; s++) {
            long[] d = new long[n];
            Arrays.fill(d, Long.MAX_VALUE);
            d[s] = 0;
            for (int pass = 0; pass < n; pass++) {
                for (int[] r : roads) {
                    if (d[r[0]] != Long.MAX_VALUE && d[r[0]] + r[2] < d[r[1]]) d[r[1]] = d[r[0]] + r[2];
                    if (d[r[1]] != Long.MAX_VALUE && d[r[1]] + r[2] < d[r[0]]) d[r[0]] = d[r[1]] + r[2];
                }
            }
            int near = 0;
            for (int v = 0; v < n; v++) if (v != s && d[v] != Long.MAX_VALUE && d[v] < limit) near++;
            if (near <= bestCount) {
                bestCount = near;
                bestCity = s;
            }
        }
        return bestCity;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        int[][] roads = {{0, 1, 3}, {1, 2, 1}, {1, 3, 4}, {2, 3, 1}};
        if (fewestNearCity(4, roads, 5) != 3) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        if (fewestNearCity(4, roads, 3) != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Limit 1 makes every count 0 because lengths are at least 1, so the greatest index wins.
        if (fewestNearCity(4, roads, 1) != 3) throw new AssertionError("limit one");
        // The sentinel claim: adding two copies of Long.MAX_VALUE wraps to a negative number.
        if (Long.MAX_VALUE + Long.MAX_VALUE >= 0) throw new AssertionError("long wrap");
        // Disconnected map: the isolated city has no near city and the greatest index among ties.
        if (fewestNearCity(3, new int[][] {{0, 1, 2}}, 10) != 2) throw new AssertionError("isolated");
        // Random maps are checked against the per-source oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(23);
        for (int iter = 0; iter < 500; iter++) {
            int n = 2 + rnd.nextInt(7);
            List<int[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int a = 0; a < n; a++)
                for (int b = a + 1; b < n; b++)
                    if (rnd.nextInt(3) == 0) list.add(new int[] {a, b, 1 + rnd.nextInt(9)});
            if (list.isEmpty()) list.add(new int[] {0, 1, 1 + rnd.nextInt(9)});
            int[][] rd = list.toArray(new int[0][]);
            int limit = 1 + rnd.nextInt(12);
            if (fewestNearCity(n, rd, limit) != oracle(n, rd, limit)) throw new AssertionError("random " + iter);
        }
        System.out.println("ok");
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Reconstruct Itinerary

LeetCode 332

Problem Specification. The list tickets holds pairs [from, to] of airport codes, and each pair is one flight. A trip starts at JFK and uses every ticket exactly once. Return the trip as a list of airport codes. Among all valid trips, return the smallest one when the codes are compared one position at a time as strings. At least one valid trip exists.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Tickets satisfy 1 <= tickets.length <= 300, and equal tickets may repeat.
  • Codes are strings of three uppercase letters.
  • Validity is guaranteed, so the result has tickets.length + 1 codes.
  • Input stays unchanged.

Example 1. Input tickets = [["JFK","SFO"],["JFK","ATL"],["SFO","ATL"],["ATL","JFK"],["ATL","SFO"]], output ["JFK","ATL","JFK","SFO","ATL","SFO"].

Example 2. Input tickets = [["JFK","AAA"],["AAA","JFK"],["JFK","AAA"],["AAA","BBB"]], output ["JFK","AAA","JFK","AAA","BBB"].

Prerequisites. The Hierholzer lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. Each airport keeps a queue of destinations ordered from smallest to largest. The walk polls the smallest, and the method reverses the output once.

Hint

Which vertex does the walk write to the output first, and why must the output be reversed at the end?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The cue is the demand to use every ticket exactly once, which makes each ticket an edge that the walk consumes. The method maps each airport to a PriorityQueue<String> of destinations, so polling returns the smallest unused destination. It keeps a stack that starts with JFK. While the stack is not empty, it looks at the top airport. When that airport has an unused ticket, the method polls the smallest destination and pushes it. Otherwise the airport is a dead end, and the method moves it from the stack to the front of the answer list.

The invariant is that the answer list holds a valid tail of the trip, written in order, and every ticket in it is consumed. An airport enters the list only when no unused ticket leaves it, which is the postorder write. Taking the smallest destination first makes the first complete walk the smallest one in dictionary order. A dead end that is smaller than a longer route still comes out last, so the trip with all tickets wins. Strings compare with compareTo, and the queue orders three-letter codes in that order.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(m log m) for m tickets, because each ticket enters and leaves a queue once.
  • Space is O(m) for the queues, the stack and the answer list.
import java.util.*;

public final class ChooseItinerarySmallest {
    /**
     * Returns the smallest trip from JFK that uses every ticket once.
     * Time: O(m log m). Space: O(m).
     * Invariant: the answer list is a valid tail of the trip made of consumed tickets only.
     */
    static List<String> smallestItinerary(List<List<String>> tickets) {
        // Each airport owns a queue that returns its smallest unused destination first.
        Map<String, PriorityQueue<String>> unused = new HashMap<>();
        for (List<String> t : tickets) {
            unused.computeIfAbsent(t.get(0), k -> new PriorityQueue<>()).add(t.get(1));
        }
        Deque<String> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
        LinkedList<String> trip = new LinkedList<>();
        stack.push("JFK");
        // Every turn either consumes one ticket or moves one airport to the answer.
        while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
            PriorityQueue<String> q = unused.get(stack.peek());
            if (q == null || q.isEmpty()) {
                // Dead end: no unused ticket leaves the airport, so it is written to the front.
                trip.addFirst(stack.pop());
            } else {
                // Consume the smallest unused ticket and walk to its destination.
                stack.push(q.poll());
            }
        }
        return trip;
    }

    /** Oracle: tries every order of tickets and keeps the smallest valid trip. */
    static List<String> oracle(List<List<String>> tickets) {
        // The recursion explores each unused ticket that leaves the current airport.
        return search("JFK", new ArrayList<>(List.of("JFK")), new boolean[tickets.size()], tickets, null);
    }

    /** Extends the path by every unused ticket from at and returns the smallest complete trip found. */
    static List<String> search(String at, List<String> path, boolean[] used, List<List<String>> tickets, List<String> best) {
        if (path.size() == tickets.size() + 1) {
            // A complete trip replaces the best one when it is smaller position by position.
            if (best == null || compare(path, best) < 0) return new ArrayList<>(path);
            return best;
        }
        for (int k = 0; k < tickets.size(); k++) {
            if (used[k] || !tickets.get(k).get(0).equals(at)) continue;
            used[k] = true;
            path.add(tickets.get(k).get(1));
            best = search(tickets.get(k).get(1), path, used, tickets, best);
            path.remove(path.size() - 1);
            used[k] = false;
        }
        return best;
    }

    /** Compares two trips of equal length position by position. */
    static int compare(List<String> a, List<String> b) {
        for (int i = 0; i < a.size(); i++) {
            int c = a.get(i).compareTo(b.get(i));
            if (c != 0) return c;
        }
        return 0;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples of the exercise.
        List<List<String>> t1 = List.of(List.of("JFK", "SFO"), List.of("JFK", "ATL"), List.of("SFO", "ATL"),
                List.of("ATL", "JFK"), List.of("ATL", "SFO"));
        if (!smallestItinerary(t1).toString().equals("[JFK, ATL, JFK, SFO, ATL, SFO]")) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        List<List<String>> t2 = List.of(List.of("JFK", "AAA"), List.of("AAA", "JFK"), List.of("JFK", "AAA"), List.of("AAA", "BBB"));
        if (!smallestItinerary(t2).toString().equals("[JFK, AAA, JFK, AAA, BBB]")) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Strings compare by dictionary order, which the queue relies on.
        if ("ATL".compareTo("JFK") >= 0) throw new AssertionError("string order");
        // The input list stays unchanged because the method only reads it.
        List<List<String>> before = new ArrayList<>(t1);
        smallestItinerary(t1);
        if (!before.equals(t1)) throw new AssertionError("mutated");
        // Random valid inputs come from random walks that start at JFK.
        String[] names = {"JFK", "ATL", "SFO", "LAX"};
        Random rnd = new Random(5);
        for (int iter = 0; iter < 400; iter++) {
            int steps = 1 + rnd.nextInt(7);
            List<List<String>> tickets = new ArrayList<>();
            String cur = "JFK";
            for (int i = 0; i < steps; i++) {
                String nxt = names[rnd.nextInt(names.length)];
                tickets.add(List.of(cur, nxt));
                cur = nxt;
            }
            Collections.shuffle(tickets, rnd);
            if (!smallestItinerary(tickets).equals(oracle(tickets))) throw new AssertionError("random " + iter);
        }
        System.out.println("ok");
    }
}verified
